- nuisance explanations matched only by weak controls;
- numerical conditioning, discretization, leakage, or selection effects;
- a result that validates one witness but not the intended existence claim.

Use [adversarial-audit.md](references/adversarial-audit.md) and state what each surviving objection would change.

### 8. Select one bottleneck

Rank candidate next steps by logical leverage, expected uncertainty reduction, mechanism relevance, and cost. Prefer the smallest step whose possible outcomes would change the research state.

Do not optimize for volume of algebra, number of experiments, or apparent completeness. Avoid polishing downstream machinery while an upstream identification or compactness gap remains decisive.

Normally pursue one bottleneck at a time. In an authorized multi-route proof search, retain a small structurally diverse portfolio and select one explicit bottleneck per active route using [proof-search-orchestration.md](references/proof-search-orchestration.md).

### 9. Precommit any authorized experiment

Before implementation or execution, read [decisive-experiments.md](references/decisive-experiments.md) and freeze:

- competing hypotheses;
- the disputed mechanism and why the testbed preserves it;
- primary metric and matched controls;
- pass, fail, and inconclusive thresholds;
- numerical validity gates;
- replication rule and conditional branches;
- exact budget and terminal stopping condition.

Use the smallest nondegenerate testbed, not automatically the smallest instance. Do not launch exploratory grids when one theory-selected stress or ablation can discriminate the hypotheses.

### 10. Update causally

After a derivation or experiment, ask:

1. What mechanism was supported, rejected, or left unresolved?
2. Which exact ledger claims change status?
3. Does a failure kill the broad conjecture, the current witness, one assumption, or only one proof route?
4. Which earlier conclusion is superseded?
5. Does the result authorize a precommitted branch? If not, stop.

Salvage valid identities, tools, counterexamples, and mechanisms from failed programs. Do not preserve their invalid conclusion.

### 11. Synthesize the current state

Lead with the strongest defensible conclusion. Report separately:

- established results;
- supported but unproved claims;
- disfavored or falsified explanations;
- unresolved decisive gaps;
- superseded conclusions;
- the single highest-leverage next action, if requested and authorized.

When later work corrects earlier work, use the corrected result in the main narrative and record the supersession once. Do not accumulate mutually inconsistent report layers.

## Decision rules

- **Strong surrogate performance:** Treat it as evidence of utility, not hierarchy convergence or arbitrary-accuracy existence.
- **A successful matched nonlinear or causal control:** Downgrade that particular mundane explanation; do not infer a theorem.
- **A failed high-order trend:** Check parity, conditioning, common-reference comparability, and replication before inferring divergence.
- **A correct local expansion with growing coefficients:** Preserve the local algebra while rejecting unsupported positive-horizon convergence.
- **A stability theorem without a source estimate:** Record propagation control; leave error production open.
- **Failure of one representation:** Distinguish failure of the explicit witness from failure of the broader admissible class.
- **Confounded evidence:** Mark inconclusive and stop unless a resolution run was preauthorized.
- **Budget exhausted:** Stop even when the scientific question remains open.

## Output standards

Match the requested artifact. For a concise assessment, provide the current conclusion, decisive evidence, and leading gap. For a full research state, provide:

1. canonical target and research contract;
2. sharp conjecture;
3. exact causal skeleton;
4. claim and evidence ledger;
5. adversarial alternatives;
6. experimental evidence and validity limits;
7. what is established, disfavored, falsified, open, and superseded;
8. ranked proof obligations or next decision.

Keep notation stable and minimal. Attach claims to evidence close to where they appear. State assumptions at the claim they support. Never hide a decisive gap in a generic limitations section.

</skill>

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 12742 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.013Z | assistant | final_answer ===
Yes—the idea is valuable, but the decisive mechanism is stronger than near-identity continuity.

After taking width to infinity at each fixed \(\eta\), define

\[
\Delta_{t,L}(\eta)
=
F_{2t,L}(\eta)-F_{t,L}(2\eta).
\]

For every RMS-normalized activation \(\psi\) in the stated weighted \(C^{12}\) class, let

\[
q_m=\frac{\mathcal J^{\mathrm{PJ}}_{5,m,L}(\psi)}{5!},
\qquad q_0=0,
\]

where the \(\mathcal J^{\mathrm{PJ}}_{5,m,L}\) are the terminating singular-Price Gaussian integrals at horizons \(m=1,\ldots,5\), and define

\[
\Theta_{5,j}
=
\sum_{m=0}^{j}
(-1)^{j-m}\binom jm q_m.
\]

Then the exact fifth coefficient is

\[
\boxed{
[\eta^5]\Delta_{t,L}(\eta)
=
\sum_{j=1}^{5}
\left[
\binom{2t}{j}-32\binom tj
\right]\Theta_{5,j}.
}
\]

The degree-five term cancels identically. Consequently this is a polynomial of degree at most four in \(t\). Explicitly,

\[
\boxed{
\left|[\eta^5]\Delta_{t,L}(\eta)\right|
\le C^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\psi,L}t^4,
}
\]

where

\[
C^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\psi,L}
=
30|\Theta_{5,1}|
+14|\Theta_{5,2}|
+4|\Theta_{5,3}|
+\frac23|\Theta_{5,4}|
+\frac43|\Theta_{5,5}|.
\]

This is noncircular: every \(\Theta_{5,j}\) is a finite rational combination of explicit Gaussian activation integrals.

With

\[
B_\psi=\max\{4,M_\psi\},
\]

the completely explicit envelope is

\[
\boxed{
C^{\mathrm{loc}}_{\psi,L}
\le
\frac{227}{180}B_\psi^{E_{L,5}}.
}
\]

Thus

\[
\boxed{
\left|[\eta^5]\Delta_{t,L}(\eta)\right|
\le
\frac{227}{180}B_\psi^{E_{L,5}}t^4.
}
\]

For your genuine near-identity class

\[
\psi_{\alpha,\varphi}(x)
=
\frac{x+\alpha\varphi(x)}
{\|G+\alpha\varphi(G)\|_2},
\]

assuming

\[
\max_{0\le r\le12}\|\varphi^{(r)}\|_\infty\le R,
\qquad
\varphi''\not\equiv0,
\qquad
0<|\alpha|R\le\frac14,
\]

one has \(B_{\psi_{\alpha,\varphi}}=4\). Therefore, uniformly over this entire genuinely nonlinear class,

\[
\boxed{
\left|[\eta^5]\Delta_{t,L}(\eta)\right|
\le
\frac{227}{180}\,4^{E_{L,5}}t^4.
}
\]

This holds at \(L=2\), \(L=3\), and every separately fixed finite depth. In particular,

\[
E_{2,5}
=
4p^{11}
+r\frac{p^{11}-1}{p-1},
\qquad
E_{3,5}
=
6p^{22}
+r\frac{p^{22}-1}{p-1},
\]

with \(p,r\) given by the terminating numerical recursion in the proof.

The reason identity was controlled is therefore not merely the absence of nonlinear terms. At scalar order five, only five temporal marks can occur. Autonomous Euler combinatorics makes \([\eta^5]F_{N,L}\) degree at most five in \(N\), and dyadic refinement cancels its highest-degree component. Nonlinearity changes the five Gaussian coefficients \(\Theta_{5,j}\), but not this temporal cancellation. More generally, through the justified orders \(r\le5\),

\[
[\eta^r]\{F_{2t,L}(\eta)-F_{t,L}(2\eta)\}
\]

has degree at most \(r-1\) in \(t\).

The \(t^4\) power is sharp: already for identity at \(L=1\),

\[
[\eta^5]\Delta_{t,1}
=
\frac{64}{3}t^4-56t^3+\frac{140}{3}t^2-12t,
\]

and the targeted \(L=2\) model also has a nonzero quartic leading term.

The important boundary is that this proves the exact fifth Taylor coefficient, not yet the stronger interval-uniform estimate

\[
\sup_{0<|\eta|\le\rho/t}
\frac{
|\Delta_{t,L}(\eta)-[\eta^3]\Delta_{t,L}\eta^3|
}{|\eta|^5}
\lesssim_{\psi,L}t^4.
\]

Ordinary small-\(\alpha\) continuity cannot prove that stronger statement. At nonzero \(\eta\), unmarked reused-adjoint transports pass through all \(t\) steps. The missing estimate is a step-weighted reachable-source bound of the form

\[
\|I+\eta D\mathbf g(\theta_s)\|_{\mathrm{reach}}
\le 1+C_\psi|\eta|,
\]

including all mixed aggregate-adjoint source derivatives. Finite Fourier, finite-chaos, finite-algebra, and finite-source-ledger shortcuts were all ruled out.

The full proof and final audits are here:

- [FIFTH_JET_POLYNOMIAL_THEOREM.md](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_near_identity_omfp/FIFTH_JET_POLYNOMIAL_THEOREM.md)
- [REVISED_FIFTH_JET_REDTEAM.md](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_near_identity_omfp/REVISED_FIFTH_JET_REDTEAM.md)

Work with the already established width-first OMFP DAG for the same \(q=1\), \(L=2\) network. Define

\[
\Delta_t(\eta)=F_{2t}(\eta)-F_t(2\eta).
\]

Consider the genuinely nonlinear analytic near-identity class

\[
\psi_{\alpha}(x)=
\frac{x+\alpha\varphi(x)}
{\|G+\alpha\varphi(G)\|_2},
\qquad
\|\varphi^{(r)}\|_\infty\le MA^r r!,
\quad \varphi''\not\equiv0.
\]

Prove that there are explicit activation-defined constants \(\alpha_0,\rho,C>0\) such that, for \(0<|\alpha|\le\alpha_0\), every integer \(t\ge1\), and \(|\eta|\le\rho/t\),

\[
\boxed{
|\Delta_t(\eta)-\kappa_t\eta^3|
\le Ct^4|\eta|^5.
}
\]

Carry out the proof, not merely a plan. The required missing step is an all-source-order OMFP stability theorem. You must:

1. Derive the exact paired-step factorization

\[
\Delta_t(\eta)=\eta^2\sum_{j=1}^t A_j(\eta)
\]

before applying absolute values.

2. Construct an explicit reachable-source analytic norm

\[
\|V\|_{\mathfrak X}
=\sum_{m\ge0}w_m\|\mathcal D^mV\|_{p_m},
\]

specifying every weight \(w_m\), moment exponent \(p_m\), and aggregation over source histories.

3. Prove closure of this norm under products, activation composition, singular Gaussian expectations, learned rank-one updates, and the exact reused-adjoint identity

\[
J^*V=\mathbb E[D_JV].
\]

Do not truncate the source-derivative hierarchy.

4. Prove, rather than assume, the one-step estimate

\[
\|(I+\eta Dg(\theta_s))V\|_{\mathfrak X}
\le(1+C_0|\eta|)\|V\|_{\mathfrak X},
\]

together with its first three \(\eta\)-derivative analogues.

5. Deduce

\[
\|\partial_\eta^rP_{j,t}(\eta)\|_{\mathfrak X}
\le C_r t^r,
\qquad 0\le r\le3,
\]

and then

\[
\sup_{j\le t,\ |\eta|\le\rho/t}|A_j'''(\eta)|
\le C_3t^3.
\]

Use Taylor’s integral remainder to obtain the displayed uniform theorem.

6. Express \(\alpha_0,\rho,C\) through \(M,A\) and explicit numerical recursions only. They may not use output suprema, trained-trajectory quantities, or unspecified continuity moduli.

Forbidden shortcuts: finite-\(t\) continuity in \(\alpha\); ambient Fréchet differentiability on an \(L^2\) ball; a finite source ledger; separately bounding \(F_{2t}\) and \(F_t\) and thereby losing coarse/fine cancellation; finite-width Taylor expansion; unspecified \(O(\eta^5)\) or \(o(\eta^5)\); or assuming the required aggregate-adjoint estimate.

After completing \(L=2\), run an adversarial audit of every closure inequality and quantifier. Extend to \(L=3\) only if the additional connector satisfies the same proved norm estimates.

If the reachable-source estimate cannot be proved, do not claim the theorem. State `OPEN`, identify the first precise inequality that fails, and determine whether the obstruction is genuine or requires a different weight sequence.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 13589 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.032Z | assistant | final_answer ===
The requested theorem cannot be proved as stated: its required single-space all-source norm is mathematically impossible. This is a rigorous obstruction to the prescribed proof architecture, not a disproof of the scalar \(Ct^4|\eta|^5\) conjecture.

### What is proved

Let

\[
E_hx=x+h\,g(x),\qquad B_h=E_h^2,\qquad C_h=E_{2h}.
\]

The paired local defect factors exactly as

\[
B_hx-C_hx
=h\bigl(g(x+hg(x))-g(x)\bigr)
=h^2a_h(x),
\]

where

\[
a_h(x)=\int_0^1Dg(x+shg(x))[g(x)]\,ds.
\]

Using the noncommuting hybrids

\[
H_j=\mathcal O\!\left(C_h^{\,t-j}B_h^j\theta_0\right),
\]

one obtains before taking absolute values

\[
\boxed{\Delta_t(h)=h^2\sum_{j=1}^tA_{j,t}(h)},
\]

with

\[
A_{j,t}(h)=
\int_0^1
D(\mathcal O\circ C_h^{t-j})
\bigl(c_j+\lambda h^2a_h(y_j)\bigr)[a_h(y_j)]\,d\lambda,
\]

\[
y_j=B_h^{j-1}\theta_0,\qquad c_j=C_hy_j.
\]

Writing \(S_t=\sum_jA_{j,t}\), the established OMFP parity makes \(S_t\) odd. Thus, whenever \(A_{j,t}\in C^3\),

\[
\kappa_t=S_t'(0)
=\frac{t(2t-1)}2J_{\psi_\alpha,2},
\]

where \(J_{\psi_\alpha,2}\) is the explicit nine-Gaussian-moment activation formula recorded in the proof. Taylor’s integral remainder then gives exactly

\[
\left|\Delta_t(h)-\kappa_th^3\right|
\le
\frac{|h|^5}{6}
\sup_{|u|\le |h|}|S_t'''(u)|.
\]

Consequently,

\[
\sup_{\substack{j\le t\\|u|\le\rho/t}}
|A_{j,t}'''(u)|\le C_A t^3
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\left|\Delta_t(h)-\kappa_th^3\right|
\le\frac{C_A}{6}t^4|h|^5.
\]

This proves the exact paired cancellation and the complete \(t^4\) reduction.

### Why the required norm cannot exist

Suppose the demanded norm

\[
\|V\|_{\mathfrak X}
=\sum_{m\ge0}w_m\|\mathcal D^mV\|_{p_m}
\]

contains constants and the initialized activation atom

\[
X_\alpha=\psi_\alpha(G).
\]

Quantitative same-space product closure would require some finite \(C_\times\) with

\[
\|UV\|_{\mathfrak X}
\le C_\times\|U\|_{\mathfrak X}\|V\|_{\mathfrak X}.
\]

Because constants have no positive source derivatives, definiteness forces \(w_0>0\). If \(p_0=\infty\), the unbounded variable \(X_\alpha\) is excluded. Hence \(p_0<\infty\).

For \(|\alpha|M\le \frac12\),

\[
\frac12\le
\|G+\alpha\varphi(G)\|_2
\le\frac32
\]

and

\[
\|X_\alpha\|_q
\ge
\frac{\|G\|_q-|\alpha|M}{1+|\alpha|M}
\ge c\sqrt q
\]

for all sufficiently large \(q\), with a numerical \(c>0\).

Iterating product closure gives

\[
\|X_\alpha^n\|_{\mathfrak X}
\le
C_\times^{n-1}\|X_\alpha\|_{\mathfrak X}^{n}.
\]

But its source-order-zero term gives

\[
\|X_\alpha^n\|_{\mathfrak X}
\ge
w_0\|X_\alpha^n\|_{p_0}
=
w_0\|X_\alpha\|_{np_0}^{n}.
\]

Taking \(n\)-th roots therefore yields

\[
w_0^{1/n}c\sqrt{np_0}
\le
C_\times^{1-1/n}\|X_\alpha\|_{\mathfrak X},
\]

which is impossible as \(n\to\infty\).

This contradiction uses only \(m=0\). No choices of higher weights, moment exponents, singular-Gram quotients, or source-history aggregation can repair it.

Independently, unrestricted coefficientwise adjoint and product closure would require

\[
w_{m-1}\le C_Jw_m,
\qquad
m w_m\le C_Pw_1w_{m-1},
\]

and hence \(m\le C_PC_Jw_1\) for every \(m\), another contradiction.

### Corrected analytic structure

The viable object is a radius-and-moment scale,

\[
\|V\|_{\rho,p}
=
\sum_{m\ge0}\frac{\rho^m}{m!}
\|\mathcal D^mV\|_{L^p},
\]

for which

\[
\|UV\|_{\rho,p}
\le
\|U\|_{\rho,2p}\|V\|_{\rho,2p},
\]

while the exact adjoint/source shift satisfies only the cross-radius estimate

\[
\|S^kV\|_{\rho',p}
\le
\frac{k!\rho}{(\rho-\rho')^{k+1}}\|V\|_{\rho,p},
\qquad \rho'<\rho.
\]

Chronological simplex factors can cancel \(k!\), but the complete marked \(L=2\) OMFP graph still lacks a proved uniform control of the interspersed products, moving pointers, and coupled \(\bar\rho,\bar\sigma\) responses. A proposed causal charging proof was independently rejected by an explicit same-time nested-response branch.

Therefore the audited status is

\[
\boxed{
\begin{array}{c}
\text{exact paired factorization and conditional }t^4\text{ reduction: proved},\\
\text{requested single-space all-source closure: impossible},\\
\text{uniform scalar }Ct^4|\eta|^5\text{ theorem: still open}.
\end{array}}
\]

The complete proof and audits are in:

- [Final resolution](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_uniform_near_identity_l2/FINAL_RESOLUTION.md)
- [Independent final audit](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_uniform_near_identity_l2/FINAL_RESOLUTION_AUDIT.md)
- [Analytic radius-loss construction](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_uniform_near_identity_l2/ROUTE_ANALYTIC_SCALE.md)

<skill>
<name>solve-math-rigorously</name>
<path>/etc/codex/skills/solve-math-rigorously/SKILL.md</path>
---
name: solve-math-rigorously
description: Produce compact but complete solutions to standalone mathematical questions and proof requests. Use when the user asks to solve, prove, derive, analyze, verify, or correct a mathematical problem that is not primarily an incremental lesson about supplied technical documents. Infer the strongest reasonable interpretation without inventing assumptions, establish a minimal setup, use minimal notation, expose every nontrivial step, state and verify external theorem hypotheses, give proof architecture when useful, and silently audit the draft before answering.
---

# Solve Mathematics Rigorously

Produce a complete mathematical answer rather than a staged document lesson, unless the user explicitly requests incremental pacing. Optimize simultaneously for correctness, rigor, clarity, and economy.

## Determine the intended problem

Interpret the strongest reasonable version supported by the wording and mathematical context. Do not exploit an omission to answer a trivial or uninteresting straw-man version.

Handle ambiguity according to its effect:

- If it does not change the substance, adopt the conventional interpretation and state it briefly.
- If one reasonable assumption makes the intended problem precise, state that assumption before solving.
- If several reasonable interpretations lead to materially different answers, ask for clarification or give a compact case split when that fully resolves the ambiguity.
- If a missing assumption is necessary for the claimed conclusion, do not invent it silently.

If the stated claim is false, say so and give a decisive counterexample or contradiction. Present a corrected nearby statement only when it helps, and label it as a correction rather than the original result.

Answer the problem actually asked. Do not weaken a universal claim to a convenient special case, replace an exact question with a numerical approximation, or prove a converse instead of the stated direction.

## Establish a minimal setup

Begin with a compact setup or preliminaries passage when it reduces ambiguity or supports later reasoning. State only the necessary:

- domain and assumptions;
- definitions used in the solution;
- boundary, regularity, integrability, independence, or nondegeneracy conditions that matter;
- notation that must persist through the answer.

Reuse the problem's notation whenever possible. Introduce a new symbol only when the unabbreviated expression is genuinely cumbersome and recurs often. Define every new symbol before use and state its mathematical type when that is not immediately evident.

Do not force a visible setup heading for a short elementary problem. Do not hide substantive assumptions in prose later in the solution.

## Give the architecture before a substantial proof

Before a long or conceptually difficult proof, explain the plan in a short paragraph or compact list. Identify the main steps, cases, claims, or lemmas and how they combine to yield the conclusion.

Keep the architecture at the level needed to orient the reader. Do not duplicate the detailed proof, and do not substitute a plan for the proof itself. For a routine calculation or one-step argument, omit the architecture.

## Execute every nontrivial step

Derive each nontrivial algebraic, analytic, probabilistic, geometric, combinatorial, or logical transition. Give enough detail for the user to verify the step without reconstructing missing reasoning.

In particular:

- identify the definition, identity, inequality, or prior claim being used;
- show substitutions, rearrangements, case transitions, and changes of variables that carry real mathematical content;
- track domains, signs, quantifiers, equality conditions, exceptional cases, and possible zero denominators;
- justify limit exchanges, differentiation or integration under a sign, convergence claims, and optimization steps;
- distinguish exact arguments from heuristics, approximations, and numerical evidence;
- show that intermediate claims actually imply the requested conclusion.

Avoid words such as “clearly,” “obviously,” “standard,” and “it follows” when they replace reasoning. They are acceptable only after the supporting reason has been supplied.

Do not over-explain elementary arithmetic or definitions already established. Compress routine steps while preserving the chain of verification.

## Invoke external results completely

Whenever the solution relies on a nontrivial theorem or external fact:

1. state the exact form needed, including its assumptions and conclusion;
2. verify each assumption in the present problem;
3. identify the current objects substituted into the theorem;
4. state exactly what conclusion the theorem yields here.

Do not merely name a theorem or call a fact standard. Prefer the relevant specialization over an unnecessarily general statement.

Verify a recent, specialized, source-specific, or uncertain result against a primary or authoritative source before relying on it. Cite the source near the claim. Paraphrase the relevant result unless its exact wording matters; quote only a short compliant excerpt when useful.

If the required theorem is unavailable, uncertain, or does not apply, expose the gap instead of proceeding as though it were valid.

## Use examples and checks proportionately

Include an example, boundary case, counterexample, or remark only when it clarifies an abstraction, tests a hypothesis, explains a consequence, or exposes why an assumption is needed.

Use low-cost sanity checks when they add confidence, such as:

- substituting the result back into the defining relation;
- checking dimensions, signs, or units;
- testing a boundary or small case;
- comparing with a known special case;
- deriving the same conclusion by a second short route.

Do not add examples mechanically or let them replace the general argument.

## Keep the presentation compact

Use headings only when they make a substantial solution easier to navigate. Keep notation sparse and stable. Prefer explicit expressions to disposable shorthand.

Use equations to carry mathematical content, not decoration. Avoid formula dumps, decorative summaries, and boxed equations. State the conclusion plainly.

Let the problem's complexity determine the answer length. Preserve every necessary assumption, theorem check, and logical transition, but remove repetition and exposition that does not improve understanding or verification.

## Audit and revise silently

Draft the solution, then review it before sending. Check:

1. Did the solution address the strongest reasonable interpretation rather than a straw man?
2. Were all adopted assumptions stated, and were none invented merely to force the result?
3. Was the setup sufficient but minimal?
4. Was every persistent symbol necessary and defined?
5. Did a substantial proof receive a useful architecture?
6. Can the reader verify every nontrivial transition?
7. Was every external theorem stated in the needed form, with its hypotheses checked and its conclusion applied explicitly?
8. Were recent, specialized, or uncertain results verified from an authoritative source?
9. Were edge cases, equality conditions, domains, and quantifiers handled?
10. Does the final conclusion answer the exact question?
11. Do examples or remarks earn their space?
12. Is the answer as compact as rigor allows, with no boxed equations?

Revise any failing part and repeat the audit until the answer satisfies the checklist as well as possible. Do not mention the drafting or audit process in the final answer.

</skill>

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 14739 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.065Z | assistant | final_answer ===
The uniform result fails for every fixed nonzero quadratic component, however small. The obstruction is stronger than \(t^5\): the effective fifth-order remainder coefficient grows faster than every polynomial in \(t\).

Let

\[
\psi(x)=px+qx^2,\qquad
p=\frac{\alpha}{\sqrt{\alpha^2+3\beta^2}},\qquad
q=\frac{\beta}{\sqrt{\alpha^2+3\beta^2}},
\]

so that \(\mathbb E\psi(G)^2=1\). Thus “small nonlinear part” means small \(|q|\), equivalently small \(|\beta/\alpha|\), not small linear coefficient \(\alpha\).

Define

\[
\Delta_t(h)=F_{2t}(h)-F_t(2h).
\]

Then the following holds.

### Uniform no-go theorem

For every fixed \(q\neq0\) and every \(\rho>0\),

\[
\boxed{\Delta_t(\rho/t)\longrightarrow+\infty.}
\]

Consequently, if

\[
\kappa_t=[h^3]\Delta_t(h),
\qquad
B_t(\rho)=
\sup_{0<h\le\rho/t}
\frac{|\Delta_t(h)-\kappa_th^3|}{h^5},
\]

then, for every fixed \(r\),

\[
\boxed{\frac{B_t(\rho)}{t^r}\longrightarrow+\infty.}
\]

In particular, neither

\[
|\Delta_t(h)-\kappa_th^3|
\le Ct^4h^5
\]

nor the same inequality with \(t^4\) replaced by any fixed polynomial in \(t\) can hold uniformly on \(0<h\le\rho/t\).

### Why it happens

At finite width, freeze only the bottom-feature update. The remaining top subsystem closes exactly as

\[
A_{k+1}=A_k+h(pZ_k+qZ_k^2),
\]

\[
Z_{k+1}=Z_k+hA_k(p+2qZ_k),
\]

with independent \(A_0,Z_0\sim N(0,1)\) after the width limit.

For \(p,q\ge0\), the full network update, its observable, and this frozen subsystem are polynomials with nonnegative coefficients in the raw initialization variables. Writing the full field as \(g_0+\lambda g_1\), where \(g_1\) is the deleted bottom update, gives

\[
E_h^2-E_{2h}
=
h\bigl(g(x+hg(x))-g(x)\bigr),
\]

whose coefficients are nonnegative. Iteration shows that the entire paired defect is a polynomial in \(\lambda\) with nonnegative coefficients. Gaussian expectation preserves this order because independent Gaussian monomials have either zero or positive expectation. Hence

\[
\Delta_t(h)\ge D_t(h),
\]

where \(D_t\) is the paired defect of the scalar recursion above.

Now select only the quadratic branches \(qZ^2\) and \(2qAZ\). After \(N\) steps, the selected state term has \(h\)-degree \(2^N-1\). At the fine horizon \(N=2t\), the selected output term has

\[
M_t=3(4^t-1)
\]

powers of \(h\), and its raw Gaussian exponents are exactly

\[
(4^t,\,2\cdot4^t).
\]

The coarse \(t\)-step polynomial has maximal degree only \(3(2^t-1)\), so it cannot cancel this term. Therefore

\[
D_t(h)\ge
q^{M_t+1}h^{M_t}
\,\mathbb EG^{4^t}\,
\mathbb EG^{2\cdot4^t}.
\]

Since

\[
\mathbb EG^{2\cdot4^t}
=(2\cdot4^t-1)!!
\ge
\left(\frac{4^t}{\mathrm e}\right)^{4^t},
\]

at \(h=\rho/t\),

\[
D_t(\rho/t)\ge L_t,
\]

where

\[
L_t=
q^{3(4^t-1)+1}
\left(\frac{\rho}{t}\right)^{3(4^t-1)}
\left(\frac{4^t}{\mathrm e}\right)^{4^t}.
\]

Its logarithm satisfies

\[
\frac{\log L_t}{4^t}
=
t\log4-3\log t+O_{q,\rho}(1)
\longrightarrow+\infty.
\]

Thus \(L_t\), and hence \(\Delta_t(\rho/t)\), grows faster than every polynomial.

The exact cubic coefficient remains harmless:

\[
\kappa_t=\frac{t(2t-1)}2J_{p,q},
\]

with

\[
\begin{aligned}
J_{p,q}={}&48p^{12}+3446p^{10}q^2+54820p^8q^4
+387560p^6q^6\\
&+1380876p^4q^8+2399274p^2q^{10}
+1592952q^{12}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Hence

\[
|\kappa_t|(\rho/t)^3\le J_{p,q}\rho^3/t,
\]

which cannot offset \(L_t\).

The signs of \(p,q\) do not matter: two exact orthogonal conjugacies reduce the network to \((|p|,|q|)\).

The width-first qualification is also closed: at each fixed \((N,h)\), expanding

\[
W^s=W^0+\frac h{\sqrt n}\sum_{r<s}C^r(H^r)^T
\]

turns the trained network exactly into a finite reused-\(W^0/(W^0)^\top\), polynomially smooth Gaussian program. Its terminal output converges in every finite \(L^r\). Thus the order is genuinely

\[
n\to\infty\text{ at fixed }(t,h),
\qquad\text{then }t\to\infty,\ h=\rho/t.
\]

This does not contradict the previously computed local result

\[
[h^5]\Delta_t(h)=O(t^4).
\]

The obstruction occurs at order \(3(4^t-1)\), which itself grows with \(t\); no fixed jet can detect it. The identity case \(q=0\) is therefore singular: arbitrarily small but fixed quadratic curvature eventually destroys the uniform positive-time bound.

Full proof and audits:

- [Uniform no-go theorem](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_quadratic_l2_order5/UNIFORM_NO_GO_THEOREM.md)
- [Quadratic width-first bridge](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_quadratic_l2_order5/FIXED_H_QUADRATIC_WIDTH_LEMMA.md)
- [Final adversarial audit](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_quadratic_l2_order5/FINAL_NO_GO_RELEASE_AUDIT.md)

A useful parity split is appearing. For degree \(d\), the pure leading branch of the frozen top subsystem has step degree
\[
\frac{d+1}{d-1}(d^N-1)
\]
and Gaussian exponents \((d^N,d^{N+1})\). This immediately yields a positive surviving moment when \(d\) is even. For odd \(d\), that particular moment vanishes by Gaussian parity, so I’m tracing the first nonvanishing branch and, separately, the higher-degree branch created by training the lower layer.

I’m now checking whether that coefficient controls a fixed \(|\eta|\asymp 1/t\) interval or whether signed lower-order terms can invalidate the desired strict lower bound.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 15231 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.078Z | assistant | commentary ===

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 15737 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.091Z | assistant | final_answer ===
The fully general claim for the actual \(L=2\) network is not yet proved. The audit produced a complete arbitrary-sign theorem for the frozen mean-field recursion and a full-network theorem for sign-coherent polynomials, but it also found exact counterexamples to both bridges needed for arbitrary signed coefficients.

Assume \(d\ge2\); otherwise the normalized identity activation is an immediate exception.

### 1. Arbitrary signed polynomial: complete frozen-block obstruction

Let

\[
P(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{d}c_jx^j,\qquad c_d>0,\qquad \mathbb E P(G)^2=1,
\]

and consider

\[
A^{+}=A+hP(Z),\qquad Z^{+}=Z+hAP'(Z).
\]

Write

\[
D_t(h)=\mathbb E[A_{2t}P(Z_{2t})]
-\mathbb E[A_t(2h)P(Z_t(2h))].
\]

Set

\[
s=d^{2t},\qquad r=d^{2t-1},\qquad
M=\frac{d+1}{d-1}(s-1).
\]

For even \(d\), define

\[
k_t=M,
\]

\[
b_t=C_{2t}\,\mathbb EG^s\,
\mathbb E\!\left[P(G)^rP'(G)^s\right]>0,
\]

where

\[
C_N=
c_d^{\frac{(d+1)d^{N-1}-2}{d-1}}
d^{\frac{d(d^{N-1}-1)}{d-1}}.
\]

For odd \(d\), let \(a=c_{d-1}/c_d\), set \(k_t=M-1\), and define

\[
b_t
=
sC_{2t}\mathbb EG^{s-1}
\mathbb E\!\left[
P^{r-1}(P')^{s-1}
\left\{
rP'+\left(G+\frac ad\right)P
\right\}
\right].
\]

A tail-versus-compact estimate proves \(b_t>0\) for every sufficiently large \(t\). The odd-degree calculation includes every deficit-one source; equivalently, one first translates by

\[
Y=Z+\frac{c_{d-1}}{dc_d}
\]

to remove the degree-\((d-1)\) coefficient exactly.

For any cubic coefficient \(\kappa_t\), Chebyshev extremality gives the strict bound

\[
\boxed{
\sup_{0\le h\le\rho/t}
|D_t(h)-\kappa_t h^3|
\ge
2^{1-2k_t}b_t\left(\frac{\rho}{t}\right)^{k_t}.
}
\]

Moreover, for an explicit activation-dependent \(C_{P,\rho}\),

\[
\log\!\left[
2^{1-2k_t}b_t\left(\frac{\rho}{t}\right)^{k_t}
\right]
\ge
(d+1)d^{2t}t\log d
-\frac{d+1}{d-1}d^{2t}\log t
-C_{P,\rho}d^{2t}.
\]

Hence

\[
\sup_{0<h\le\rho/t}
\frac{|D_t(h)-\kappa_t h^3|}{t^4h^5}
\longrightarrow\infty.
\]

This proves the desired uniform no-go for every signed polynomial in the frozen mean-field block.

### 2. Full network: proved sign-coherent class

If one of

\[
\varepsilon P(\sigma x),
\qquad \varepsilon,\sigma\in\{\pm1\},
\]

has nonnegative coefficients, coefficientwise bottom-update deletion is valid. Consequently, with \(\Delta_t(h)=F_{2t}(h)-F_t(2h)\),

\[
\Delta_t(H)-\kappa_tH^3
\ge
\begin{cases}
c_d^{M+1}H^M\,\mathbb EG^s\,\mathbb EG^{ds},
& d\ \text{even},\\[1mm]
c_d^MH^{M-1}\,\mathbb EG^{s+1}\,
\mathbb EG^{d(s-1)},
& d\ \text{odd},
\end{cases}
\]

at \(H=\rho/t\). Both lower bounds diverge. This covers nonnegative polynomials, monomials, shifted powers, and their exact sign/reflection conjugates.

### 3. Why arbitrary signed coefficients remain open for the full network

Two exact width-first calculations invalidate the required shortcuts.

For

\[
P_K(x)=b_K(x^2-K),
\qquad
b_K=(K^2-2K+3)^{-1/2},
\]

the actual one-step OMFP output satisfies

\[
\boxed{
[h^7]F_1(h)=1024\,b_K^{16}(3-K)^3.
}
\]

Thus \(P_3(x)=(x^2-3)/\sqrt6\) is normalized, nonlinear, and has positive leading coefficient, yet its maximal full-network \(h\)-sector vanishes.

More decisively, coefficientwise deletion itself is false. For

\[
P(x)=-\frac{11}{9}+\frac{10}{27}x^2,
\qquad \mathbb EP(G)^2=1,
\]

the exact width-first Wick DAG gives

\[
[h^{11}]
\left(\Delta_1^{\mathrm{full}}-\Delta_1^{\mathrm{frozen}}\right)<0.
\]

Therefore the arbitrary-sign scalar lower bound cannot currently be transferred to the full network. These examples refute the intermediate lemmas, not the eventual full-network no-go conjecture. Proving that conjecture requires a new complete signed-OMFP coefficient theorem.

The complete proof and adversarial audit are in:

- [Final theorem scope](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_polynomial_uniform_no_go/FINAL_POLYNOMIAL_SCOPE.md)
- [Signed scalar proof](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_polynomial_uniform_no_go/SIGNED_SCALAR_CHEBYSHEV_THEOREM.md)
- [Full signed-bridge audit](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_polynomial_uniform_no_go/FULL_SIGNED_BRIDGE_AUDIT.md)

So the rigorous answer is: the strict uniform lower bound is proved for every signed polynomial in the frozen block and for a broad sign-coherent class in the full network; for the actual full network with completely arbitrary signed coefficients, it remains open.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 18615 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.173Z | assistant | final_answer ===
Yes. The universal claim is false for the uniform, nonzero-learning-rate remainder—even for a \(C^\infty\), globally Lipschitz, linearly growing activation.

Define

\[
\Delta_t(h)=F_{2t}(h)-F_t(2h)
\]

and the best possible uniform remainder constant

\[
\mathcal B_\phi(t,\rho)
=
\inf_{\kappa\in\mathbb R}
\sup_{0<|h|\le \rho/t}
\frac{|\Delta_t(h)-\kappa h^3|}{|h|^5}.
\]

For every \(\rho>0\), we construct an RMS-normalized activation satisfying

\[
\phi\in C^\infty(\mathbb R),
\qquad
0<c_\phi\le \phi'(x)\le C_\phi,
\qquad
|\phi(x)|\le C_\phi(1+|x|),
\]

whose fixed-step width-first outputs all exist and are finite, but for \(t_N=N\),

\[
\boxed{
\mathcal B_\phi(t_N,\rho)
\ge 10\,t_N^{N+5}.
}
\]

Hence

\[
\frac{\mathcal B_\phi(t_N,\rho)}{t_N^q}\longrightarrow\infty
\]

for every fixed \(q\), particularly \(q=5\).

The decisive calculation is the exact general-\(t\) narrow-bump symbol. A compact \(C^1\)-small perturbation localized near \(X\), with width

\[
w=\delta\sqrt{\gamma(X)},
\]

produces the single-node principal coefficient

\[
-\frac{t(8t^3+3)}{24}\,
r^2sv^4\,
\gamma(X)\frac{\delta^2}{w^3}
\int (p'')^2.
\]

The temporal factors for \(t=1,2,3\) are

\[
-\frac{11}{24},
\qquad
-\frac{67}{12},
\qquad
-\frac{219}{8}.
\]

For the lower hidden layer, the strict response-aware contribution is

\[
-\left(5t^4+\frac{15}{8}t\right)
d^3b^4
\gamma(X)\frac{\delta^2}{w^3}
\int(p'')^2,
\]

where \(d=\mathbb E[\phi_{\rm old}'(G)^2]>0\). Because

\[
\gamma(X)\frac{\delta^2}{w^3}
=
\frac{1}{\delta\sqrt{\gamma(X)}},
\]

this tends to infinity while the perturbation’s \(C^1\)-size tends to zero.

The final activation is a locally finite sum

\[
\Phi(x)
=
x+\sum_{N\ge2}
\delta_Nw_N
P\!\left(\frac{x-X_N}{w_N}\right),
\qquad
\phi=\frac{\Phi}{\|\Phi(G)\|_2},
\]

where \(P'=p\), \(p\in C_c^\infty\), and \(\int p=0\). The amplitudes are summable, so \(\phi'\) remains bounded above and away from zero.

At stage \(N\), the smooth truncation is chosen with fifth coefficient \(|\beta_N|\ge20t_N^{N+5}\). Two rates, \(h_N\) and \(h_N/2\), are then preserved under all future \(C^1\)-small insertions. For any proposed cubic coefficient \(\kappa\), writing \(x=(\kappa_N-\kappa)/h_N^2\), the two normalized defects are

\[
x+\beta_N+e_1,
\qquad
4x+\beta_N+e_2.
\]

Since

\[
3|\beta_N|
\le
5\max\{|x+\beta_N|,\ |4x+\beta_N|\},
\]

no choice of \(\kappa\) can cancel both rates. This gives the displayed super-polynomial lower bound using exact fixed-nonzero-step outputs; no cubic or fifth jet of the final activation is assumed.

The all-six-source audit also closes the previously missing \(E_6=\|H^2g\|^2\) sector: the complete general-\(t\) map has 1045 terms and no negative-margin old/new subset. \(E_6\)’s apparent zero-margin terms integrate to \(16-16+0=0\).

One important distinction:

- If “coefficient” means the literal Taylor coefficient \([h^5]\Delta_t\), then whenever it exists it is actually \(O_\phi(t^4)\), because
  \[
  [h^5]\Delta_t
  =
  \sum_{j=1}^5
  \left[
  \binom{2t}{j}-32\binom tj
  \right]\Theta_{5,j},
  \]
  whose degree-five term cancels.
- The counterexample concerns the uniform remainder coefficient on \(0<|h|\le\rho/t\), which is the quantity needed for the gradient-flow limit.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 19401 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.198Z | assistant | final_answer ===
The rigorous answer splits in two.

### Exact ReLU and leaky-ReLU

Write the RMS-normalized activation as

\[
\phi_{a,b}(x)=
\begin{cases}
ax,&x\ge 0,\\
bx,&x<0,
\end{cases}
\qquad
0\le b<a,
\qquad
\frac{a^2+b^2}{2}=1.
\]

For leak \(\lambda\in[0,1)\),

\[
a=\sqrt{\frac{2}{1+\lambda^2}},
\qquad
b=\lambda a.
\]

The audited hard-kink calculation produces

\[
p=Y+2bA,
\qquad
q=Y+2aA,
\]

where

\[
A\sim N(0,1),
\qquad
Y\sim N\!\left(0,e\right),
\qquad
e=\frac{a^4+b^4}{2},
\]

independently. For one switching surface, the exact all-\(t\) paired Euler identity is

\[
J_t(p,q,c)
=
t\,c\,pq\,
\mathbf 1_{\{p>0\ \mathrm{or}\ q<0\}}.
\]

Consequently, the candidate top-gate coefficient is

\[
D_{a,b}
=
\gamma(0)\,
\mathbb E\!\left[
(a-b)A\,pq\,
\mathbf 1_{\{p>0\ \mathrm{or}\ q<0\}}
\right],
\]

or equivalently,

\[
\boxed{
D_{a,b}
=
\frac{3(a-b)}{4\pi\sqrt e}
\int_{2b}^{2a}
\frac{(y-2b)(2a-y)}
     {(1+y^2/e)^{5/2}}\,dy
>0.
}
\]

The lower-layer kink contribution vanishes by Gaussian oddness. For normalized ReLU,

\[
\boxed{
D_{\mathrm{ReLU}}
=
\frac{\sqrt2}{\pi}
\left(1-\frac1{\sqrt5}\right)
\approx0.2488413097.
}
\]

If the remaining nonsmooth OMFP bridges hold, then

\[
\Delta_t(h)
=
F_{2t}(h)-F_t(2h)
=
tD_{a,b}\,h|h|+o_t(h^2).
\]

This would imply

\[
\inf_{\kappa\in\mathbb R}
\sup_{0<|h|\le \rho/t}
\frac{|\Delta_t(h)-\kappa h^3|}{|h|^5}
=+\infty
\]

for every \(t\ge1\). Thus the obstruction would be much stronger than either failure of \(o(t^5)\) or finite \(\Omega(t^t)\) growth: the cubic-subtracted fifth quotient would already be infinite at \(t=1\).

However, the adversarial audit found that this is not yet an unconditional theorem for the actual width-first network. Two substantive bridges remain:

1. Fixed-\(h\) identification of the reused-\(W/W^\top\) finite network with an indicator-valued adaptive Gaussian DAG.
2. A marked-source response-intertwining and singular-\(h\) boundary expansion proving that the complete DAG—not merely its initial normal velocities—reduces to the displayed affine-hinge coefficient.

Therefore the exact-network ReLU/leaky-ReLU classification remains conditional/open. A finite-width sign-cell expansion cannot close it because it would exchange the width and learning-rate limits.

### Smooth ReLU-like counterexample

The smooth version is unconditional.

Define a compact \(C^\infty\) transition

\[
\vartheta(x)=
\begin{cases}
0,&x\le0,\\
e^{-1/x},&x>0,
\end{cases}
\qquad
S(x)=
\frac{\vartheta(x+1)}
{\vartheta(x+1)+\vartheta(1-x)},
\]

and

\[
b_{\lambda,\tau}(x)
=
\lambda x
+
(1-\lambda)
\int_{-\infty}^{x}S(y/\tau)\,dy.
\]

Then

\[
b_{\lambda,\tau}(x)=\lambda x
\quad(x\le-\tau),
\qquad
b_{\lambda,\tau}(x)=x
\quad(x\ge\tau).
\]

Starting from this smooth exact-affine-tail ReLU/leaky-ReLU, add the previously audited remote bump ladder:

\[
\Phi(x)
=
b_{\lambda,\tau}(x)
+
\sum_{m\ge2}
\delta_m w_m
P\!\left(\frac{x-X_m}{w_m}\right),
\qquad
w_m=\delta_m\sqrt{\gamma(X_m)},
\]

and normalize

\[
\widetilde\phi
=
\frac{\Phi}{\|\Phi(G)\|_2}.
\]

The supports are disjoint and tend to \(+\infty\), so the sum is locally finite. Hence \(\widetilde\phi\in C^\infty(\mathbb R)\), is globally Lipschitz and linearly growing, and can be made arbitrarily close in weighted \(C^1\) to the smooth ReLU/leaky-ReLU base.

For every \(\lambda\in[0,1)\), \(\tau,\varepsilon,\rho>0\), the amplitudes can be chosen so that

\[
d_1\!\left(
\widetilde\phi,
\frac{b_{\lambda,\tau}}{\|b_{\lambda,\tau}(G)\|_2}
\right)<\varepsilon
\]

and, simultaneously,

\[
\boxed{
\mathcal B_{\widetilde\phi}(N,\rho)
\ge
\frac35N^{N+6}-2
\ge N^N,
\qquad N\ge2.
}
\]

Thus there are genuinely \(C^\infty\), bounded-slope, ReLU-looking activations whose exact width-first uniform remainder violates \(o(t^5)\) by at least \(\Omega(t^t)\). This does not prove that ordinary softplus or an unperturbed single-scale smoothing violates the property.

The completed notes and audits are here:

\]

V(\theta)=(1-f(\theta))\,\nabla f(\theta).
\]

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 19695 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.207Z | assistant | final_answer ===
Assuming the conventional half-squared loss

\[
\ell(f)=\frac12(1-f)^2,
\]

loss descent changes the picture substantially: the fine/coarse discrepancy generically begins at order \(\eta^2\). This is ordinary first-order Euler error and is compatible with a continuous-time limit.

### Exact reduction

Define

\[
P(\theta)=f(\theta)-\frac12f(\theta)^2.
\]

Then

\[
-\nabla\ell=(1-f)\nabla f=\nabla P,
\qquad
\ell=\frac12-P.
\]

Thus one loss-gradient step is exactly Euler ascent for \(P\):

\[
E_\eta(\theta)=\theta+\eta\nabla P(\theta).
\]

Let

\[
D_t^\ell(\eta)
=
\ell(E_\eta^{2t}\theta_0)
-
\ell(E_{2\eta}^{t}\theta_0).
\]

This is not constant-step feature ascent: the effective feature step \(\eta(1-f)\) is recomputed at every iteration.

### Complete expansion through order five

For a scalar function \(u\), define

\[
T_k u=\frac1{k!}D^ku[v,\ldots,v],
\qquad
v=\nabla P,
\]

and

\[
\Lambda_{m,r}
=
\sum_{\substack{k_1+\cdots+k_r=m\\k_i\ge1}}
(T_{k_1}\cdots T_{k_r}\ell)(\theta_0).
\]

Then, whenever the width-first population DAG is \(C^6\) on the required segment,

\[
\boxed{
D_t^\ell(\eta)
=
\sum_{m=2}^{5}\eta^m
\sum_{r=1}^{m}
\left[
{2t\choose r}
-
2^m{t\choose r}
\right]\Lambda_{m,r}
+
R_{6,t}(\eta),
}
\]

with the exact integral remainder

\[
R_{6,t}(\eta)
=
\frac{\eta^6}{5!}
\int_0^1(1-s)^5
\frac{d^6}{d\eta^6}D_t^\ell(s\eta)\,ds.
\]

Every \(\Lambda_{m,r}\) is an initialization quantity and can be reduced by Gaussian peeling; it is not defined from the trained trajectory.

For the \(q=1,L=2\) network, let

\[
d=\mathbb E[\phi'(G)^2],
\qquad
\Theta=1+d+d^2,
\]

and let the already established cubic invariants be

\[
\mathsf S_{\phi,2}
=
D^3f[g,g,g],
\qquad
\mathsf H_{\phi,2}
=
\|Dg[g]\|^2,
\qquad g=\nabla f.
\]

Readout symmetry gives \(D^2f[g,g]=0\) at initialization. Hence

\[
\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
D_t^\ell(\eta)
={}&t\Theta^2\eta^2\\
&-\frac{t(2t-1)}2
\left(
4\Theta^3+\mathsf S_{\phi,2}+4\mathsf H_{\phi,2}
\right)\eta^3\\
&+K_{4,\phi}(t)\eta^4
+K_{5,\phi}(t)\eta^5
+R_{6,t}(\eta),
\end{aligned}
}
\]

where

\[
\deg K_{4,\phi}\le3,
\qquad
\deg K_{5,\phi}\le4.
\]

The explicit Gaussian-integral formulas for \(\mathsf S_{\phi,2}\), \(\mathsf H_{\phi,2}\), \(K_{4,\phi}\), and \(K_{5,\phi}\) are recorded in the study note linked below.

### ReLU and leaky-ReLU

For normalized ReLU/leaky-ReLU,

\[
d=1,\qquad \Theta=3.
\]

The smooth formula is not directly valid at the kink. Conditional on the same two open hard-kink OMFP bridges identified previously, the Gaussian boundary term changes sign because \(\ell'(0)=-1\). This gives

\[
\boxed{
D_t^\ell(\eta)
=
9t\eta^2
-
tD_{a,b}\eta|\eta|
+
o_t(\eta^2),
}
\]

where

\[
D_{a,b}
=
\frac{3(a-b)}{4\pi\sqrt e}
\int_{2b}^{2a}
\frac{(y-2b)(2a-y)}
     {(1+y^2/e)^{5/2}}\,dy,
\qquad
e=\frac{a^4+b^4}{2}.
\]

For positive \(\eta\),

\[
D_t^\ell(\eta)
=
t(9-D_{a,b})\eta^2+o_t(\eta^2).
\]

For ReLU,

\[
D_{\mathrm{ReLU}}
=
\frac{\sqrt2}{\pi}
\left(1-\frac1{\sqrt5}\right),
\]

so the candidate coefficient is

\[
9-D_{\mathrm{ReLU}}
\approx 8.75115869.
\]

This hard-ReLU formula remains conditional for the actual width-first network.

### Does this violate the continuous-time limit?

No. At fixed physical time

\[
T=2t\eta,
\]

the leading loss discrepancy is

\[
t\eta^2=\frac{T^2}{4t}=O(t^{-1}).
\]

More generally, Euler chronology implies that the order-\(\eta^m\) paired coefficient has degree at most \(m-1\) in \(t\). Therefore every fixed Taylor order satisfies

\[
t^{m-1}\eta^m=O(t^{-1})
\qquad
\left(\eta=\frac{T}{2t}\right).
\]

So neither the ReLU kink nor the previously constructed super-large fifth coefficient is a continuous-time obstruction.

But no finite expansion—even the complete fifth-order expansion—proves convergence, because \(R_{6,t}\) may be nonuniform in \(t\).

A sufficient noncircular state estimate would be: on an activation-envelope-certified reachable set,

\[
\|v\|\le M_T,\qquad
\operatorname{Lip}(v)\le L_T,\qquad
\operatorname{Lip}(\ell)\le R_T.
\]

Then an exact hybrid telescoping argument gives

\[
\boxed{
|D_t^\ell(\eta)|
\le
R_TL_TM_T\,
t\eta^2e^{2L_Tt|\eta|}.
}
\]

At \(\eta=T/(2t)\),

\[
|D_t^\ell(T/(2t))|
\le
\frac{R_TL_TM_TT^2e^{L_TT}}{4t},
\]

which is summable along dyadic refinements and proves scalar mesh convergence.

Those reachable-state bounds have not yet been proved for nonlinear reused-matrix OMFP, and classical Lipschitz control does not apply directly to hard ReLU gates. Therefore the definitive conclusion is:

- the loss discrepancy is consistent with a normal first-order continuous-time limit;
- it contains no proved obstruction;
- the actual nonlinear width-first ReLU/leaky-ReLU loss-gradient limit remains open, because an all-source reachable stability theorem is still missing.

If you use the unhalved loss \((1-f)^2\), replace \(\eta\) by \(2\eta\) and multiply the displayed loss discrepancy by \(2\).

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 19852 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.212Z | assistant | commentary ===
A key mechanism is emerging. Loss descent is an exact residual time-change only for a well-defined feature-flow trajectory. For unbounded \(x^2\), the earlier feature-ascent mesh has an all-orders rare-tail explosion, so that time-change identity alone cannot establish a flow; the residual may either regularize it or produce an even stiffer explicit-Euler instability. I’m now testing this at the exact population recursion rather than extrapolating from the fifth jet. ReLU is structurally different: linear growth removes moment explosion, but the kink and reused \(W/W^\top\) responses still have to be controlled uniformly over the mesh.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 19933 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.215Z | assistant | commentary ===
For \(x^2\), I now have a width-first obstruction that does not use any local jet: before any fixed positive physical time, the loss-Euler path must cross every fixed output level \(0<\delta<1\) as the mesh is refined. This follows by comparing the still-positive-residual phase to the already proved all-orders feature-ascent explosion. === OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 20061 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.219Z | assistant | commentary ===
The \(x^2\) obstruction has now survived an independent reconstruction. It is stronger than “a bad fifth coefficient”: the entire width-first Euler family develops an initialization layer, so no continuous compact-time loss trajectory can be its uniform mesh limit. Importantly, this does not by itself force the single terminal number \(\ell_{2t}(\eta)-\ell_t(2\eta)\) to stay nonzero; terminal agreement is too weak to certify a flow.

For ReLU, a finite-width two-neuron calculation gives a positive-probability attracting gate where the standard fixed-derivative classical ODE cannot continue. The discrete Euler scheme instead chatters with a precise source-dependent occupation fraction. === OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 20181 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.222Z | assistant | final_answer ===
The two activations have different verdicts.

| Activation | Full compact-time mesh guarantee | Continuous-time conclusion |
|---|---|---|
| \(\phi(x)=x^2/\sqrt3\) | **Fails rigorously** | No continuous width-first gradient-flow trace from initialization |
| \(\phi(x)=\sqrt2\,x_+\) | **Open for Euler/Filippov meshes** | A fixed-\(\phi'(0)\) classical flow is not well posed |

Here “full mesh guarantee” means uniform compact-time convergence of the interpolated width-first Euler losses—not merely agreement at one terminal time.

### Quadratic activation: rigorous failure

Let \(F_k^h\) be the width-first output after \(k\) half-MSE steps of size \(h\), and let \(\mathcal F_m(s_0,\ldots,s_{m-1})\) denote the width-first feature-ascent output with deterministic nonnegative step schedule \(s\).

For pure \(x^2\), every finite-width feature trajectory and terminal output is a polynomial with nonnegative formal coefficients in the initialization variables and in the \(s_j\). Gaussian expectation eliminates odd monomials and is positive on every even monomial. Hence

\[
s_j\le \widetilde s_j\quad\forall j
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\mathcal F_m(s)\le \mathcal F_m(\widetilde s).
\]

The fixed-schedule polynomial OMFP theorem lets this inequality pass through \(n\to\infty\).

Loss descent is exactly an adaptive feature schedule:

\[
F_k^h=\mathcal F_k(s_0,\ldots,s_{k-1}),
\qquad
s_k=h(1-F_k^h).
\]

Fix \(0<\delta<1\). Until \(F_k^h\) first reaches \(\delta\),

\[
s_k\ge (1-\delta)h>0.
\]

But the previously proved all-orders quadratic result gives, for every \(\rho>0\),

\[
\mathcal F_{2t}\left(\frac{\rho}{t},\ldots,\frac{\rho}{t}\right)
\longrightarrow+\infty .
\]

Now set \(h_N=T/N\). Given any \(\varepsilon>0\), choose

\[
t_N=\left\lfloor\frac{\varepsilon}{2h_N}\right\rfloor,
\qquad
\rho_*=\frac{(1-\delta)\varepsilon}{4}.
\]

If no hit occurred before \(2t_N\), schedule monotonicity would imply

\[
F_{2t_N}^{h_N}
\ge
\mathcal F_{2t_N}
\left(\frac{\rho_*}{t_N},\ldots,\frac{\rho_*}{t_N}\right)
\longrightarrow+\infty,
\]

contradicting \(F_{2t_N}^{h_N}<\delta\). Therefore the first hitting time satisfies

\[
\tau_N(\delta)
=
h_N\min\{k:F_k^{h_N}\ge\delta\}
\longrightarrow0.
\]

Thus every continuous predictor interpolation reaches the fixed value \(\delta\) at times tending to zero, although \(F_0=0\). Its loss simultaneously reaches

\[
\frac12(1-\delta)^2\ne\frac12=\ell(0).
\]

Consequently the meshes cannot converge uniformly on any compact interval to a continuous trajectory with the correct initial condition. In particular, no dyadically summable split-mesh estimate controlling the whole loss path can hold.

This keeps the required order \(n\to\infty\) first and uses an all-orders comparison, not a finite jet.

A crucial qualification is that this does **not** prove

\[
\ell_{2t}(\eta)-\ell_t(2\eta)\not\to0
\]

at one fixed positive terminal time. Both meshes could traverse the same instantaneous initial layer and subsequently agree. Hence terminal agreement alone would not establish a continuous gradient-flow limit.

### ReLU: classical failure, generalized mesh limit open

For normalized ReLU, take \(c=\sqrt2\). At width \(n=2\), consider

\[
u=(1,1),\quad
W_1=(1,-1),\quad
W_2=(2,0),
\]

\[
a_1=-\frac1c,\qquad
a_2=\frac1{4c}.
\]

Then

\[
z_1=0,\qquad z_2=2,\qquad f=\frac14,\qquad r=1-f=\frac34.
\]

If \(s\) denotes the selected top-gate slope, direct differentiation of both the connector and lower-feature updates gives

\[
\dot z_1=r(4a_1s+2a_2c).
\]

The two one-sided velocities are therefore

\[
p=\frac38>0,
\qquad
q=-\frac{21}{8}<0.
\]

Both sides point toward the gate. With the standard convention \(\phi'(0)=0\), the assigned gate velocity equals \(p\ne0\). A local \(|z|\)-argument proves that no absolutely continuous classical continuation exists: off the gate, \(|z|\) must strictly decrease, forcing \(z\equiv0\), while the nonzero assigned gate velocity forbids \(z\equiv0\).

A transversal flow-tube argument turns this contact into an open set of initial parameters whose trajectories hit the attracting gate. Gaussian initialization assigns that open set positive probability. The same construction works for any fixed value of \(\phi'(0)\) after varying \(a_1\) away from the unique exceptional value.

So a fixed derivative convention does not define a globally well-posed classical ReLU loss flow.

However, this does not disprove an Euler-selected generalized limit. For frozen \(p>0>q\), the Euler gate chatters in an \(O(h)\) strip and has the exact positive-side occupation

\[
\lambda_+=\frac{p}{p-q},
\]

with

\[
\left|
\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\mathbf 1_{\{z_k>0\}}
-N\lambda_+
\right|<1.
\]

Thus the scalar gate has a canonical Filippov-type weak limit. But the network needs more than its mean occupation: since \(I_k^2=I_k\),

\[
I_h\rightharpoonup\lambda_+,
\qquad
I_h^2\rightharpoonup\lambda_+,
\]

not \(\lambda_+^2\). The loss rate sees these second moments through

\[
\|A\phi'(Z)\|_2^2
\quad\text{and}\quad
\|G^*(A\phi'(Z))\|_2^2.
\]

Therefore a valid ReLU theorem requires an occupation/Young-measure OMFP retaining reused \(G/G^*\) responses.

Positive homogeneity does give conditional compact-time norm bounds, so ordinary parameter explosion is not the obstacle. The unresolved bridges are:

1. fixed-mesh width identification for the adaptive hard-indicator program;
2. an occupation-augmented reused-adjoint OMFP;
3. mesh-uniform tail and sewing estimates;
4. uniqueness and restartability of the resulting loss trace.

Accordingly, exact ReLU currently neither passes nor is disproved for the generalized width-first Euler mesh. The classical fixed-derivative flow is rigorously ill posed; the Euler/Filippov limit remains genuinely open.

The combined proof and adversarially audited claim boundary are in [FINAL_X2_RELU_MESH_VERDICT.md](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_loss_mesh_resolution/FINAL_X2_RELU_MESH_VERDICT.md). Supporting details are in the [quadratic initial-layer theorem](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_quadratic_loss_initial_layer/INITIAL_LAYER_THEOREM.md) and [ReLU compact-time audit](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_loss_time_doubling/RELU_COMPACT_TIME_AUDIT.md).

## Verdict

For the network

\[
f_n(\theta)
=
\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n
a_i\,
\phi\!\left(
\frac1{\sqrt n}\sum_{j=1}^nW_{ij}\phi(u_j)
\right),
\qquad
\ell_n=\frac12(1-f_n)^2,
\]

with loss-gradient Euler updates

\[
\theta_{k+1}
=
\theta_k+\eta(1-f_n(\theta_k))\,n\nabla f_n(\theta_k),
\qquad t=k\eta,
\]

the conclusions are:

| Activation | Width-first continuous-time limit | Joint \((n,\eta)\) limit |
|---|---|---|
| \(\phi(x)=x^2/\sqrt3\) | **No.** A fixed output/loss change occurs at times tending to zero. | **No for every ODE-resolving scaling.** Arbitrarily under-resolved numerical diagonals remain unclassified. |
| \(\phi(x)=\sqrt2\,x_+\) | Classical ODE: **no**, because it is not globally well posed. Euler-selected generalized flow: **open**. | Scalar predictor/loss are locally precompact for every \(\eta(n)\to0\), but uniqueness, identification, and global continuation are open. |

### 1. Quadratic: the full mesh guarantee fails

Let \(F_k^h\) denote the width-first output. Loss descent is feature ascent with adaptive step

\[
s_k=h(1-F_k^h).
\]

Before \(F_k^h\) reaches any fixed \(\delta\in(0,1)\),

\[
s_k\ge (1-\delta)h.
\]

For deterministic nonnegative schedules, the quadratic expected output is coordinatewise monotone in every step size. The audited all-order result gives

\[
\mathcal F_{2t}\left(\frac{\rho}{t},\ldots,\frac{\rho}{t}\right)
\longrightarrow+\infty
\qquad(\rho>0).
\]

Taking \(h_N=T/N\) and \(t_N=\lfloor\varepsilon/(2h_N)\rfloor\), monotonicity implies that the loss trajectory must cross \(\delta\) before time \(\varepsilon\), for all sufficiently large \(N\). Hence its first crossing time satisfies

\[
\tau_N(\delta)\longrightarrow0.
\]

At that time,

\[
F_N(\tau_N)=\delta,
\qquad
\ell_N(\tau_N)=\frac12(1-\delta)^2,
\]

whereas initialization gives \(F_N(0)=0\) and \(\ell_N(0)=1/2\). Therefore neither predictor nor loss can converge in \(C([0,T])\) with the initialized trace.

This also shows why the terminal scalar comparison

\[
\ell_{2t}(\eta)-\ell_t(2\eta)
\]

is insufficient: both meshes could traverse the same initial layer and later agree. The terminal comparison remains unresolved, but the full path guarantee is definitively false.

### 2. Quadratic: shrinking \(\eta\) jointly with width does not cure it

Let \(f_n^{\mathrm{ode}}\) be the actual finite-width loss flow. There are \(\delta_0>0\) and random times

\[
\tau_n\le \frac{2}{\sqrt{\log n}}
\]

such that, with probability tending to one,

\[
f_n^{\mathrm{ode}}(\tau_n)-f_n^{\mathrm{ode}}(0)\ge\delta_0.
\]

Consequently, every joint scaling satisfying the genuine Euler-consistency condition

\[
\sup_{0\le t\le2/\sqrt{\log n}}
\left|
\bar f_n^{\eta_n}(t)-f_n^{\mathrm{ode}}(t)
\right|
\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0
\]

inherits the same vanishing-time jump and cannot converge to a continuous initialized limit.

An explicit sufficient condition for this Euler consistency is

\[
\boxed{
\eta_n n^{14}
\exp\!\left(
\frac{6\cdot10^{11}n^9}{\sqrt{\log n}}
\right)
\longrightarrow0.
}
\]

For example, \(\eta_n=e^{-10^{12}n^9}\) qualifies—and still fails to produce a continuous initialized limit.

Thus, under the standard clock \(t=k\eta_n\), making \(\eta_n\) smaller only resolves the finite-width flow more accurately; it cannot remove the underlying width-dependent initial layer.

A deliberately under-resolved intermediate diagonal is not completely ruled out. But any limit obtained there would require an independent consistency theorem and would be a discretization-selected regularization, not presently a joint limit of the canonical finite-width gradient flow.

### 3. ReLU: classical flow fails, but continuous Euler subsequences exist

At width two there is an explicit attracting gate with one-sided normal velocities

\[
p=\frac38>0,
\qquad
q=-\frac{21}{8}<0.
\]

Both sides point toward the gate. Any fixed choice of \(\phi'(0)\) assigns, generically, a nonzero velocity on the gate, producing no absolutely continuous classical continuation. An open set of Gaussian initializations reaches this obstruction. Hence the fixed-convention classical ReLU gradient ODE is not globally well posed.

However, this does not imply a discontinuous Euler limit. In fact, for every deterministic \(\eta_n\to0\), define

\[
R=\max\left\{
1,\|a\|_n,\|u\|_n,
\left\|\frac W{\sqrt n}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\right\}.
\]

The exact ReLU Euler update obeys

\[
R^+\le R+6\eta R^5.
\]

On a high-probability initialization event, this gives \(R\le12\) on the explicit interval

\[
0\le t\le T_0,
\qquad
T_0=\frac1{384\cdot6^4}.
\]

Moreover,

\[
|f^+-f|\le 60\cdot12^7\eta.
\]

Therefore the predictor and loss interpolants are uniformly bounded and equi-Lipschitz on \([0,T_0]\). Their laws are tight in \(C([0,T_0])\), and since

\[
\mathbb E f_n(0)^2=\frac1n,
\]

every subsequential limit is continuous and begins at

\[
F(0)=0,
\qquad
L(0)=\frac12.
\]

So ReLU does not have the quadratic scalar initial-jump obstruction. What remains unproved is whether all subsequences give the same deterministic path and whether the required occupation-augmented OMFP state is unique and restartable.

There is currently no proved \(\eta(n)\) guaranteeing that canonical generalized limit. The initialization slab scale \(n\eta_n\) can be calculated exactly, but it is not sufficient: attracting gates create temporal Young measures whose joint correlations are visible to reused adjoints and squared tangent-kernel terms.

### 4. Ways to obtain an actual continuous limit

Three mathematically valid modifications are available:

1. **Use normalized arctangent.**  
   For the same depth-two feature-learning scaling, normalized \(\arctan\) has a proved unique global restartable pointed-action IDE, with compact-time convergence of predictors, tangent kernels, and losses.

2. **Kernel-normalize the quadratic optimizer.**  
   With
   \[
   K_n=Df_n[g_n]=\frac1n\|g_n\|_2^2,
   \qquad
   \dot\theta=\frac{(1-f_n)g_n}{K_n},
   \]
   one obtains exactly
   \[
   \dot f_n=1-f_n,\qquad
   f_n(t)=1-(1-f_n(0))e^{-t},\qquad
   \ell_n(t)=\ell_n(0)e^{-2t}.
   \]
   This fixes the predictor/loss clock, although it is a different optimizer.

3. **Slow the physical vector field separately.**  
   Multiplying the quadratic vector field by \(\alpha_n\) with
   \[
   \alpha_n n^{14}\to0
   \]
   produces a rigorously frozen predictor/loss limit. This is trivial and changes physical time; it is not achieved merely by shrinking the Euler mesh.

The complete audited synthesis is in [FULL_RESOLUTION_AND_JOINT_SCALING.md](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_loss_mesh_resolution/FULL_RESOLUTION_AND_JOINT_SCALING.md). Detailed proofs are in [JOINT_SCALING_VERDICT.md](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_joint_width_mesh_quadratic/JOINT_SCALING_VERDICT.md) and [RELU_NO_CONTINUOUS_LIMIT_TEST.md](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_loss_time_doubling/RELU_NO_CONTINUOUS_LIMIT_TEST.md).

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 20824 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.242Z | user | None ===
is the following scaling wrong or different than the one you calculated? is it accurate? do you agree with it and if yes how does it compare to your analysis?

Yes—provided the initialization admits a neuron-embedded/nested mean-field structure. A concrete scaling is

\[
\operatorname{Var}(a_i)=\Theta(1),\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(b_{ji})=\Theta(n^{-2}),\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(c_j)=\Theta(n^{-2}),
\]

with raw layerwise learning rates

\[
\eta_{a,n}=n h_n,\qquad
\eta_{b,n}=h_n,\qquad
\eta_{c,n}=\frac{h_n}{n},
\qquad nh_n\longrightarrow0.
\]

For example, \(h_n=n^{-3}\) gives \((\eta_a,\eta_b,\eta_c)=(n^{-2},n^{-3},n^{-4})\). On the macroscopic clock \(t=k h_n\), both hidden-layer preactivations then have generically nonzero \(O(1)\) velocities.

There is an essential caveat: variances and learning rates alone do not specify the limit. Fully i.i.d. centered middle weights require a different variance, \(\Theta(n^{-1})\), and do not admit an autonomous mean-field equation based only on the ordinary empirical weight law.

## 1. Exact finite-width dynamics

Assume no biases and simultaneous full-batch GD. In raw coordinates,

\[
f_n(a,b,c)=\sum_{j=1}^n c_j
 \left(\sum_{i=1}^n b_{ji}a_i^2\right)^2,
\qquad L=(f_n-1)^2.
\]

Set, with all powers componentwise,

\[
q=a^{\odot2},\qquad z=bq,\qquad
g=b^\top(c\odot z),\qquad r=f_n-1.
\]

The gradients are

\[
\nabla_a f_n=4a\odot g,\qquad
\nabla_b f_n=2(c\odot z)q^\top,\qquad
\nabla_c f_n=z^{\odot2}.
\]

Therefore one GD step is exactly

\[
\begin{aligned}
a^+&=a\odot(1-8\eta_a r g),\\
q^+&=q\odot(1-8\eta_a r g)^{\odot2},\\
b^+&=b-4\eta_b r(c\odot z)q^\top,\\
c^+&=c-2\eta_c r z^{\odot2},\\
z^+&=bq^+-4\eta_b r(c\odot z)(q^\top q^+),\\
f^+&=(c^+)^\top(z^+)^{\odot2},\qquad
L^+=(f^+-1)^2.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus \(L_1\) is obtained by applying these formulas to the initial state, and \(L_2\) by applying the identical displayed map to the resulting state. This is a genuinely explicit expression; \(L_{k+1}\) is not a function of \(L_k\) alone.

The exact correction to the second preactivation is also useful. Define

\[
\begin{aligned}
R_0&=\sum_i q_i^2,&
P&=b(q\odot g),&
Q&=b(q\odot g^{\odot2}),\\
T&=\sum_iq_i^2g_i,&
U&=\sum_iq_i^2g_i^2.
\end{aligned}
\]

Then

\[
\begin{aligned}
z^+-z={}&-16\eta_a rP
-4\eta_b rR_0(c\odot z)\\
&+64\eta_a^2r^2Q
+64\eta_a\eta_b r^2T(c\odot z)\\
&-256\eta_a^2\eta_b r^3U(c\odot z).
\end{aligned}
\]

This identity shows why merely requiring \(\eta_{\ell,n}\to0\) is insufficient: the sums multiplying the quadratic corrections can themselves grow with \(n\).

## 2. The constructive scaling

Introduce \(O(1)\) normalized variables

\[
A_i=a_i,\qquad W_{ji}=n b_{ji},\qquad C_j=n c_j.
\]

Then

\[
Z_j=\frac1n\sum_iW_{ji}A_i^2,\qquad
F_n=\frac1n\sum_j C_jZ_j^2.
\]

Define the backpropagated field

\[
R_i=\frac1n\sum_j C_jZ_jW_{ji}.
\]

With raw learning rates

\[
(\eta_a,\eta_b,\eta_c)=(nh,h,h/n),
\]

the normalized variables satisfy the exact updates

\[
\begin{aligned}
A_i^+&=A_i-8hrA_iR_i,\\
W_{ji}^+&=W_{ji}-4hrC_jZ_jA_i^2,\\
C_j^+&=C_j-2hrZ_j^2.
\end{aligned}
\]

All normalized increments are therefore \(O(h)\), uniformly in width when the normalized fields remain bounded.

Indeed, let \(S_i=A_i^2\), \(U_j=C_jZ_j\), and define

\[
M_\ell=\frac1n\sum_i S_i^2R_i^\ell,\qquad
P_{\ell,j}=\frac1n\sum_iW_{ji}S_iR_i^\ell.
\]

Then the exact second-preactivation update is

\[
Z_j^+-Z_j
=-hr(16P_{1,j}+4U_jM_0)
+64h^2r^2(P_{2,j}+U_jM_1)
-256h^3r^3U_jM_2.
\]

Consequently, the \(h^2\) and \(h^3\) terms vanish after division by \(h\), uniformly for bounded neuron-embedded states.

## 3. Joint mean-field/gradient-flow theorem

Let first- and second-layer neuron types be \(\alpha\in\Omega_1\) and \(\beta\in\Omega_2\), with probability measures \(\mu,\nu\). Initialize using fixed bounded profiles

\[
A_i(0)=A_0(\alpha_i),\qquad
b_{ji}(0)=\frac1nW_0(\beta_j,\alpha_i),\qquad
c_j(0)=\frac1nC_0(\beta_j).
\]

The continuum state is

\[
\begin{aligned}
Z_t(\beta)
 &=\int W_t(\beta,\alpha)A_t(\alpha)^2\,d\mu(\alpha),\\
R_t(\alpha)
 &=\int C_t(\beta)Z_t(\beta)W_t(\beta,\alpha)\,d\nu(\beta),\\
F_t&=\int C_t(\beta)Z_t(\beta)^2\,d\nu(\beta),
\qquad r_t=F_t-1.
\end{aligned}
\]

The limiting gradient flow is

\[
\begin{aligned}
\partial_tA_t(\alpha)
 &=-8r_tA_t(\alpha)R_t(\alpha),\\
\partial_tW_t(\beta,\alpha)
 &=-4r_tC_t(\beta)Z_t(\beta)A_t(\alpha)^2,\\
\partial_tC_t(\beta)
 &=-2r_tZ_t(\beta)^2.
\end{aligned}
\]

In particular,

\[
\partial_tZ_t(\beta)
=-4r_tC_t(\beta)Z_t(\beta)\int A_t^4\,d\mu
-16r_t\int W_t(\beta,\alpha)A_t(\alpha)^2R_t(\alpha)\,d\mu.
\]

Thus both hidden preactivations \(A_t(\alpha)\) and \(Z_t(\beta)\) have finite, generically nonzero \(O(1)\) velocities.

For finite-type \(\Omega_1,\Omega_2\), assign each type its limiting proportion up to an \(O(n^{-1})\) counting error. Symmetry within types reduces finite-width training exactly to Euler discretization of a fixed finite-dimensional polynomial ODE. On every compact interval,

\[
\sup_{kh_n\le T}
\|\theta_{n,k}-\theta(kh_n)\|
\le C_T\left(h_n+\frac1n\right).
\]

The proof is direct: on a bounded region the polynomial vector field is Lipschitz, its Euler local error is \(O(h_n^2)\), and the type-frequency error per step is \(O(h_n/n)\). Hence

\[
e_{k+1}\le(1+Lh_n)e_k+C h_n^2+C\frac{h_n}{n},
\]

and discrete Grönwall gives the stated bound.

Global existence holds for finite types. Define the weighted squared norms \(N_A,N_W,N_C\). Along the limiting flow,

\[
\dot N_A=-16rF,\qquad
\dot N_W=-8rF,\qquad
\dot N_C=-4rF,
\]

so \(N_A-2N_W\) and \(N_W-2N_C\) are conserved. Moreover, the loss decreases, implying that \(r\) and \(F=1+r\) remain bounded. Hence the three norms cannot blow up in finite time.

## 4. An explicit centered, nondegenerate witness

The construction need not use nonzero weight means. Let

\[
\alpha=(s,x),\quad s\in\{-1,1\},\quad x\in\{1,2\},
\]

and let \(\beta=v\in\{-2,-1,1,2\}\), all types uniformly weighted. Set

\[
\begin{aligned}
A_0(s,x)&=sx,\\
W_0(v;s,x)&=v\left(x^2-\frac52\right),\\
C_0(v)&=\frac{32}{729}\left(v^2-\frac52\right).
\end{aligned}
\]

Every layer has zero marginal mean. The raw initialization variances are

\[
\operatorname{Var}(a)=\frac52,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(b)=\frac{45}{8n^2},\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(c)
=\frac{9}{4n^2}\left(\frac{32}{729}\right)^2.
\]

At time zero,

\[
Z_0(v)=\frac94v,\qquad F_0=\frac12,\qquad r_0=-\frac12.
\]

The first-layer velocity is

\[
\dot A_0(s,x)
=\frac89A_0(s,x)\left(x^2-\frac52\right),
\]

and the second-layer preactivation velocity is

\[
\dot Z_0(v)
=10v+\frac{136}{81}v\left(v^2-\frac52\right).
\]

Both are nonzero and independent of \(n\). This is therefore genuine \(O(1)\) feature motion under the definition in the question.

## 5. Loss after one, two, and many steps

In normalized coordinates let

\[
\mathsf M_n=\operatorname{diag}(nI_A,n^2I_W,nI_C),
\]

so one step is

\[
\theta^+=\theta-2hr\,\mathsf M_n\nabla F_n.
\]

Define

\[
K_n=\nabla F_n^\top\mathsf M_n\nabla F_n
\]

and

\[
\Gamma_n=
(\mathsf M_n\nabla F_n)^\top
\nabla^2F_n
(\mathsf M_n\nabla F_n).
\]

Explicitly,

\[
K_n=
\frac1n\sum_jZ_j^4
+4\left(\frac1n\sum_iA_i^4\right)
 \left(\frac1n\sum_jC_j^2Z_j^2\right)
+16\frac1n\sum_iA_i^2R_i^2.
\]

Taylor expansion of the exact recurrence gives, with all right-hand quantities evaluated at the initial state,

\[
\begin{aligned}
r_1
 &=r-2hrK_n+2h^2r^2\Gamma_n+O(h^3),\\
L_1
 &=r^2-4hr^2K_n
 +4h^2\left(r^2K_n^2+r^3\Gamma_n\right)+O(h^3),
\end{aligned}
\]

and

\[
\begin{aligned}
r_2
 &=r-4hrK_n
 +h^2\left(4rK_n^2+12r^2\Gamma_n\right)+O(h^3),\\
L_2
 &=r^2-8hr^2K_n
 +24h^2\left(r^2K_n^2+r^3\Gamma_n\right)+O(h^3).
\end{aligned}
\]

More generally, for fixed integer \(k\),

\[
L_k
=r^2-4kh\,r^2K_n
+4k(2k-1)h^2
 \left(r^2K_n^2+r^3\Gamma_n\right)
+O_k(h^3).
\]

This fixed-\(k\) series must not be extrapolated to \(k\asymp h^{-1}\). On macroscopic intervals one instead uses the limiting ODE:

\[
\dot r_t=-2r_tK_t,\qquad
\dot L_t=-4r_t^2K_t,
\]

together with

\[
\sup_{kh_n\le T}|L_{n,k}-L(kh_n)|
=O_T(h_n+n^{-1})
\]

for deterministic type discretizations, or typically \(O_{\mathbb P}(h_n+n^{-1/2})\) for sampled latent types.

## 6. What changes for fully i.i.d. centered weights?

If \(b_{ji}\) are fully i.i.d. centered, the preceding \(n^{-2}\) variance gives

\[
\mathbb E Z_j^2=O(n^{-1}),
\]

so the regular nested mean-field limit is dead. To keep \(Z_j=O(1)\), the balanced centered-i.i.d. scaling is instead

\[
\operatorname{Var}(a_i)=\Theta(1),\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(b_{ji})=\Theta(n^{-1}),\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(c_j)=\Theta(n^{-2}),
\]

with the same mobility ratio \(n:1:n^{-1}\).

This does produce \(O(1)\) RMS velocities for both hidden layers, but it is not a classical empirical-measure mean field. A fixed middle edge changes only \(O(n^{-1/2})\), while \(n\) such changes alter a preactivation by \(O(1)\). Row/column Gaussian fields and two-time overlap/response kernels survive.

Indeed, writing \(s_i=a_i^2\), replacing one row \(W_j\) by

\[
W'_j=W_j-\frac{W_j\cdot s}{\|s\|^2}s
\]

changes every fixed entry by only \(O_{\mathbb P}(n^{-1/2})\), so \(W\) and \(W'\) have the same limiting entrywise empirical law. Nevertheless,

\[
\frac1{\sqrt n}W'_j\cdot s=0,
\]

whereas \(n^{-1/2}W_j\cdot s\) has a nondegenerate Gaussian limit. Thus the ordinary empirical weight law cannot determine the preactivations or close the dynamics.

So the precise resolution is:

- Yes: the neuron-embedded scaling above gives a rigorous joint mean-field/gradient-flow limit with \(O(1)\) feature learning.
- No: for fully i.i.d. centered middle weights, an autonomous mean-field flow of only the ordinary weight empirical laws cannot exist.
- An enriched random-operator/dynamical mean-field limit for the centered-i.i.d. model remains plausible, but the one- and two-step expansions alone do not prove its compact-time convergence.
- Since \(x=1\) is the only input, this establishes feature motion in the stated sense, not input-dependent representation learning across a dataset.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 20946 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.246Z | assistant | commentary ===
The main comparison is already clear: the mobility ratio \(n:1:n^{-1}\) is the same muP mobility as ours after changing coordinates. The proposed construction changes the middle-layer initialization from the Gaussian-action scale \(b_{ji}\sim n^{-1/2}\) to a structured kernel scale \(b_{ji}\sim n^{-1}\). That removes the extreme-column mechanism, so its much milder mesh requirement is not in conflict with our no-go result.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 21026 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.248Z | assistant | final_answer ===
Short answer: the construction is mostly correct, but it describes a different initialization ensemble. The learning-rate powers are essentially the same as ours; the positive result comes from replacing the iid Gaussian connector by a coherent neuron-embedded kernel.

## Exact comparison

For our normalized quadratic activation \(\phi(x)=x^2/\sqrt3\), set

\[
a_i=u_i,\qquad
b_{ji}=\frac{W_{ji}}{\sqrt{3n}},\qquad
c_j=\frac{a_j^{\mathrm{out}}}{n\sqrt3}.
\]

Then our network becomes exactly

\[
f_n=\sum_j c_j\left(\sum_i b_{ji}a_i^2\right)^2.
\]

Ignoring fixed \(\sqrt3\) factors and the factor two between half-loss and full loss, the original muP update induces

\[
(\eta_a,\eta_b,\eta_c)=(nh,h,h/n).
\]

So that mobility ratio is not a competing scaling—it is our muP scaling expressed in the effective coordinates \((a,b,c)\).

What differs is:

| | Original iid OMFP | Proposed nested model |
|---|---:|---:|
| \(\operatorname{Var}(b_{ji})\) | \(\Theta(n^{-1})\) | \(\Theta(n^{-2})\) |
| Connector | iid Gaussian | \(W_0(\beta_j,\alpha_i)/n\) |
| Preactivation | Gaussian/CLT sum | Coherent LLN integral |
| Layer dependence | Independent | Correlated through neuron types |
| Witness \(F_0\) | \(F_0\to0\) | \(F_0=1/2\) |

In the nested model,

\[
Z_j=\frac1n\sum_i
W_0(\beta_j,\alpha_i)A_0(\alpha_i)^2
\longrightarrow
\int W_0(\beta_j,\alpha)A_0(\alpha)^2\,d\mu(\alpha),
\]

which can be nonzero because \(W_0\) and \(A_0^2\) share the same latent type.

By contrast, if \(b_{ji}\) were genuinely independent and centered with variance \(\sigma^2/n^2\), then

\[
\mathbb E[Z_j^2\mid a]
=
\frac{\sigma^2}{n^2}\sum_i a_i^4
=
O_{\mathbb P}(n^{-1}),
\]

so \(Z_j\to0\). The \(n^{-2}\) variance works only because of the nested correlations.

## What checks out

I verified the following parts of the proposed derivation:

- All three raw gradients are correct.
- The simultaneous finite-step map and exact \(z^+-z\) identity are correct.
- The normalized updates for \(A,W,C\) are exact.
- The \(K_n\) and \(\Gamma_n\) expressions are correct.
- The one-step, two-step, and fixed-\(k\) loss coefficients are correct.
- The centered finite-type witness and all its stated variances and velocities are correct.
- The finite-type Euler-to-ODE argument is valid.
- The finite-type global-existence argument is valid.

The witness is “centered” only marginally. In fact,

\[
W_{ji}=v_j\left(x_i^2-\frac52\right)
\]

is rank one, \(W_{ji}\) is correlated with \(A_i^2=x_i^2\), and \(C_j\) is correlated with the same row type \(v_j\). Those dependencies are exactly what produce \(Z_0\neq0\) and \(F_0=1/2\).

## Three corrections

First, \(nh_n\to0\) is unnecessary for the nested finite-type theorem. The normalized increments are already \(O(h_n)\), so

\[
h_n\to0
\]

is sufficient. The stronger condition \(nh_n\to0\) merely forces the nominal raw number \(\eta_{a,n}=nh_n\) itself to vanish.

Second, the precise finite-type theorem is

\[
\max_{kh_n\le T}
\|x_{n,k}-x(kh_n)\|
\le
C_T(h_n+\delta_n),
\]

where \(x\) is the fixed-dimensional type-reduced state and

\[
\delta_n=\|\mu_n-\mu\|_1+\|\nu_n-\nu\|_1.
\]

Thus \(\delta_n=O(n^{-1})\) for deterministically rounded type counts and \(O_{\mathbb P}(n^{-1/2})\) for sampled finite types. This should not be interpreted as an unnormalized Euclidean estimate for the complete \(n+n^2+n\) parameter array.

Third, the proof given establishes the theorem for fixed finite type spaces with positive type masses. Arbitrary measurable latent spaces require an additional quadrature/empirical-process and stability theorem.

The fixed-\(k\) remainder \(O_k(h^3)\) is also uniform in width only while the normalized type states remain uniformly bounded. It cannot automatically be transferred to the iid Gaussian model.

## Comparison with our joint-scaling result

Your proposed requirement

\[
nh_n\to0
\]

is far weaker than our explicit iid-Gaussian ODE-shadowing condition

\[
h_n n^{14}
\exp\!\left(
\frac{6\cdot10^{11}n^9}{\sqrt{\log n}}
\right)
\to0.
\]

There is no contradiction:

- The nested finite-type vector field has width-independent local Lipschitz constants.
- The iid Gaussian model contains extreme rows/columns whose effective rates grow with \(n\).
- Our condition was a sufficient condition under which Euler follows the finite-width Gaussian ODE—and therefore inherits its initial-layer failure. It was not a proposed positive scaling.

Accordingly, \(h_n=n^{-3}\) rigorously gives the claimed limit for the finite-type nested model. It does not establish anything positive about the original iid Gaussian model; that precise iid diagonal is not covered by the present all-diagonal theorem.

## Final assessment

I agree with the result after rewriting its main conclusion as:

> The mobility \(n:1:n^{-1}\), combined with a bounded finite-type neuron-embedded \(1/n\) connector, yields a rigorous joint mean-field/gradient-flow limit. This is a graphon/nested mean-field model, not the original iid-Gaussian OMFP model.

For the iid centered connector, variance \(\Theta(n^{-1})\) is required, recovering our original Gaussian-action regime. The row-projection argument correctly proves that an ordinary entrywise empirical law cannot close that regime. It does not rule out enriched operator/response descriptions—OMFP is precisely such a description—but our quadratic initial-layer theorem does rule out a classical continuous initialized limit in the width-first and proved ODE-consistent regimes.

The detailed independent audit is [INDEPENDENT_COMPARISON.md](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_scaling_comparison/INDEPENDENT_COMPARISON.md).
