=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 21118 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.251Z | assistant | commentary ===
The current proof has an important history: an earlier version really did contain a broken uniform-integrability step for the raw kernel. The present theorem is the repaired version. Its repair uses one fixed auxiliary MFP mesh to establish square-tail tightness of the exact trajectory, then a separate truncation argument to remove the comparison mesh; it does not assume a moment bound uniform in the number of steps.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 21183 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.253Z | assistant | commentary ===
There is a decisive limit-order distinction. The arctangent proof is valid for the width limit of the exact finite-width gradient-flow ODE. It does not, as currently written, prove that actual discrete GD may be taken to infinite width at fixed step size and only then have its mesh removed. The auxiliary MFP Euler scheme is Euler in the transformed natural coordinate, and finite-step coordinate changes do not commute exactly with Euler.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 21207 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.254Z | assistant | final_answer ===
The current arctangent theorem is correct for the theorem it actually states, but it does not prove every possible meaning of “width-first continuous-time limit.”

| Claim | Status |
|---|---|
| Width limit of the exact finite-width gradient-flow ODE | Proved |
| Width-first auxiliary Euler scheme in the natural coordinate, then mesh removal | Proved |
| Width-first actual discrete GD at fixed \(\eta\), followed by \(\eta\to0\) | Open |
| Earlier direct-Hölder proof of kernel uniform integrability | Broken, then repaired |

So if the repository claimed

\[
\lim_{n\to\infty}
\{\text{finite-width continuous gradient flow}\},
\]

the claim survives. If it claimed

\[
\lim_{\eta\to0}
\left[
\lim_{n\to\infty}
\{\text{actual discrete GD with step }\eta\}
\right],
\]

that was too strong.

## MFP reconstruction of the valid theorem

Use

\[
X=\arctan(u),\qquad
Z=GX,\qquad
Y=\arctan(Z),\qquad
f_n=\langle A,Y\rangle_n,
\]

where \(G_0=W_0/\sqrt n\) and \(A_0,u_0,W_0\) are mutually independent standard Gaussians. Define

\[
B=\frac{A}{1+Z^2},
\qquad
Q=G^*B.
\]

Under feature ascent, with the normalized vector metrics and Frobenius matrix metric,

\[
A'=Y,\qquad
u'=\frac{Q}{1+u^2},\qquad
G'=B\otimes X,
\]

where

\[
(b\otimes x)v=b\langle x,v\rangle_n
=\frac1n bx^{\mathsf T}v.
\]

### 1. Natural-coordinate peeling

Set

\[
\rho=\Theta(u):=u+\frac{u^3}{3}.
\]

Since

\[
\Theta'(u)=1+u^2=\frac1{\phi'(u)},
\]

the inner derivative disappears exactly:

\[
\rho'=Q.
\]

Writing \(u=\Theta^{-1}(\rho)\), define

\[
\Psi(\rho)=\arctan(\Theta^{-1}(\rho)),
\qquad
c(\rho)=\frac1{1+\Theta^{-1}(\rho)^2}.
\]

Then

\[
\Psi'(\rho)=c(\rho)^2,
\]

and the feature system becomes

\[
A'=Y,\qquad
\rho'=Q,\qquad
G'=B\otimes X.
\]

All coordinate nonlinearities except the Gaussian multiplier \(A\) are now bounded and globally Lipschitz.

### 2. Exact MFP operator DAG

Write

\[
G=G_0+q,
\qquad
q(s)=\int_0^s B(\sigma)\otimes X(\sigma)\,d\sigma.
\]

On an auxiliary Euler mesh \(h\),

\[
q^k=h\sum_{\ell<k}B^\ell\otimes X^\ell.
\]

Therefore the forward and reused-adjoint nodes peel as

\[
Z^k
=
G_0X^k
+
h\sum_{\ell<k}
B^\ell\langle X^\ell,X^k\rangle_n,
\]

\[
Q^k
=
G_0^*B^k
+
h\sum_{\ell<k}
X^\ell\langle B^\ell,B^k\rangle_n.
\]

These are precisely the MFP response formulas. Every reuse of \(G_0\) and \(G_0^*\) remains in one shared two-sided Gaussian source; nothing is refreshed.

For every fixed mesh, this is a finite MFP program. Its width limit produces an immutable source

\[
\mathfrak G=(H_R,H_C;a_0,\rho_0,\Gamma),
\]

where

\[
\Gamma:H_C\to H_R,\qquad \|\Gamma\|\le2,
\]

and the reverse action is its genuine Hilbert adjoint \(\Gamma^*\).

The limiting autonomous OMFP equation is

\[
A'=Y,\qquad
\rho'=Q,\qquad
q'=B\otimes X,\qquad
G=\Gamma+q.
\]

The source \(\Gamma\) is fixed at initialization; \(q\) is the single current trace-class learned operator. Hence the state is restartable and contains no two-time response kernel.

### 3. Output and tangent kernel

Differentiating the MFP DAG gives

\[
X'=c(\rho)^2Q,
\]

\[
Z'=B\,\mathbb E[X^2]+G\{c(\rho)^2Q\}.
\]

Consequently,

\[
F'(s)=K(s),
\]

with

\[
K
=
\mathbb E[Y^2]
+
\mathbb E[B^2]\mathbb E[X^2]
+
\mathbb E[c(\rho)^2Q^2]
\ge0.
\]

This is the infinite-width limit of the exact finite identity.

### 4. Global well-posedness

Since \(|X|,|Y|\le\pi/2\) and \(|B|\le|A|\),

\[
\|A(s)\|_2
\le
\|a_0\|_2+\frac{\pi}{2}|s|,
\]

\[
\|q(s)\|_1
\le
\frac{\pi}{2}
\left(
\|a_0\|_2|s|+\frac{\pi}{4}s^2
\right),
\]

and \(\|\rho(s)\|_2\) is bounded on every compact feature-time interval. Thus there is no finite-time escape.

The only delicate map is

\[
A\,d(Z),\qquad d(z)=\frac1{1+z^2},
\]

because \(A=a_0+\) bounded and \(a_0\) is Gaussian rather than \(L^\infty\). For bounded Lipschitz \(h\),

\[
\|a_0[h(z)-h(\widetilde z)]\|_2
\le
C\delta\sqrt{\log(e/\delta)},
\qquad
\delta=\|z-\widetilde z\|_2.
\]

Because

\[
\int_{0^+}
\frac{d\delta}
{\delta\sqrt{\log(e/\delta)}}=\infty,
\]

Bihari–Osgood gives uniqueness in the reachable Gaussian-envelope class. Existence follows by cutting off \(a_0\), solving the Lipschitz system, and removing the cutoff; the stability loss \(e^{C_SM}\) is dominated by the Gaussian clipping tail \(e^{-M^2/4}\).

### 5. Width identification and the repaired step

At a fixed readout cutoff and fixed auxiliary mesh:

- the MFP DAG above has a deterministic width limit;
- both finite- and infinite-width exact flows differ from their auxiliary Euler schemes by \(O(h)\), uniformly in width.

The raw term

\[
\mathbb E[c(\rho)^2Q^2]
\]

requires square uniform integrability. The earlier proof incorrectly tried to use a \((2+\varepsilon)\)-moment constant uniformly as \(h\to0\).

The repaired argument uses

\[
q^2\mathbf 1_{\{|q|>R\}}
\le
4(q-v)^2
+
2v^2\mathbf 1_{\{|v|>R/2\}}.
\]

One first fixes an auxiliary mesh. MFP then supplies its finite \((2+\varepsilon)\)-moments. The \(O(h)\) Euler comparison transfers these tails to the exact continuous trajectory. Only after square-tail tightness has been established does one introduce a separate comparison mesh, truncate at fixed \(R\), and remove that mesh. No moment constant uniform in the number of steps is assumed.

This closes convergence of \(K_n\), not merely of the bounded predictor.

### 6. Loss-gradient clock

For the full squared loss, define feature time by

\[
\dot s=2\eta e,
\qquad
e=y-F(s).
\]

Then

\[
\dot e=-2\eta eK(s),
\]

so

\[
e(t)
=
y\exp\left(
-2\eta\int_0^tK(s(\tau))\,d\tau
\right),
\]

and

\[
|s(t)|\le2\eta|y|t.
\]

Thus the physical trajectory is global and unique, and

\[
f(t)+e(t)=y,
\qquad
\dot{\mathcal L}(t)=-4\eta K(t)\mathcal L(t).
\]

The resulting theorem is

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\left(
|f_n(t)-f(t)|
+
|K_n(t)-K(t)|
+
|(y-f_n(t))^2-e(t)^2|
\right)
\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0.
\]

## Why this does not yet prove width-first actual GD

Actual Euler updates \(u\), not \(\rho\):

\[
u^+=u+h\frac{Q}{1+u^2}.
\]

Transforming this exact discrete update gives

\[
\rho^+
=
\rho+hQ
+
h^2\frac{uQ^2}{(1+u^2)^2}
+
\frac{h^3Q^3}{3(1+u^2)^3}.
\]

The auxiliary MFP scheme uses only \(\rho^+=\rho+hQ\). At finite width the additional terms vanish in the ordinary mesh limit. After width is taken first, proving their accumulated disappearance requires mesh-uniform reachable \(L^4/L^6\) control of \(Q\), or an equivalent response-tail theorem. The existing square-\(L^2\) uniform-integrability proof does not supply that.

So the final answer is:

- The repaired arctangent proof is valid for the width limit of the exact finite-width continuous gradient flow.
- The old kernel proof contained a real gap, but that gap was repaired.
- The stronger width-first-actual-discrete-GD mesh theorem remains open.
- The MFP reconstruction above proves the valid theorem, conditional only on the fixed finite two-sided MFP master law. A fully self-contained MFP proof would additionally need to reprove its adaptive Gaussian-conditioning and singular-core theorem.

The full reconstruction is in [MFP_RECONSTRUCTION.md](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_arctan_mfp_reconstruction/MFP_RECONSTRUCTION.md). The current source theorem is [ARCTAN_THEOREM_AND_PROOF.md](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/nonlinear_activation_operator_ide/ARCTAN_THEOREM_AND_PROOF.md).

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 21217 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.255Z | user | None ===
the following claism to have proven the limit exists for arctan , L=2 case, do you agree or not?

Yes. The requested joint limit exists.

Take

\[
\boxed{\phi(u)=\arctan u}
\]

and

\[
\boxed{
\sigma_{1,n}^2=1,\qquad
\sigma_{2,n}^2=\frac1n,\qquad
\sigma_{3,n}^2=\frac1{n^4},
}
\]

\[
\boxed{
\lambda_n^{(1)}=n,\qquad
\lambda_n^{(2)}=1,\qquad
\lambda_n^{(3)}=\frac1n,\qquad
\eta_{0,n}=\frac1{n^2}.
}
\]

Then \(\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(\ell)}\to0\) for every layer.

## 1. Exact finite-width equations

Use normalized inner products \(\langle u,v\rangle_n=n^{-1}u^\top v\), and set

\[
A=W^{(1)},\qquad B=W^{(2)},\qquad C=nW^{(3)}.
\]

Write

\[
H=\arctan A,\qquad S=BH,\qquad G=\arctan S,
\]

\[
q(u)=\frac1{1+u^2},\qquad D=C\odot q(S),
\qquad e=\langle C,G\rangle_n-1.
\]

Thus \(f_n=\langle C,G\rangle_n\). For \(u\) on the left and \(v\) on the right, define

\[
(u\otimes_n v)w=u\langle v,w\rangle_n .
\]

The layer-scaled gradient flow is exactly

\[
\dot A=-2e\,q(A)\odot B^*D,
\]

\[
\dot B=-2e\,D\otimes_n H,
\]

\[
\dot C=-2e\,G.
\tag{1}
\]

The three kernel blocks are

\[
K_n^{(1)}
=
\left\|q(A)\odot B^*D\right\|_n^2,
\]

\[
K_n^{(2)}
=
\|H\|_n^2\|D\|_n^2,
\]

\[
K_n^{(3)}
=
\|G\|_n^2.
\tag{2}
\]

Consequently,

\[
\dot f_n=-2eK_n,\qquad
\dot L_n=-4L_nK_n.
\tag{3}
\]

The hidden-layer velocities are

\[
V_n^{(1)2}=4e^2K_n^{(1)}
\tag{4}
\]

and

\[
\dot S
=
-2e
\left[
\|H\|_n^2D+
B\!\left(q(A)^2\odot B^*D\right)
\right].
\tag{5}
\]

## 2. The regularizing coordinate

The convergence obstruction in the original \(A\)-coordinate is the product
\(q(A)B^*D\). Define instead

\[
F(a)=a+\frac{a^3}{3},\qquad X=F(A).
\]

Since \(F'(a)=1+a^2=q(a)^{-1}\),

\[
\dot X=-2eB^*D.
\tag{6}
\]

Moreover, \(F\) is a bijection, its inverse \(J=F^{-1}\) is globally 1-Lipschitz, and

\[
H(X)=\arctan J(X)
\]

is globally Lipschitz because

\[
H'(X)=q(J(X))^2\le1.
\]

In \((X,B,C)\), the closed evolution is

\[
\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
A&=J(X),\\
H&=\arctan A,\\
S&=BH,\\
G&=\arctan S,\\
D&=Cq(S),\\
e&=\langle C,G\rangle-1,\\
\dot X&=-2eB^*D,\\
\dot B&=-2eD\otimes H,\\
\dot C&=-2eG.
\end{aligned}}
\tag{7}
\]

On sets where \(\|B\|_{\rm op}\) and \(\|C\|_\infty\) are bounded, this vector field is dimension-independently Lipschitz in

\[
\|X-\widetilde X\|_2+
\|C-\widetilde C\|_2+
\|B-\widetilde B\|_{\rm op}.
\]

Indeed,

\[
\|S-\widetilde S\|_2
\le
\|B-\widetilde B\|_{\rm op}\|H\|_2
+
\|\widetilde B\|_{\rm op}\|X-\widetilde X\|_2,
\]

and, using bounded Lipschitzness of \(q,\arctan,H\),

\[
\|D-\widetilde D\|_2
\le
\|C-\widetilde C\|_2+
\|\widetilde C\|_\infty\,O(\|S-\widetilde S\|_2).
\]

The rank-one identity

\[
\|u\otimes v\|_{\rm op}=\|u\|_2\|v\|_2
\]

completes the Lipschitz estimate.

Equation (3) gives \(|e(t)|\le |e(0)|\). Hence, on every finite interval,

\[
\|C(t)\|_\infty
\le
\|C(0)\|_\infty+\pi |e(0)|t,
\]

and integration of (7) gives dimension-independent bounds for \(\|B(t)\|_{\rm op}\) and \(\|X(t)\|_2\).

## 3. The autonomous mean-field state

The middle matrix cannot converge as an ordinary graphon: its entries vanish while its operator action remains order one. The correct state is its current cyclic action law.

Starting from the right mark \(X\), the left mark \(C\), constants, \(B,B^*\), normalized inner products, and bounded Lipschitz coordinate maps, form every finite typed probe program. The action state records the joint empirical law of every finite same-side tuple of probes. Enumerating a countable dense family and summing the associated truncated \(W_2\) distances defines a metric.

This is not a selected-observable state: it contains every finite expression obtainable from the current parameters by the exact forward/backward algebra. In particular it determines (7), every kernel block, both hidden velocities, output and loss.

The limiting probe laws give two commutative coordinate algebras. Quotienting by their limiting \(L^2\) null seminorms and completing gives two separable GNS \(L^2\) spaces. Since

\[
\|B_n(0)\|_{\rm op}\le 3
\]

with probability tending to one, the limiting \(B\) extends to a bounded operator between these spaces, with \(B^*\) its adjoint. Thus the action state has an actual operator realization, rather than being merely a formal moment sequence.

A rank-one update is closed on this state because

\[
(B+\theta D\otimes H)v
=
Bv+\theta D\langle H,v\rangle,
\]

and similarly for the adjoint. Therefore one Euler step of (7) is an autonomous map of the current action law.

## 4. Full-sequence convergence

The only imported result is the Tensor Program Master Theorem: for a fixed finite program initialized by independent \(N(0,1/n)\) matrices and independent Gaussian coordinate vectors, with pseudo-Lipschitz coordinate maps and continuous scalar Moment maps, empirical averages of every pseudo-Lipschitz function of finitely many program vectors converge almost surely, and all scalar program variables converge. This is [Yang–Hu, Theorem 7.4](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2011.14522).

Its hypotheses hold here:

- \(B_0\) has iid \(N(0,1/n)\) entries.
- \(X_0=F(A_0)=A_0+A_0^3/3\) is a polynomial Gaussian coordinate map.
- \(J=F^{-1}\), \(H=\arctan\circ J\), \(q\), and \(\arctan\) are globally Lipschitz or polynomially bounded.
- At any fixed Euler mesh, \(C\) is uniformly bounded.
- Inner products and \(e\) are allowed Moment scalars.
- Reuse of \(B_0,B_0^*\) is covered.

For fixed mesh \(\delta\) and finitely many steps,

\[
B_k
=
B_0-2\delta\sum_{r<k} e_rD_r\otimes H_r.
\]

Thus every multiplication by \(B_k\) or \(B_k^*\) reduces to \(B_0,B_0^*\), coordinate maps, and normalized inner products: it is a fixed finite tensor program. Applying the theorem simultaneously to the countable probe family gives full-sequence convergence of the fixed-mesh action states.

The dimension-independent Lipschitz estimate gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\left(
\|X_n(t)-X_n^\delta(t)\|_2+
\|C_n(t)-C_n^\delta(t)\|_2+
\|B_n(t)-B_n^\delta(t)\|_{\rm op}
\right)
\le C_T\delta.
\tag{8}
\]

Applying the fixed-program theorem jointly to two meshes \(\delta,\delta'\), sharing the same initialization, shows that their limiting probe laws form a Cauchy family in \(W_2\). Continuous maps such as

\[
(X,U)\longmapsto q(J(X))U
\]

are handled by joint \(W_2\) convergence and the bound \(|q(J(X))U|\le |U|\). This proves convergence of the first-layer kernel and of the \(B[q(A)^2B^*D]\) term in (5), including the required uniform integrability.

The limit

\[
\mathfrak S(t)
=
\lim_{\delta\downarrow0}
\mathcal E_\delta^{\lfloor t/\delta\rfloor}\mathfrak S(0)
\]

therefore exists uniformly on compact intervals. The same stability estimate proves uniqueness. Since every Euler map uses only the current action law,

\[
\Phi_{t+s}=\Phi_t\Phi_s,
\]

so the limiting flow is autonomous and restartable. It retains neither the finite array nor its history.

For path laws, on a mesh interval \(I\),

\[
\frac1n\sum_i
\sup_{s,t\in I}|z_i(t)-z_i(s)|^2
\le
|I|\int_I V_n(t)^2\,dt.
\]

Combining this with convergence at mesh points gives convergence in \(W_2(C([0,T]))\) for both raw preactivation path laws. The same estimate establishes tightness and path-square uniform integrability.

## 5. Exact GD and the joint diagonal limit

The \(B\)- and \(C\)-updates of exact GD are exactly Euler updates of (7). Only the \(X\)-update has a defect.

Let

\[
R=-2eB^*D.
\]

One exact first-layer GD step is

\[
A^+=A+\eta q(A)R.
\]

The cubic identity

\[
F(A+\eta q(A)R)
=
F(A)+\eta R
+
\eta^2Aq(A)^2R^2
+
\frac{\eta^3}{3}q(A)^3R^3
\]

is exact. Since \(\sup_a|a|q(a)^2<\infty\),

\[
\|\rho_{\rm step}\|_2
\le
C_T\left(\eta^2\sqrt n+\eta^3n\right),
\]

using \(\|R\|_\infty\le\sqrt n\|R\|_2\). Discrete Grönwall over \(T/\eta\) steps yields

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
d\bigl(\text{exact GD}_n(t),\text{flow}_n(t)\bigr)
\le
C_T\left(\eta+\eta\sqrt n+\eta^2n\right).
\]

For \(\eta=n^{-2}\), the right side tends to zero. This proves the simultaneous, rather than iterated, width/gradient-flow limit.

The actual initialization satisfies

\[
\|C_n(0)\|_2=O_{\mathbb P}(n^{-1}),
\qquad
\|C_n(0)\|_\infty\to0,
\]

so it is coupled to the limiting mark \(C(0)=0\) by the same stability estimate.

## 6. Nondegeneracy and feature learning

Let

\[
A_0\sim N(0,1),\qquad H_0=\arctan A_0,
\qquad m=\mathbb E H_0^2>0.
\]

The initial second-layer preactivation has law

\[
Z\sim N(0,m).
\]

Put

\[
G_0=\arctan Z,\qquad
U=\frac{\arctan Z}{1+Z^2},
\qquad
\nu=\mathbb E U^2>0.
\]

Let

\[
\Xi=B_0^*U,\qquad
d=\left\|q(A_0)\Xi\right\|_2^2.
\]

Gaussian conditioning gives

\[
\Xi=\sqrt{\nu}\,\Gamma+\kappa H_0,
\]

where \(\Gamma\sim N(0,1)\) is independent of \(A_0\) and
\(\kappa=\mathbb E U'(Z)\). Hence

\[
d
=
\nu\,\mathbb E q(A_0)^2+
\kappa^2\mathbb E\!\left[q(A_0)^2H_0^2\right]
>0.
\]

Define the positive operator

\[
\mathcal R
=
mI+B_0M_{q(A_0)^2}B_0^*.
\]

Then

\[
\langle U,\mathcal RU\rangle=m\nu+d>0.
\]

Expanding the limiting ODE at zero gives, in \(L^2\),

\[
C(t)=2tG_0+O(t^2),
\qquad
D(t)=2tU+O(t^2),
\]

\[
A(t)-A_0
=
2t^2q(A_0)\Xi+o(t^2),
\]

\[
S(t)-Z
=
2t^2\mathcal RU+o(t^2).
\tag{9}
\]

Therefore

\[
K^{(1)}(t)=4dt^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(2)}(t)=4m\nu t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(3)}(t)
=
k_3+4(d+m\nu)t^2+o(t^2),
\qquad
k_3=\mathbb E(\arctan Z)^2>0.
\]

Thus

\[
\boxed{
K(t)=k_3+8(d+m\nu)t^2+o(t^2),
}
\tag{10}
\]

which is finite, positive, and nonconstant.

Likewise,

\[
V^{(1)}(t)^2=16dt^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
V^{(2)}(t)^2
=
16t^2\|\mathcal RU\|_2^2+o(t^2).
\tag{11}
\]

Both coefficients are strictly positive. From (9),

\[
\mathbb E|A(t)-A_0|^2
=
4dt^4+o(t^4)>0,
\]

\[
\mathbb E|S(t)-S_0|^2
=
4\|\mathcal RU\|_2^2t^4+o(t^4)>0.
\]

Hence both raw hidden layers undergo macroscopic feature motion, and all three kernel blocks have positive time integrals.

The middle operator itself is not frozen:

\[
B(t)-B(0)
=
2t^2U\otimes H_0+o_{\rm op}(t^2),
\]

whose leading operator norm is positive. No magnified \(B-B_0\) tangent mark is retained; the current physical action operator \(B(t)\) moves at leading order and is used nonlinearly in \(S(t)=B(t)H(t)\).

## 7. Hidden variances, nonlinearity, and loss

Initially,

\[
\operatorname{Var}(A_0)=1,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(S_0)=m>0.
\]

Their \(W_2\)-continuous path laws therefore retain finite positive variance on a sufficiently short interval \([0,T_*]\).

For a nondegenerate Gaussian \(Y\),

\[
\inf_{a,b}
\mathbb E|\arctan Y-aY-b|^2>0,
\]

since equality would force \(\arctan\) to agree with an affine function on a full-support measure, and hence everywhere. This regression residual depends continuously on the five moments

\[
\mathbb EY,\quad
\mathbb EY^2,\quad
\mathbb E\arctan Y,\quad
\mathbb EY\arctan Y,\quad
\mathbb E(\arctan Y)^2
\]

while the variance remains bounded below. Thus both nonlinear residuals remain positive on a short interval.

Finally,

\[
f(0)=0\ne1,
\]

and

\[
f(t)=2k_3t+o(t),
\qquad
L(t)=1-4k_3t+o(t)<1
\]

for every sufficiently small \(t>0\).

Therefore, for some \(T_*>0\), every compact interval \([0,T]\subseteq[0,T_*]\) satisfies all six requested conditions: full joint compact-time convergence, autonomous well-posed action-law dynamics, finite nondegenerate hidden laws, feature learning in both hidden layers, all three active parameter blocks, surviving nonlinearity, evolving kernel, and decreasing leading-order loss.

I’m checking both points separately, because either one can make the statement converge while also making it a different—or degenerate—model.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 21421 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.261Z | assistant | final_answer ===
Qualified yes: the simultaneous diagonal limit is valid, but the displayed argument needs several corrections. It does not prove the original width-first limit, nor does it reproduce the earlier arctan initialization.

| Claim | Verdict |
|---|---|
| Finite-width equations | Correct |
| Natural coordinate \(X=A+A^3/3\) | Correct |
| Joint scaling \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\) | Works |
| Same initialization as the earlier theorem | False |
| Iterated limit \(n\to\infty\) first, then \(\eta\to0\) | Not proved |
| All parameter blocks active at \(t=0\) | False |
| Complete width-limit justification as written | Needs repair |

The decisive calculation supporting the joint limit is sound. If

\[
R=-2eB^*D,\qquad q(a)=\frac1{1+a^2},
\]

then exact GD gives

\[
A^+=A+\eta q(A)R
\]

and therefore exactly

\[
X^+
=
X+\eta R
+\eta^2Aq(A)^2R^2
+\frac{\eta^3}{3}q(A)^3R^3.
\]

Since

\[
\sup_a |a q(a)^2|=\frac9{16\sqrt3},
\qquad
\|R\|_\infty\le\sqrt n\,\|R\|_n,
\]

the defect from Euler evolution in \(X\) satisfies

\[
\|\rho_k\|_n
\le
\frac9{16\sqrt3}\eta^2\sqrt n\,\|R_k\|_n^2
+\frac13\eta^3n\,\|R_k\|_n^3.
\]

The required uniform bound on \(R_k\) can be proved explicitly. Writing \(a=\pi/2\),

\[
\|C_{k+1}\|_n
\le
(1+2a^2\eta)\|C_k\|_n+2a\eta,
\]

and discrete Grönwall then bounds \(\|C_k\|_n\). Direct summation subsequently bounds \(\|C_k\|_\infty\) and \(\|B_k\|_{\mathrm{op}}\), while

\[
\|R_k\|_n
\le
2|e_k|\,\|B_k\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|C_k\|_n .
\]

Consequently, on every compact macroscopic interval,

\[
\sup_{k\eta\le T}
d_n\!\left(\theta^{\mathrm{GD}}_{n,k},
\theta^{\mathrm{flow}}_n(k\eta)\right)
\le
C_T\bigl(\eta+\eta\sqrt n+\eta^2n\bigr).
\]

Hence \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\) works. In fact, this part works whenever

\[
\eta_n\sqrt n\longrightarrow0.
\]

There are nevertheless four material qualifications.

1. This is a different initialization.

Because \(C=nW^{(3)}\),

\[
\operatorname{Var}(C_{0,j})
=n^2\operatorname{Var}(W^{(3)}_{0,j})
=n^{-2},
\]

so

\[
C_0\longrightarrow0.
\]

The earlier repo theorem has an \(O(1)\) Gaussian normalized readout, corresponding to raw top-weight variance \(n^{-2}\), not \(n^{-4}\). The new zero-readout model is analytically easier.

2. The result is a simultaneous diagonal limit:

\[
n\to\infty,\qquad
\eta_n=n^{-2},\qquad
k_n\eta_n\to t.
\]

It does not establish

\[
\lim_{\eta\to0}\left[
\lim_{n\to\infty}
\mathrm{GD}_{n,\eta}^{\lfloor t/\eta\rfloor}
\right].
\]

Thus it does not resolve the width-first fixed-\(\eta\) problem discussed earlier.

3. The width argument requires the repaired OMFP source theorem.

The reused \(B_0,B_0^*\) programs have singular Gram matrices because \(C_0=D_0=0\). Yang–Hu Theorem 7.4 by itself is not a sufficient citation unless its singular-core extension is supplied. One must use the repaired two-sided source construction, including null-space preservation and the exact adjoint identity.

Likewise, \(B_n\) must not be claimed to converge across widths in operator norm. Write

\[
B=\Gamma+Q
\]

with immutable Gaussian action \(\Gamma\), and compare the learned part \(Q\) in trace norm/action-law topology. Operator norm is only a uniform size bound.

4. At \(t=0\),

\[
K_3(0)>0,\qquad K_1(0)=K_2(0)=0.
\]

The hidden blocks become active at order \(t^2\):

\[
K_2(t)
=
4t^2\mathbb E[H_0^2]\mathbb E[U^2]+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K_1(t)
=
4t^2\mathbb E[q(A_0)^2\Xi^2]+o(t^2)>0,
\]

where

\[
\Xi=\mathbb E[U'(Z)]H_0+\sqrt{\mathbb E U^2}\,\Gamma_0.
\]

So the correct final verdict is:

\[
\boxed{\text{The altered zero-readout joint limit exists, after the stated repairs.}}
\]

But:

\[
\boxed{\text{The displayed argument is neither the original arctan theorem nor a width-first proof.}}
\]

The detailed scaling and small-time audit is in [ZERO_READOUT_SCALING_AUDIT.md](/home/amir/Codes/PDE/studies/mean_field_peeling/temporary_arctan_mfp_reaudit/ZERO_READOUT_SCALING_AUDIT.md).

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 21431 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.261Z | user | None ===
DO NOT LOOK AT ANY OTHER PROJECT FILES FOR THIS TASK. THIS IS A. FULLY SELF CONTIANED list&#x20;

\# Task: nonlinear joint mean-field/gradient-flow scaling

For each width \\(n\\), consider the bias-free network trained on \\((x,y)=(1,1)\\):

\\[
z\_i^{(1)}=W\_i^{(1)},
\\]

\\[
z\_j^{(2)}
\=
\sum\_{i=1}^{n}
W\_{ji}^{(2)}
\phi\\!\left(z\_i^{(1)}\right),
\\]

\\[
f\_n
\=
\sum\_{j=1}^{n}
W\_j^{(3)}
\phi\\!\left(z\_j^{(2)}\right),
\qquad
L\_n=(f\_n-1)^2.
\\]

The benchmark activation is \\(\phi(z)=z^2\\). An affirmative result may instead use any fixed, width-independent, non-affine \\(\phi\in C^2(\mathbb R)\\) whose first two derivatives have polynomial growth.

Initialize all entries independently:

\\[
W\_i^{(1)}(0)\sim N(0,\sigma\_{1,n}^2),
\\]

\\[
W\_{ji}^{(2)}(0)\sim N(0,\sigma\_{2,n}^2),
\\]

\\[
W\_j^{(3)}(0)\sim N(0,\sigma\_{3,n}^2),
\\]

where the positive variances are arbitrary deterministic functions of \\(n\\).

Use exact full-batch GD:

\\[
W\_{k+1}^{(\ell)}
\=
W\_k^{(\ell)}
\-
\eta\_{0,n}\lambda\_n^{(\ell)}
\nabla\_{W^{(\ell)}}L\_n,
\qquad
\ell=1,2,3,
\\]

with

\\[
\eta\_{0,n}\to0,
\qquad
\lambda\_n^{(\ell)}>0,
\qquad
\eta\_{0,n}\lambda\_n^{(\ell)}\to0.
\\]

No normalization or boundedness is imposed on \\(\lambda\_n^{(\ell)}\\); each may vanish, remain bounded, or diverge. Interpolate at time \\(t=k\eta\_{0,n}\\). The required finite nonzero dynamics below determine the macroscopic clock.

Since \\(x=1\\), proofs may optimize \\(z^{(1)}\\) directly using the exact identity \\(z^{(1)}=W^{(1)}\\); this does not remove the first trainable layer.

Determine whether there exist an admissible activation and hyperparameter sequence such that, along the full sequence \\(n\to\infty\\), the exact interpolated GD dynamics converge on every compact interval \\([0,T]\\) inside some nonzero existence interval to a well-posed, autonomous, genuinely nonlinear mean-field gradient flow satisfying all conditions below.

\## Required properties

1\. \*\*Full joint convergence.\*\* &#x20;
&#x20;  The empirical network state converges in probability, uniformly on \\([0,T]\\), in a topology strong enough to pass every term in the exact gradient vector field and determine both hidden-layer laws, every layer update, the kernel, output, and loss. The proof must establish tightness, sufficient uniform integrability, identification, well-posedness, uniqueness, and vanishing discrete-GD error jointly as \\(n\to\infty\\) and \\(\eta\_{0,n}\to0\\).

2\. \*\*Closed mean-field dynamics.\*\* &#x20;
&#x20;  The limit is an \\(n\\)-independent, autonomous, restartable law-level evolution. It may contain conditional or edge laws genuinely required by the middle layer, but may not retain the finite parameter array, its history, or an externally prescribed trajectory.

3\. \*\*Finite nondegenerate hidden states.\*\* &#x20;
&#x20;  For \\(\ell=1,2\\), the empirical preactivation path laws

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  \overline\mu\_n^{(\ell)}
&#x20;  \=
&#x20;  \frac1n\sum\_i
&#x20;  \delta\_{z\_i^{(\ell)}(\cdot)}
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  converge in a Wasserstein path topology strong enough to control all required moments. Their limiting marginals have finite, strictly positive variance.

4\. \*\*Feature learning in both hidden layers.\*\* &#x20;
&#x20;  Define

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  V\_n^{(\ell)}(t)^2
&#x20;  \=
&#x20;  \frac1n\sum\_i
&#x20;  \left|\dot z\_i^{(\ell)}(t)\right|^2.
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  For \\(\ell=1,2\\), these converge in a compact-time mode sufficient to pass time integrals to finite limits satisfying

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  0<
&#x20;  \int\_0^T V^{(\ell)}(t)^2\\,dt
&#x20;  <\infty.
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  The limiting paths also have nonzero macroscopic displacement:

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  \sup\_{0\le t\le T}
&#x20;  \int
&#x20;  |z(t)-z(0)|^2\\,
&#x20;  d\overline\mu^{(\ell)}(z)
&#x20;  \>0.
&#x20;  \\]

5\. \*\*Every parameter block remains active.\*\* &#x20;
&#x20;  Define

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  K\_n^{(\ell)}(t)
&#x20;  \=
&#x20;  \lambda\_n^{(\ell)}
&#x20;  \left\\|
&#x20;  \nabla\_{W^{(\ell)}}f\_n(t)
&#x20;  \right\\|^2,
&#x20;  \qquad
&#x20;  K\_n=\sum\_{\ell=1}^{3}K\_n^{(\ell)}.
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  Each \\(K\_n^{(\ell)}\\) converges to a finite limit satisfying

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  0<
&#x20;  \int\_0^T K^{(\ell)}(t)\\,dt
&#x20;  <\infty.
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  Thus all three parameter blocks contribute at leading order.

6\. \*\*Nonlinearity survives in both hidden layers.\*\* &#x20;
&#x20;  For the limiting marginal \\(\mu\_t^{(\ell)}\\), define

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  \mathcal N^{(\ell)}(t)
&#x20;  \=
&#x20;  \inf\_{a,b\in\mathbb R}
&#x20;  \int
&#x20;  |\phi(z)-az-b|^2\\,
&#x20;  d\mu\_t^{(\ell)}(z).
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  Require

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  \int\_0^T
&#x20;  \mathcal N^{(\ell)}(t)\\,dt
&#x20;  \>0,
&#x20;  \qquad
&#x20;  \ell=1,2.
&#x20;  \\]

7\. \*\*Kernel and loss move at leading order.\*\* &#x20;
&#x20;  Require uniform compact-time convergence

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  K\_n\to K,
&#x20;  \qquad
&#x20;  f\_n\to f,
&#x20;  \qquad
&#x20;  L\_n\to L,
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  to finite limits with

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  f(0)\ne1,
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  \sup\_{0\le t\le T}|K(t)-K(0)|>0,
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  and

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  L(T)\<L(0).
&#x20;  \\]

\## Proof-search strategy: crux–workbench loop

Do not attack the entire theorem only at a high level. Repeatedly apply the following discovery loop without weakening the final proof obligations:

1\. Select the smallest nondegenerate subproblem retaining one essential difficulty, such as nonlinear feedback, dependence generation, competing scales, nonclosure, instability, or moment growth.

2\. State what was simplified, which essential mechanism remains, what solving the crux would establish, and what bridge to the full problem would still require proof. Reject reductions that become linear, lazy, frozen, or decoupled.

3\. Choose an exactly tractable workbench and calculate through the first stage at which the hard feedback mechanism appears, preferably one stage further.

4\. Preserve intermediate structure: exact updates, generated dependencies, scaling balances, cancellations, symmetries, recurrences, invariants, moment hierarchies, degree growth, and closure residuals.

5\. Convert observed patterns into explicit falsifiable lemmas, then test the next case, perturb the simplifying assumptions, inspect boundary scalings, and search for counterexamples.

6\. Seek a uniform lifting argument—such as induction, generating functions, diagrammatics, martingales, energy estimates, stability, or compactness—and prove the bridge from the crux to the original system.

7\. Treat finite calculations only as discovery tools. A finite-step pattern, simulation, or correct fixed-order expansion is not compact-time convergence.

8\. Preserve valid identities, necessary scaling laws, and counterexamples from failed routes. Failure of one crux or representation defeats only that route.

The quadratic activation is a preferred initial workbench because finite-width GD iterates are polynomial functions of Gaussian initialization, making exact conditional moments and finite-step calculations available. Its purpose is to expose a provable recurrence, invariant, scaling law, hierarchy, or obstruction.

\## Forbidden shortcuts

The following are inadmissible:

\- freezing any layer or allowing any layer contribution to vanish;
\- feature learning in only one hidden layer;
\- vanishing or divergent hidden feature scales;
\- divergent-background motion with vanishing relative feature change;
\- frozen-feature, NTK, tangent-mark, or initialization-linearized limits;
\- affine or deep-linear limiting dynamics;
\- width-dependent activations;
\- biases, centering, normalization, residual connections, trainable scales, or changes to the forward map;
\- momentum, adaptive optimization, clipping, weight decay, noise, gradient modification, or changes to GD;
\- convergence of only the output, loss, kernel, finitely many moments, or another nonclosing surrogate;
\- an imposed \\(n\\)-dependent clock normalization unrelated to the required finite nonzero limiting dynamics;
\- separate width and gradient-flow limits without uniform estimates proving the joint diagonal limit;
\- formal or nonrigorous DMFT arguments in place of a theorem.

Fixed deterministic preactivation, postactivation, or output multipliers are exact reparameterizations and must be absorbed into the permitted variances and learning-rate scalers.

\## Resolution standard

An affirmative resolution must exhibit one fixed admissible nonlinear activation, one explicit hyperparameter sequence, a closed limiting mean-field gradient flow, and a proof of every required property.

A negative resolution must rule out every admissible hyperparameter sequence for every activation in the stated class. Failure of one activation, scaling ansatz, closure, crux, expansion, or proof method is not nonexistence.

Any external theorem must be stated precisely and all hypotheses verified. If neither complete theorem is established, the task remains unresolved.

<skill>
<name>solve-math-rigorously</name>
<path>/etc/codex/skills/solve-math-rigorously/SKILL.md</path>
---
name: solve-math-rigorously
description: Produce compact but complete solutions to standalone mathematical questions and proof requests. Use when the user asks to solve, prove, derive, analyze, verify, or correct a mathematical problem that is not primarily an incremental lesson about supplied technical documents. Infer the strongest reasonable interpretation without inventing assumptions, establish a minimal setup, use minimal notation, expose every nontrivial step, state and verify external theorem hypotheses, give proof architecture when useful, and silently audit the draft before answering.
---

# Solve Mathematics Rigorously

Produce a complete mathematical answer rather than a staged document lesson, unless the user explicitly requests incremental pacing. Optimize simultaneously for correctness, rigor, clarity, and economy.

## Determine the intended problem

Interpret the strongest reasonable version supported by the wording and mathematical context. Do not exploit an omission to answer a trivial or uninteresting straw-man version.

Handle ambiguity according to its effect:

- If it does not change the substance, adopt the conventional interpretation and state it briefly.
- If one reasonable assumption makes the intended problem precise, state that assumption before solving.
- If several reasonable interpretations lead to materially different answers, ask for clarification or give a compact case split when that fully resolves the ambiguity.
- If a missing assumption is necessary for the claimed conclusion, do not invent it silently.

If the stated claim is false, say so and give a decisive counterexample or contradiction. Present a corrected nearby statement only when it helps, and label it as a correction rather than the original result.

Answer the problem actually asked. Do not weaken a universal claim to a convenient special case, replace an exact question with a numerical approximation, or prove a converse instead of the stated direction.

## Establish a minimal setup

Begin with a compact setup or preliminaries passage when it reduces ambiguity or supports later reasoning. State only the necessary:

- domain and assumptions;
- definitions used in the solution;
- boundary, regularity, integrability, independence, or nondegeneracy conditions that matter;
- notation that must persist through the answer.

Reuse the problem's notation whenever possible. Introduce a new symbol only when the unabbreviated expression is genuinely cumbersome and recurs often. Define every new symbol before use and state its mathematical type when that is not immediately evident.

Do not force a visible setup heading for a short elementary problem. Do not hide substantive assumptions in prose later in the solution.

## Give the architecture before a substantial proof

Before a long or conceptually difficult proof, explain the plan in a short paragraph or compact list. Identify the main steps, cases, claims, or lemmas and how they combine to yield the conclusion.

Keep the architecture at the level needed to orient the reader. Do not duplicate the detailed proof, and do not substitute a plan for the proof itself. For a routine calculation or one-step argument, omit the architecture.

## Execute every nontrivial step

Derive each nontrivial algebraic, analytic, probabilistic, geometric, combinatorial, or logical transition. Give enough detail for the user to verify the step without reconstructing missing reasoning.

In particular:

- identify the definition, identity, inequality, or prior claim being used;
- show substitutions, rearrangements, case transitions, and changes of variables that carry real mathematical content;
- track domains, signs, quantifiers, equality conditions, exceptional cases, and possible zero denominators;
- justify limit exchanges, differentiation or integration under a sign, convergence claims, and optimization steps;
- distinguish exact arguments from heuristics, approximations, and numerical evidence;
- show that intermediate claims actually imply the requested conclusion.

Avoid words such as “clearly,” “obviously,” “standard,” and “it follows” when they replace reasoning. They are acceptable only after the supporting reason has been supplied.

Do not over-explain elementary arithmetic or definitions already established. Compress routine steps while preserving the chain of verification.

## Invoke external results completely

Whenever the solution relies on a nontrivial theorem or external fact:

1. state the exact form needed, including its assumptions and conclusion;
2. verify each assumption in the present problem;
3. identify the current objects substituted into the theorem;
4. state exactly what conclusion the theorem yields here.

Do not merely name a theorem or call a fact standard. Prefer the relevant specialization over an unnecessarily general statement.

Verify a recent, specialized, source-specific, or uncertain result against a primary or authoritative source before relying on it. Cite the source near the claim. Paraphrase the relevant result unless its exact wording matters; quote only a short compliant excerpt when useful.

If the required theorem is unavailable, uncertain, or does not apply, expose the gap instead of proceeding as though it were valid.

## Use examples and checks proportionately

Include an example, boundary case, counterexample, or remark only when it clarifies an abstraction, tests a hypothesis, explains a consequence, or exposes why an assumption is needed.

Use low-cost sanity checks when they add confidence, such as:

- substituting the result back into the defining relation;
- checking dimensions, signs, or units;
- testing a boundary or small case;
- comparing with a known special case;
- deriving the same conclusion by a second short route.

Do not add examples mechanically or let them replace the general argument.

## Keep the presentation compact

Use headings only when they make a substantial solution easier to navigate. Keep notation sparse and stable. Prefer explicit expressions to disposable shorthand.

Use equations to carry mathematical content, not decoration. Avoid formula dumps, decorative summaries, and boxed equations. State the conclusion plainly.

Let the problem's complexity determine the answer length. Preserve every necessary assumption, theorem check, and logical transition, but remove repetition and exposition that does not improve understanding or verification.

## Audit and revise silently

Draft the solution, then review it before sending. Check:

1. Did the solution address the strongest reasonable interpretation rather than a straw man?
2. Were all adopted assumptions stated, and were none invented merely to force the result?
3. Was the setup sufficient but minimal?
4. Was every persistent symbol necessary and defined?
5. Did a substantial proof receive a useful architecture?
6. Can the reader verify every nontrivial transition?
7. Was every external theorem stated in the needed form, with its hypotheses checked and its conclusion applied explicitly?
8. Were recent, specialized, or uncertain results verified from an authoritative source?
9. Were edge cases, equality conditions, domains, and quantifiers handled?
10. Does the final conclusion answer the exact question?
11. Do examples or remarks earn their space?
12. Is the answer as compact as rigor allows, with no boxed equations?

Revise any failing part and repeat the audit until the answer satisfies the checklist as well as possible. Do not mention the drafting or audit process in the final answer.

</skill>

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 21818 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.272Z | assistant | final_answer ===
Yes. The task has an affirmative solution. A convenient choice is a bounded nonlinear activation with a natural coordinate that regularizes the first-layer equation.

## Theorem

Take

\[
\phi(x)=\arctan x,
\]

\[
\sigma_{1,n}^{2}=1,\qquad
\sigma_{2,n}^{2}=\frac1n,\qquad
\sigma_{3,n}^{2}=\frac1{n^{4}},
\]

and

\[
\lambda_n^{(1)}=n,\qquad
\lambda_n^{(2)}=1,\qquad
\lambda_n^{(3)}=\frac1n,\qquad
\eta_{0,n}=\frac1{n^{2}}.
\]

Then

\[
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(1)}=n^{-1},\quad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(2)}=n^{-2},\quad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(3)}=n^{-3},
\]

so all raw layerwise steps vanish.

There is a deterministic \(T_*>0\) and an autonomous pointed-action mean-field gradient flow such that, for every \(0<T\le T_*\), the exact GD interpolation converges in probability along the full sequence \(n\to\infty\), uniformly on \([0,T]\). It satisfies all seven requested properties.

The limiting state is not determined by ordinary neuron marginals or an ordinary empirical edge law. It contains one immutable two-sided Gaussian action operator and its current learned trace-class perturbation. This is necessary because reused \(W^{(2)}\) and \(W^{(2)\top}\) create order-one response terms invisible to ordinary edge laws.

## 1. Exact finite-width dynamics

Use

\[
\langle u,v\rangle_n=\frac1n u^\top v,\qquad
u\otimes_n v=\frac1nuv^\top.
\]

Set

\[
u=W^{(1)},\qquad M=W^{(2)},\qquad c=nW^{(3)},
\]

and write

\[
h=\phi(u),\qquad s=Mh,\qquad g=\phi(s),
\]

\[
q(x)=\phi'(x)=\frac1{1+x^2},\qquad
d=c\odot q(s),
\]

\[
f_n=\langle c,g\rangle_n,\qquad e=f_n-1.
\]

Since \(W^{(3)}=c/n\), this is exactly the original predictor.

Direct differentiation gives

\[
\nabla_u f_n
=
\frac1n q(u)\odot M^\top d,
\]

\[
\nabla_M f_n
=
d\otimes_n h,
\qquad
\nabla_{W^{(3)}}f_n=g.
\]

Therefore exact GD is

\[
u^+
=
u-2\eta e\,q(u)\odot M^\top d,
\]

\[
M^+
=
M-2\eta e\,d\otimes_n h,
\]

\[
c^+
=
c-2\eta e\,g,
\tag{1}
\]

where \(\eta=\eta_{0,n}\).

The three kernel blocks are exactly

\[
K_n^{(1)}
=
\left\|q(u)\odot M^\top d\right\|_n^2,
\]

\[
K_n^{(2)}
=
\|h\|_n^2\|d\|_n^2,
\]

\[
K_n^{(3)}
=
\|g\|_n^2.
\tag{2}
\]

For the associated finite-width gradient flow,

\[
\dot f_n=-2eK_n,
\qquad
\dot L_n=-4e^2K_n,
\qquad
K_n=\sum_{\ell=1}^3K_n^{(\ell)}.
\tag{3}
\]

The hidden velocities are

\[
\dot u=-2e\,q(u)\odot M^\top d,
\qquad
V_n^{(1)2}=4e^2K_n^{(1)},
\tag{4}
\]

and

\[
\dot s
=
-2e
\left[
\|h\|_n^2d+
M\!\left(q(u)^2\odot M^\top d\right)
\right].
\tag{5}
\]

Equation (5) retains the reused-matrix response; replacing
\(Mq(u)^2M^\top\) by a scalar multiple of the identity would be incorrect.

## 2. The natural coordinate

Define

\[
\Theta(a)=a+\frac{a^3}{3},
\qquad
x=\Theta(u),
\qquad
J=\Theta^{-1}.
\]

Since

\[
\Theta'(a)=1+a^2=\frac1{q(a)},
\]

the gradient-flow equation becomes

\[
\dot x=-2eM^\top d.
\tag{6}
\]

Also,

\[
u=J(x),\qquad
h=\Psi(x):=\arctan J(x),
\]

with

\[
|J'(x)|=q(J(x))\le1,
\qquad
\Psi'(x)=q(J(x))^2\le1.
\tag{7}
\]

Thus \(J,\Psi\) are globally Lipschitz. This removes the uncontrolled product that would arise when trying to prove ambient-\(L^2\) Lipschitzness directly in \(u\).

Write

\[
M=\Gamma_n+Q_n,\qquad \Gamma_n=M(0).
\]

Then

\[
\dot Q_n=-2e\,d\otimes_n h.
\tag{8}
\]

The learned part satisfies

\[
\|d\otimes_n h\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
=
\|d\|_n\|h\|_n,
\]

so \(Q_n\) is naturally controlled in trace norm.

## 3. The required Gaussian-action source

We use the following specialization of the two-sided Tensor Program Master Theorem.

> Let \(\Gamma_n\) have independent \(N(0,1/n)\) entries, independently of finitely many iid seed vectors with moments of every order. Fix a finite program formed from \(\Gamma_n,\Gamma_n^\top\), coordinatewise pseudo-Lipschitz maps, finite linear combinations, and normalized empirical moments. Then all finite same-side joint empirical laws and all scalar moments converge almost surely to deterministic master laws. The conclusion permits repeated use of \(\Gamma_n,\Gamma_n^\top\) and singular limiting Gram matrices.

This is the precise content needed from the Tensor Program Master Theorem, [Yang–Hu, Theorem G.4 and Assumption L.4](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf), based on the extended two-sided theorem in [Tensor Programs III](https://arxiv.org/abs/2009.10685).

Its hypotheses hold here:

- \(\Gamma_n\) is iid \(N(0,1/n)\).
- The initial seed \(x_0=\Theta(u_0)\) is a polynomial Gaussian map.
- \(J,\Psi,q,\arctan\) are globally Lipschitz.
- Products and moment maps are pseudo-Lipschitz with polynomial growth.
- At a fixed auxiliary Euler mesh, only finitely many operations occur.
- Reuse of \(\Gamma_n,\Gamma_n^\top\) is explicitly allowed.
- The zero limiting readout creates singular Grams, but the cited pseudo-Lipschitz version does not require rank stability.

For completeness, the source has a genuine bounded operator realization. A \(1/4\)-net argument gives

\[
\Pr(\|\Gamma_n\|_{\mathrm{op}}>10)
\le 2e^{-8n}.
\tag{9}
\]

Enumerate a countable rational family of all finite two-sided probe programs. Their limiting inner products define two pre-Hilbert spaces. Quotient by zero norm and complete to obtain

\[
\mathcal H_R=L^2(\Omega_R),\qquad
\mathcal H_L=L^2(\Omega_L).
\]

The bound (9) implies

\[
\|\Gamma v\|_{\mathcal H_L}
\le10\|v\|_{\mathcal H_R}.
\]

Finite-dimensional duality,

\[
\langle \Gamma_nv,w\rangle_n
=
\langle v,\Gamma_n^\top w\rangle_n,
\]

passes to the limit, so the reverse action is the genuine Hilbert adjoint \(\Gamma^*\).

This pointed Gaussian action is deterministic as a law. It retains neither a finite matrix nor a training history.

A convergence-determining state metric is obtained by enumerating all finite current probes \(\mathbf P_r\) and setting

\[
d_{\mathrm{act}}(\mathsf S,\widetilde{\mathsf S})
=
\sum_{r\ge1}2^{-r}
\left(
1\wedge
W_2\bigl(
\operatorname{Law}\mathbf P_r(\mathsf S),
\operatorname{Law}\mathbf P_r(\widetilde{\mathsf S})
\bigr)
\right).
\tag{10}
\]

It detects, in particular, both \(Mv\) and \(M^*w\), all empirical Grams, and every vector appearing in (1)–(5).

## 4. Autonomous limiting gradient flow

On the pointed source \((\mathcal H_R,\mathcal H_L,\Gamma,\Gamma^*)\), initialize

\[
U_0\sim N(0,1),\qquad
X_0=\Theta(U_0),\qquad
C_0=0,\qquad
Q_0=0.
\]

For the current state \((X,C,Q)\), define

\[
U=J(X),\qquad H=\arctan U,
\]

\[
B=\Gamma+Q,\qquad S=BH,
\]

\[
G=\arctan S,\qquad
D=Cq(S),
\]

\[
F=\langle C,G\rangle_L,\qquad e=F-1.
\]

The limiting IDE is

\[
\boxed{
\begin{aligned}
\dot X&=-2eB^*D,\\
\dot C&=-2eG,\\
\dot Q&=-2e\,D\otimes H.
\end{aligned}}
\tag{11}
\]

Here

\[
(D\otimes H)v=D\langle H,v\rangle_R.
\]

This is current-state, autonomous, and restartable: at time \(\tau\), the state

\[
(\Gamma;X_\tau,C_\tau,Q_\tau)
\]

is sufficient to continue (11). No past trajectory is needed.

### Well-posedness

Put \(a=\pi/2\). From the gradient identity,

\[
\dot e=-2eK,
\tag{12}
\]

where

\[
K^{(1)}=\|q(U)B^*D\|_R^2,
\]

\[
K^{(2)}=\|H\|_R^2\|D\|_L^2,
\qquad
K^{(3)}=\|G\|_L^2.
\tag{13}
\]

Since \(e(0)=-1\),

\[
|e(t)|\le1.
\tag{14}
\]

Consequently,

\[
\|C(t)\|_\infty
\le2a t=\pi t,
\qquad
\|D(t)\|_2\le\pi t.
\tag{15}
\]

Furthermore,

\[
\|\dot Q(t)\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
\le2\|D(t)\|_2\|H(t)\|_2
\le \pi^2t,
\]

and hence

\[
\|Q(t)\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
\le\frac{\pi^2t^2}{2}.
\tag{16}
\]

Finally,

\[
\|\dot X(t)\|_2
\le2\bigl(\|\Gamma\|+\|Q(t)\|_{\mathfrak S_1}\bigr)\|D(t)\|_2.
\tag{17}
\]

These bounds rule out finite-time blowup.

For two states satisfying fixed versions of (15)–(17),

\[
\|H-\widetilde H\|_2\le\|X-\widetilde X\|_2,
\]

\[
\|S-\widetilde S\|_2
\le
\|B\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|X-\widetilde X\|_2
+a\|Q-\widetilde Q\|_{\mathfrak S_1},
\]

and

\[
\|D-\widetilde D\|_2
\le
\|C-\widetilde C\|_2
+
\|\widetilde C\|_\infty\|q'\|_\infty
\|S-\widetilde S\|_2.
\tag{18}
\]

The rank-one estimate

\[
\|D\otimes H-\widetilde D\otimes\widetilde H\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
\le
\|D-\widetilde D\|_2\|H\|_2
+
\|\widetilde D\|_2\|H-\widetilde H\|_2
\tag{19}
\]

then proves local Lipschitzness in

\[
\|X-\widetilde X\|_2+
\|C-\widetilde C\|_2+
\|Q-\widetilde Q\|_{\mathfrak S_1}.
\]

Picard iteration, with the pointwise envelope (15), gives local existence and uniqueness; (14)–(17) extend it to every finite time. Thus (11) defines a semigroup.

## 5. Width identification

Fix an auxiliary mesh \(\delta>0\). Euler iteration of (11) gives

\[
Q_k
=
-2\delta\sum_{r<k}e_rD_r\otimes H_r.
\]

Therefore, for every vector \(v\),

\[
B_kv
=
\Gamma v
-
2\delta\sum_{r<k}
e_rD_r\langle H_r,v\rangle,
\tag{20}
\]

and

\[
B_k^*w
=
\Gamma^*w
-
2\delta\sum_{r<k}
e_rH_r\langle D_r,w\rangle.
\tag{21}
\]

For fixed \(T/\delta\), equations (20)–(21) are a finite two-sided Gaussian program. The Master Theorem therefore identifies every probe, empirical Gram, output, kernel block, and velocity field at that mesh.

On the event \(\|\Gamma_n\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le10\), estimates (15)–(19) hold uniformly in \(n\). The ordinary Euler calculation gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\left[
\|X_n(t)-X_n^\delta(t)\|_n
+
\|C_n(t)-C_n^\delta(t)\|_n
+
\|Q_n(t)-Q_n^\delta(t)\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
\right]
\le C_T\delta.
\tag{22}
\]

The same estimate holds for the limiting IDE.

Taking \(n\to\infty\) at fixed \(\delta\), and then \(\delta\downarrow0\), proves

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
d_{\mathrm{act}}(\mathsf S_n^{\mathrm{flow}}(t),\mathsf S(t))
\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0.
\tag{23}
\]

This is full-sequence convergence: fixed-mesh limits are deterministic, and uniqueness of (11) removes subsequence choices.

The actual normalized readout initialization satisfies

\[
C_{0,j}=nW_j^{(3)}(0)\sim N(0,n^{-2}),
\]

so

\[
\|C_0\|_n\to0,
\qquad
\|C_0\|_\infty\to0
\quad\text{in probability}.
\tag{24}
\]

The stability estimate (18) shows that replacing it by the limiting value \(C_0=0\) changes the compact-time trajectory by \(o_{\mathbb P}(1)\).

## 6. The exact-GD diagonal

The transformed auxiliary Euler scheme is not exactly raw GD, so this bridge must be checked separately.

Let

\[
R=-2eM^\top d.
\]

From (1),

\[
u^+=u+\eta q(u)R.
\]

Because \(\Theta\) is cubic,

\[
\Theta(u^+)
=
\Theta(u)
+
\eta R
+
\eta^2u q(u)^2R^2
+
\frac{\eta^3}{3}q(u)^3R^3
\tag{25}
\]

coordinatewise.

Now

\[
\sup_x |x|q(x)^2=\frac9{16\sqrt3},
\qquad
0<q\le1,
\qquad
\|R\|_\infty\le\sqrt n\,\|R\|_n.
\]

Hence the one-step defect satisfies

\[
\|\rho_k\|_n
\le
\frac9{16\sqrt3}\eta^2\sqrt n\,\|R_k\|_n^2
+
\frac13\eta^3n\,\|R_k\|_n^3.
\tag{26}
\]

The required uniform bound on \(R_k\) follows directly. If

\[
c_k=\|C_k\|_n,
\]

then, because \(\|G_k\|_n\le a\),

\[
c_{k+1}
\le
(1+2a^2\eta)c_k+2a\eta.
\tag{27}
\]

Discrete Grönwall bounds \(c_k\) for \(k\eta\le T\). Directly summing the updates then bounds

\[
\|C_k\|_\infty,\qquad
\|M_k\|_{\mathrm{op}},
\]

and

\[
\|R_k\|_n
\le
2|e_k|\|M_k\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|C_k\|_n
\le R_T
\tag{28}
\]

uniformly in \(n\).

The perturbed-Euler recursion obtained from (25), together with the local truncation error of the continuous ODE, therefore gives

\[
\sup_{k\eta\le T}
d_n(\mathsf S_{n,k}^{\mathrm{GD}},\mathsf S_n^{\mathrm{flow}}(k\eta))
\le
C_T\left(
\eta+\eta\sqrt n+\eta^2n
\right),
\tag{29}
\]

where

\[
d_n=
\|\Delta X\|_n+
\|\Delta C\|_n+
\|\Delta Q\|_{\mathfrak S_1}.
\]

For \(\eta=n^{-2}\), the right side is

\[
O_T(n^{-2}+n^{-3/2}+n^{-3})\to0.
\]

Thus no growing-depth Tensor Program theorem is being assumed: the \(O(n^2)\) exact GD steps are compared directly with the finite-width ODE, and only the ODE is identified through a fixed auxiliary mesh.

Combining (23) and (29) proves full joint convergence of exact GD.

## 7. Path laws, uniform integrability, kernels, and velocities

At a fixed auxiliary mesh, every finite path tuple is a fixed Gaussian program. Applying the Master Theorem to fourth powers gives \(W_2\) convergence and square uniform integrability.

For mesh points \(t_k\), if

\[
\sup_k\|z_n(t_k)-z_n^\delta(t_k)\|_n\le\varepsilon_\delta,
\]

then Cauchy–Schwarz on each interval gives

\[
\frac1n\sum_i
\|z_{n,i}-z_{n,i}^\delta\|_\infty^2
\le
C\frac{T}{\delta}\varepsilon_\delta^2
+
C\delta
\int_0^T
\left(V_n^2+V_n^{\delta\,2}\right)\,dt.
\tag{30}
\]

For the auxiliary Euler approximation, \(\varepsilon_\delta=O(\delta)\), so the right side is \(O(\delta)\).

For exact GD, (29) gives
\(\varepsilon_n=O(\eta\sqrt n)\). Hence

\[
\frac{T}{\eta}\varepsilon_n^2
=
O(T\eta n)
=
O(T/n)\to0.
\]

Consequently, for \(\ell=1,2\),

\[
\overline\mu_n^{(\ell)}
\longrightarrow
\overline\mu^{(\ell)}
\quad\text{in }W_2(C([0,T])).
\tag{31}
\]

The square-tail transfer is explicit. For any coupled fields \(Y,Z\),

\[
|Y|^2\mathbf 1_{\{|Y|>R\}}
\le
2|Z|^2\mathbf 1_{\{|Z|>R/2\}}
+
4|Y-Z|^2.
\tag{32}
\]

Apply (32) first with the fixed-mesh field \(Z\), whose fourth moments converge, and then let \(\delta\downarrow0\). This establishes the required path-square uniform integrability.

The only weighted term needing extra care is

\[
q(U)B^*D.
\]

The fields \(B^*D\) have square-tail tightness by the same fixed-mesh argument. If \(X_n\to X\) and \(P_n=B_n^*D_n\to P\) in \(L^2\), then for every \(M\),

\[
\|(q(J(X_n))-q(J(X)))P\|_2^2
\le
C M^2\|X_n-X\|_2^2
+
4\mathbb E[P^2\mathbf1_{\{|P|>M\}}].
\tag{33}
\]

First let \(n\to\infty\), then \(M\to\infty\). This proves convergence of the first-layer kernel and velocity. Applying \(B\), whose operator norm is uniformly bounded, also passes the second-layer velocity term in (5).

Thus

\[
K_n\to K,\qquad
f_n\to F,\qquad
L_n\to e^2
\]

uniformly on compact intervals, while

\[
V_n^{(\ell)2}\to V^{(\ell)2}
\quad\text{in }L^1(0,T).
\tag{34}
\]

Every exact layer update is determined by the limiting pointed-action state.

## 8. Strict nondegeneracy

Let

\[
U_0\sim N(0,1),\qquad
H_0=\arctan U_0,
\qquad
m=\mathbb E H_0^2>0.
\]

The initial second preactivation is

\[
Z=\Gamma H_0\sim N(0,m).
\]

Define

\[
G=\arctan Z,
\qquad
Y=Gq(Z),
\qquad
\nu=\mathbb E Y^2>0,
\]

\[
\Xi=\Gamma^*Y,
\qquad
d_0=\|q(U_0)\Xi\|_2^2.
\]

The adjoint identity gives

\[
\langle H_0,\Xi\rangle
=
\langle Z,Y\rangle
=
\mathbb E\left[
\frac{Z\arctan Z}{1+Z^2}
\right]>0.
\]

Hence \(\Xi\ne0\). Since \(q(U_0)>0\) almost surely,

\[
d_0>0.
\tag{35}
\]

Let

\[
\mathcal R
=
mI+
\Gamma M_{q(U_0)^2}\Gamma^*.
\]

It is positive and

\[
\langle Y,\mathcal RY\rangle
=
m\nu+d_0.
\tag{36}
\]

Directly integrating (11) near zero gives, in the indicated norms,

\[
C(t)=2tG+o_{L^2}(t),
\qquad
D(t)=2tY+o_{L^2}(t),
\]

\[
X(t)=X_0+2t^2\Xi+o_{L^2}(t^2),
\]

\[
Q(t)=2t^2Y\otimes H_0+o_{\mathfrak S_1}(t^2),
\]

\[
U(t)=U_0+2t^2q(U_0)\Xi+o_{L^2}(t^2),
\]

\[
S(t)=Z+2t^2\mathcal RY+o_{L^2}(t^2).
\tag{37}
\]

These follow from \(D(t)/t\to2Y\), bounded continuity of the coordinate maps, and the adjoint identity; no finite-width Taylor/width interchange is used.

Consequently,

\[
K^{(1)}(t)
=
4d_0t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(2)}(t)
=
4m\nu t^2+o(t^2),
\]

and, putting

\[
k_3=\mathbb E(\arctan Z)^2>0,
\]

\[
K^{(3)}(t)
=
k_3+4(m\nu+d_0)t^2+o(t^2).
\]

Therefore

\[
K(t)
=
k_3+8(m\nu+d_0)t^2+o(t^2).
\tag{38}
\]

In particular, the kernel is nonconstant.

The feature velocities satisfy

\[
V^{(1)}(t)^2
=
16d_0t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
V^{(2)}(t)^2
=
16\|\mathcal RY\|_2^2t^2+o(t^2),
\tag{39}
\]

and \(\mathcal R\ge mI\), so the second coefficient is positive. Moreover,

\[
\mathbb E|U(t)-U_0|^2
=
4d_0t^4+o(t^4)>0,
\]

\[
\mathbb E|S(t)-Z|^2
=
4\|\mathcal RY\|_2^2t^4+o(t^4)>0.
\tag{40}
\]

Thus both hidden layers have genuine macroscopic feature motion.

At time zero,

\[
\operatorname{Var}(U_0)=1,
\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(Z)=m>0.
\]

Their \(W_2\)-continuous laws retain finite strictly positive variance on a sufficiently short interval.

For any nondegenerate Gaussian \(Y_0\),

\[
\inf_{a,b}
\mathbb E|\arctan Y_0-aY_0-b|^2>0:
\]

otherwise continuity and full support would force \(\arctan x=ax+b\) for every \(x\), which is false. The regression residual is continuous under \(W_2\) while the variance is bounded away from zero, since it is determined by

\[
\mathbb EY,\quad
\mathbb EY^2,\quad
\mathbb E\phi(Y),\quad
\mathbb EY\phi(Y),\quad
\mathbb E\phi(Y)^2.
\]

Hence both nonlinear residuals remain positive on a short interval.

Finally,

\[
F(0)=0\ne1,
\qquad
\dot F(0)=2k_3>0,
\]

and

\[
\dot L(0)=-4k_3<0.
\tag{41}
\]

Choose \(T_*>0\) small enough that the two variances and nonlinear residuals remain positive and the positive coefficients in (38)–(40) dominate their remainders. Then every \(0<T\le T_*\) satisfies all seven requested conditions.

A useful adversarial check is that ordinary edge laws cannot replace the pointed action state. If \(w\perp h\), \(\|w\|_n=1\), and

\[
R=\gamma\,d\otimes_n w,
\]

then \(Rh=0\), so the current forward preactivations and node laws are unchanged, while

\[
R^\top d
=
\gamma w\|d\|_n^2
\]

changes the first-layer velocity by order one. The pointed-action topology detects this through the probe \(M^\top d\); an ordinary marginal or edge empirical law does not. This is why the enriched—but autonomous and current-state—mean-field state is essential.

DO NOT LOOK AT ANY OTHER PROJECT FILES FOR THIS TASK. THIS IS A. FULLY SELF CONTIANED list&#x20;

\# Task: nonlinear joint mean-field/gradient-flow scaling

For each width \\(n\\), consider the bias-free network trained on \\((x,y)=(1,1)\\):

\\[
z\_i^{(1)}=W\_i^{(1)},
\\]

\\[
z\_j^{(2)}
\=
\sum\_{i=1}^{n}
W\_{ji}^{(2)}
\phi\\!\left(z\_i^{(1)}\right),
\\]

\\[
f\_n
\=
\sum\_{j=1}^{n}
W\_j^{(3)}
\phi\\!\left(z\_j^{(2)}\right),
\qquad
L\_n=(f\_n-1)^2.
\\]

The benchmark activation is \\(\phi(z)=z^2\\). An affirmative result may instead use any fixed, width-independent, non-affine \\(\phi\in C^2(\mathbb R)\\) whose first two derivatives have polynomial growth.

Initialize all entries independently:

\\[
W\_i^{(1)}(0)\sim N(0,\sigma\_{1,n}^2),
\\]

\\[
W\_{ji}^{(2)}(0)\sim N(0,\sigma\_{2,n}^2),
\\]

\\[
W\_j^{(3)}(0)\sim N(0,\sigma\_{3,n}^2),
\\]

where the positive variances are arbitrary deterministic functions of \\(n\\).

Use exact full-batch GD:

\\[
W\_{k+1}^{(\ell)}
\=
W\_k^{(\ell)}
\-
\eta\_{0,n}\lambda\_n^{(\ell)}
\nabla\_{W^{(\ell)}}L\_n,
\qquad
\ell=1,2,3,
\\]

with

\\[
\eta\_{0,n}\to0,
\qquad
\lambda\_n^{(\ell)}>0,
\qquad
\eta\_{0,n}\lambda\_n^{(\ell)}\to0.
\\]

No normalization or boundedness is imposed on \\(\lambda\_n^{(\ell)}\\); each may vanish, remain bounded, or diverge. Interpolate at time \\(t=k\eta\_{0,n}\\). The required finite nonzero dynamics below determine the macroscopic clock.

Since \\(x=1\\), proofs may optimize \\(z^{(1)}\\) directly using the exact identity \\(z^{(1)}=W^{(1)}\\); this does not remove the first trainable layer.

Determine whether there exist an admissible activation and hyperparameter sequence such that, along the full sequence \\(n\to\infty\\), the exact interpolated GD dynamics converge on every compact interval \\([0,T]\\) inside some nonzero existence interval to a well-posed, autonomous, genuinely nonlinear mean-field gradient flow satisfying all conditions below.

\## Required properties

1\. \*\*Full joint convergence.\*\* &#x20;
&#x20;  The empirical network state converges in probability, uniformly on \\([0,T]\\), in a topology strong enough to pass every term in the exact gradient vector field and determine both hidden-layer laws, every layer update, the kernel, output, and loss. The proof must establish tightness, sufficient uniform integrability, identification, well-posedness, uniqueness, and vanishing discrete-GD error jointly as \\(n\to\infty\\) and \\(\eta\_{0,n}\to0\\).

2\. \*\*Closed mean-field dynamics.\*\* &#x20;
&#x20;  The limit is an \\(n\\)-independent, autonomous, restartable law-level evolution. It may contain conditional or edge laws genuinely required by the middle layer, but may not retain the finite parameter array, its history, or an externally prescribed trajectory.

3\. \*\*Finite nondegenerate hidden states.\*\* &#x20;
&#x20;  For \\(\ell=1,2\\), the empirical preactivation path laws

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  \overline\mu\_n^{(\ell)}
&#x20;  \=
&#x20;  \frac1n\sum\_i
&#x20;  \delta\_{z\_i^{(\ell)}(\cdot)}
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  converge in a Wasserstein path topology strong enough to control all required moments. Their limiting marginals have finite, strictly positive variance.

4\. \*\*Feature learning in both hidden layers.\*\* &#x20;
&#x20;  Define

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  V\_n^{(\ell)}(t)^2
&#x20;  \=
&#x20;  \frac1n\sum\_i
&#x20;  \left|\dot z\_i^{(\ell)}(t)\right|^2.
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  For \\(\ell=1,2\\), these converge in a compact-time mode sufficient to pass time integrals to finite limits satisfying

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  0<
&#x20;  \int\_0^T V^{(\ell)}(t)^2\\,dt
&#x20;  <\infty.
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  The limiting paths also have nonzero macroscopic displacement:

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  \sup\_{0\le t\le T}
&#x20;  \int
&#x20;  |z(t)-z(0)|^2\\,
&#x20;  d\overline\mu^{(\ell)}(z)
&#x20;  \>0.
&#x20;  \\]

5\. \*\*Every parameter block remains active.\*\* &#x20;
&#x20;  Define

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  K\_n^{(\ell)}(t)
&#x20;  \=
&#x20;  \lambda\_n^{(\ell)}
&#x20;  \left\\|
&#x20;  \nabla\_{W^{(\ell)}}f\_n(t)
&#x20;  \right\\|^2,
&#x20;  \qquad
&#x20;  K\_n=\sum\_{\ell=1}^{3}K\_n^{(\ell)}.
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  Each \\(K\_n^{(\ell)}\\) converges to a finite limit satisfying

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  0<
&#x20;  \int\_0^T K^{(\ell)}(t)\\,dt
&#x20;  <\infty.
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  Thus all three parameter blocks contribute at leading order.

6\. \*\*Nonlinearity survives in both hidden layers.\*\* &#x20;
&#x20;  For the limiting marginal \\(\mu\_t^{(\ell)}\\), define

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  \mathcal N^{(\ell)}(t)
&#x20;  \=
&#x20;  \inf\_{a,b\in\mathbb R}
&#x20;  \int
&#x20;  |\phi(z)-az-b|^2\\,
&#x20;  d\mu\_t^{(\ell)}(z).
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  Require

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  \int\_0^T
&#x20;  \mathcal N^{(\ell)}(t)\\,dt
&#x20;  \>0,
&#x20;  \qquad
&#x20;  \ell=1,2.
&#x20;  \\]

7\. \*\*Kernel and loss move at leading order.\*\* &#x20;
&#x20;  Require uniform compact-time convergence

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  K\_n\to K,
&#x20;  \qquad
&#x20;  f\_n\to f,
&#x20;  \qquad
&#x20;  L\_n\to L,
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  to finite limits with

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  f(0)\ne1,
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  \sup\_{0\le t\le T}|K(t)-K(0)|>0,
&#x20;  \\]

&#x20;  and

&#x20;  \\[
&#x20;  L(T)\<L(0).
&#x20;  \\]

\## Proof-search strategy: crux–workbench loop

Do not attack the entire theorem only at a high level. Repeatedly apply the following discovery loop without weakening the final proof obligations:

1\. Select the smallest nondegenerate subproblem retaining one essential difficulty, such as nonlinear feedback, dependence generation, competing scales, nonclosure, instability, or moment growth.

2\. State what was simplified, which essential mechanism remains, what solving the crux would establish, and what bridge to the full problem would still require proof. Reject reductions that become linear, lazy, frozen, or decoupled.

3\. Choose an exactly tractable workbench and calculate through the first stage at which the hard feedback mechanism appears, preferably one stage further.

4\. Preserve intermediate structure: exact updates, generated dependencies, scaling balances, cancellations, symmetries, recurrences, invariants, moment hierarchies, degree growth, and closure residuals.

5\. Convert observed patterns into explicit falsifiable lemmas, then test the next case, perturb the simplifying assumptions, inspect boundary scalings, and search for counterexamples.

6\. Seek a uniform lifting argument—such as induction, generating functions, diagrammatics, martingales, energy estimates, stability, or compactness—and prove the bridge from the crux to the original system.

7\. Treat finite calculations only as discovery tools. A finite-step pattern, simulation, or correct fixed-order expansion is not compact-time convergence.

8\. Preserve valid identities, necessary scaling laws, and counterexamples from failed routes. Failure of one crux or representation defeats only that route.

The quadratic activation is a preferred initial workbench because finite-width GD iterates are polynomial functions of Gaussian initialization, making exact conditional moments and finite-step calculations available. Its purpose is to expose a provable recurrence, invariant, scaling law, hierarchy, or obstruction.

\## Forbidden shortcuts

The following are inadmissible:

\- freezing any layer or allowing any layer contribution to vanish;
\- feature learning in only one hidden layer;
\- vanishing or divergent hidden feature scales;
\- divergent-background motion with vanishing relative feature change;
\- frozen-feature, NTK, tangent-mark, or initialization-linearized limits;
\- affine or deep-linear limiting dynamics;
\- width-dependent activations;
\- biases, centering, normalization, residual connections, trainable scales, or changes to the forward map;
\- momentum, adaptive optimization, clipping, weight decay, noise, gradient modification, or changes to GD;
\- convergence of only the output, loss, kernel, finitely many moments, or another nonclosing surrogate;
\- an imposed \\(n\\)-dependent clock normalization unrelated to the required finite nonzero limiting dynamics;
\- separate width and gradient-flow limits without uniform estimates proving the joint diagonal limit;
\- formal or nonrigorous DMFT arguments in place of a theorem.

Fixed deterministic preactivation, postactivation, or output multipliers are exact reparameterizations and must be absorbed into the permitted variances and learning-rate scalers.

\## Resolution standard

An affirmative resolution must exhibit one fixed admissible nonlinear activation, one explicit hyperparameter sequence, a closed limiting mean-field gradient flow, and a proof of every required property.

A negative resolution must rule out every admissible hyperparameter sequence for every activation in the stated class. Failure of one activation, scaling ansatz, closure, crux, expansion, or proof method is not nonexistence.

Any external theorem must be stated precisely and all hypotheses verified. If neither complete theorem is established, the task remains unresolved.

PLease be very very very careful if you cite a theorem and invoke it to do the heavy lifting, DO NOT RELY ON MEMORY. you have to retrieve the full text and check the exact and fully digested statement of the theorem, its obligations, and exactly what it asserts and if it does indeed imply what you need it to imply. DMFT results are usually very non-rigorous, of course some might be, but you have to scrutinize them super well. Tensor Program is much better but they often supply only finite number ofsteps guarantee, not continuous time style (where we take inifinitely many small steps) 


use [$solve-math-rigorously](/etc/codex/skills/solve-math-rigorously/SKILL.md)  with diversified isolated agentis strategies at all stages for maximum diversity at all stages
