<skill>
<name>solve-math-rigorously</name>
<path>/etc/codex/skills/solve-math-rigorously/SKILL.md</path>
---
name: solve-math-rigorously
description: Produce compact but complete solutions to standalone mathematical questions and proof requests. Use when the user asks to solve, prove, derive, analyze, verify, or correct a mathematical problem that is not primarily an incremental lesson about supplied technical documents. Infer the strongest reasonable interpretation without inventing assumptions, establish a minimal setup, use minimal notation, expose every nontrivial step, state and verify external theorem hypotheses, give proof architecture when useful, and silently audit the draft before answering.
---

# Solve Mathematics Rigorously

Produce a complete mathematical answer rather than a staged document lesson, unless the user explicitly requests incremental pacing. Optimize simultaneously for correctness, rigor, clarity, and economy.

## Determine the intended problem

Interpret the strongest reasonable version supported by the wording and mathematical context. Do not exploit an omission to answer a trivial or uninteresting straw-man version.

Handle ambiguity according to its effect:

- If it does not change the substance, adopt the conventional interpretation and state it briefly.
- If one reasonable assumption makes the intended problem precise, state that assumption before solving.
- If several reasonable interpretations lead to materially different answers, ask for clarification or give a compact case split when that fully resolves the ambiguity.
- If a missing assumption is necessary for the claimed conclusion, do not invent it silently.

If the stated claim is false, say so and give a decisive counterexample or contradiction. Present a corrected nearby statement only when it helps, and label it as a correction rather than the original result.

Answer the problem actually asked. Do not weaken a universal claim to a convenient special case, replace an exact question with a numerical approximation, or prove a converse instead of the stated direction.

## Establish a minimal setup

Begin with a compact setup or preliminaries passage when it reduces ambiguity or supports later reasoning. State only the necessary:

- domain and assumptions;
- definitions used in the solution;
- boundary, regularity, integrability, independence, or nondegeneracy conditions that matter;
- notation that must persist through the answer.

Reuse the problem's notation whenever possible. Introduce a new symbol only when the unabbreviated expression is genuinely cumbersome and recurs often. Define every new symbol before use and state its mathematical type when that is not immediately evident.

Do not force a visible setup heading for a short elementary problem. Do not hide substantive assumptions in prose later in the solution.

## Give the architecture before a substantial proof

Before a long or conceptually difficult proof, explain the plan in a short paragraph or compact list. Identify the main steps, cases, claims, or lemmas and how they combine to yield the conclusion.

Keep the architecture at the level needed to orient the reader. Do not duplicate the detailed proof, and do not substitute a plan for the proof itself. For a routine calculation or one-step argument, omit the architecture.

## Execute every nontrivial step

Derive each nontrivial algebraic, analytic, probabilistic, geometric, combinatorial, or logical transition. Give enough detail for the user to verify the step without reconstructing missing reasoning.

In particular:

- identify the definition, identity, inequality, or prior claim being used;
- show substitutions, rearrangements, case transitions, and changes of variables that carry real mathematical content;
- track domains, signs, quantifiers, equality conditions, exceptional cases, and possible zero denominators;
- justify limit exchanges, differentiation or integration under a sign, convergence claims, and optimization steps;
- distinguish exact arguments from heuristics, approximations, and numerical evidence;
- show that intermediate claims actually imply the requested conclusion.

Avoid words such as “clearly,” “obviously,” “standard,” and “it follows” when they replace reasoning. They are acceptable only after the supporting reason has been supplied.

Do not over-explain elementary arithmetic or definitions already established. Compress routine steps while preserving the chain of verification.

## Invoke external results completely

Whenever the solution relies on a nontrivial theorem or external fact:

1. state the exact form needed, including its assumptions and conclusion;
2. verify each assumption in the present problem;
3. identify the current objects substituted into the theorem;
4. state exactly what conclusion the theorem yields here.

Do not merely name a theorem or call a fact standard. Prefer the relevant specialization over an unnecessarily general statement.

Verify a recent, specialized, source-specific, or uncertain result against a primary or authoritative source before relying on it. Cite the source near the claim. Paraphrase the relevant result unless its exact wording matters; quote only a short compliant excerpt when useful.

If the required theorem is unavailable, uncertain, or does not apply, expose the gap instead of proceeding as though it were valid.

## Use examples and checks proportionately

Include an example, boundary case, counterexample, or remark only when it clarifies an abstraction, tests a hypothesis, explains a consequence, or exposes why an assumption is needed.

Use low-cost sanity checks when they add confidence, such as:

- substituting the result back into the defining relation;
- checking dimensions, signs, or units;
- testing a boundary or small case;
- comparing with a known special case;
- deriving the same conclusion by a second short route.

Do not add examples mechanically or let them replace the general argument.

## Keep the presentation compact

Use headings only when they make a substantial solution easier to navigate. Keep notation sparse and stable. Prefer explicit expressions to disposable shorthand.

Use equations to carry mathematical content, not decoration. Avoid formula dumps, decorative summaries, and boxed equations. State the conclusion plainly.

Let the problem's complexity determine the answer length. Preserve every necessary assumption, theorem check, and logical transition, but remove repetition and exposition that does not improve understanding or verification.

## Audit and revise silently

Draft the solution, then review it before sending. Check:

1. Did the solution address the strongest reasonable interpretation rather than a straw man?
2. Were all adopted assumptions stated, and were none invented merely to force the result?
3. Was the setup sufficient but minimal?
4. Was every persistent symbol necessary and defined?
5. Did a substantial proof receive a useful architecture?
6. Can the reader verify every nontrivial transition?
7. Was every external theorem stated in the needed form, with its hypotheses checked and its conclusion applied explicitly?
8. Were recent, specialized, or uncertain results verified from an authoritative source?
9. Were edge cases, equality conditions, domains, and quantifiers handled?
10. Does the final conclusion answer the exact question?
11. Do examples or remarks earn their space?
12. Is the answer as compact as rigor allows, with no boxed equations?

Revise any failing part and repeat the audit until the answer satisfies the checklist as well as possible. Do not mention the drafting or audit process in the final answer.

</skill>

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 21853 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.273Z | assistant | final_answer ===
Yes. A fully joint nonlinear construction exists. The workable activation is not \(z^2\), but

\[
\phi(z)=\arctan z.
\]

Choose

\[
\sigma_{1,n}^2=1,\qquad
\sigma_{2,n}^2=\frac1n,\qquad
\sigma_{3,n}^2=\frac1{n^4},
\]

\[
\lambda_n^{(1)}=n,\qquad
\lambda_n^{(2)}=1,\qquad
\lambda_n^{(3)}=\frac1n,
\qquad
\eta_{0,n}=\frac1{n^2}.
\]

Then

\[
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(1)}=\frac1n,\quad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(2)}=\frac1{n^2},\quad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(3)}=\frac1{n^3},
\]

so all raw layerwise steps vanish.

The result below holds for the usual polygonal interpolation of the parameters. Piecewise-constant interpolation gives the same limit.

## Theorem

For the activation and scaling above, there is a deterministic, autonomous, restartable pointed-action mean-field flow. The exact interpolated GD trajectories converge to it in probability, along the full sequence \(n\to\infty\), uniformly on every finite time interval in a topology that determines all forward fields, backward fields, layer updates, kernel blocks, output, loss, and both hidden path laws.

The flow exists globally. Moreover, there is \(T_*>0\) such that, for every \(0<T\le T_*\), all seven properties in the question hold.

## 1. Exact finite-width equations

Write

\[
u=W^{(1)},\qquad M=W^{(2)},\qquad c=nW^{(3)}
\]

and use

\[
\langle v,w\rangle_n=\frac1n v^\top w,\qquad
\|v\|_n^2=\langle v,v\rangle_n.
\]

Set

\[
h=\phi(u),\qquad s=Mh,\qquad g=\phi(s),
\]

\[
q(x)=\phi'(x)=\frac1{1+x^2},\qquad
d=c\odot q(s),
\]

\[
f_n=\langle c,g\rangle_n,\qquad e=f_n-1.
\]

For vectors \(a,b\), define the normalized rank-one map

\[
a\otimes_n b
=
\frac1n ab^\top,
\qquad
(a\otimes_n b)v=a\langle b,v\rangle_n.
\]

Direct differentiation gives

\[
\nabla_u f_n
=
\frac1n q(u)\odot M^\top d,
\]

\[
\nabla_M f_n
=
\frac1n dh^\top,
\qquad
\nabla_{W^{(3)}}f_n=g.
\]

Hence exact GD is

\[
u^+=u-2\eta e\,q(u)\odot M^\top d,
\]

\[
M^+=M-2\eta e\,d\otimes_n h,
\]

\[
c^+=c-2\eta e\,g,
\tag{1}
\]

where \(\eta=\eta_{0,n}\).

The three kernel blocks are exactly

\[
K_n^{(1)}
=
\left\|q(u)\odot M^\top d\right\|_n^2,
\]

\[
K_n^{(2)}
=
\|h\|_n^2\|d\|_n^2,
\]

\[
K_n^{(3)}
=
\|g\|_n^2.
\tag{2}
\]

For the corresponding finite-width continuous gradient flow,

\[
\dot f_n=-2eK_n,\qquad
\dot L_n=-4e^2K_n,
\qquad
K_n=\sum_{\ell=1}^3K_n^{(\ell)}.
\tag{3}
\]

The hidden velocities are

\[
\dot u=-2e\,q(u)\odot M^\top d,
\]

and

\[
\dot s
=
-2e\left[
\|h\|_n^2d+
M\!\left(q(u)^2\odot M^\top d\right)
\right].
\tag{4}
\]

Thus

\[
V_n^{(1)2}=4e^2K_n^{(1)}.
\tag{5}
\]

## 2. Why ordinary neuron or edge laws do not close

The middle matrix must be retained through its action, not through its entry distribution alone.

For example, at a current state choose \(w\) with

\[
\langle w,h\rangle_n=0,\qquad \|w\|_n=1,
\]

and define

\[
R=\gamma\,d\otimes_n w.
\]

Then

\[
(M+R)h=Mh,
\]

so the current forward preactivations, output, \(K^{(2)}\), and \(K^{(3)}\) do not change. The ordinary empirical edge perturbation vanishes:

\[
\frac1{n^2}\sum_{ji}
\bigl(\sqrt n\,R_{ji}\bigr)^2
=
\frac{\gamma^2\|d\|_n^2}{n}\longrightarrow0.
\]

Nevertheless,

\[
(M+R)^\top d-M^\top d
=
\gamma\|d\|_n^2w,
\]

which changes the first-layer velocity and \(K^{(1)}\) at order one.

Consequently, no closure involving only hidden-node laws or the ordinary empirical edge law can satisfy property 2. The correct state is the current two-sided action law of \(M,M^\top\).

## 3. Natural coordinate and the limiting state

Define

\[
\Theta(a)=a+\frac{a^3}{3},\qquad x=\Theta(u),
\]

and let \(J=\Theta^{-1}\). Since

\[
\Theta'(a)=1+a^2=q(a)^{-1},
\]

the continuous equation for \(x\) is

\[
\dot x=-2eM^\top d.
\tag{6}
\]

Moreover, \(J\) is globally \(1\)-Lipschitz, and

\[
\Psi(x):=\arctan J(x)
\]

is bounded and globally Lipschitz, with

\[
\Psi'(x)=q(J(x))^2.
\tag{7}
\]

Write

\[
M_0=\Gamma_n,\qquad Q_n=M-\Gamma_n,
\]

where \((\Gamma_n)_{ji}\sim N(0,1/n)\). The learned part satisfies

\[
\dot Q_n=-2e\,d\otimes_n h.
\tag{8}
\]

Because

\[
\|a\otimes_n b\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
=
\|a\|_n\|b\|_n,
\]

\(Q_n\) is uniformly trace-class on compact time intervals.

### The fixed-program theorem actually used

The only external probabilistic theorem used is the following fixed-finite-program result.

Consider a fixed finite program generated from independent coordinate seeds and iid Gaussian matrices with entries \(N(0,1/n)\), using:

- finitely many \(Gv\) and \(G^\top w\) operations, with matrix reuse allowed;
- coordinatewise pseudo-Lipschitz maps;
- empirical moments and continuous scalar operations.

Then, for every fixed collection of generated vectors and every pseudo-Lipschitz test function, the empirical average converges almost surely to the deterministic Tensor-Program master law. Scalar program variables converge as well. This is precisely [Yang–Hu, Theorem G.4 under Assumption L.4](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf), referring to the two-sided master theorem in [Tensor Programs III](https://arxiv.org/abs/2009.10685).

The theorem is only a fixed-program theorem; it is not invoked with \(n\)-dependent training depth.

Its assumptions hold here:

- \(u_0\) is iid Gaussian and \(\Theta(u_0)\) is a polynomial Gaussian coordinate map.
- \(J,\Psi,q,\arctan\) are globally Lipschitz or polynomially bounded.
- Products and scalar multiplications are pseudo-Lipschitz of finite degree.
- Every auxiliary Euler mesh below has a fixed number \(T/\delta\) of steps when \(n\to\infty\).
- Reuse of both \(\Gamma_n\) and \(\Gamma_n^\top\) is part of the theorem.
- The pseudo-Lipschitz version of the theorem imposes no separate rank-stability assumption, so zero fields and singular limiting Gram matrices are permitted.

Applying the theorem to \(|z|^p\), for each fixed \(p\), also gives convergence of all required fixed-program moments.

### Pointed Gaussian action

Enumerate a countable rational program language generated from the right seed \(u_0\), constants, \(\Gamma_n,\Gamma_n^\top\), coordinate maps, and empirical inner products. The master theorem gives consistent deterministic finite-dimensional laws.

Quotient the right and left program fields by their limiting zero \(L^2\)-norms and complete them. This produces two probability \(L^2\)-spaces

\[
\mathcal H_R,\qquad \mathcal H_L.
\]

A \(1/4\)-net argument gives, for some numerical \(c>0\),

\[
\Pr(\|\Gamma_n\|_{\rm op}>10)\le 2e^{-cn}.
\]

Therefore, for every formal right probe \(v\),

\[
\|\Gamma v\|_{\mathcal H_L}
\le 10\|v\|_{\mathcal H_R}.
\]

Thus the matrix action descends to a genuine bounded operator

\[
\Gamma:\mathcal H_R\to\mathcal H_L.
\]

Finite-dimensional duality,

\[
\langle \Gamma_nv,w\rangle_n
=
\langle v,\Gamma_n^\top w\rangle_n,
\]

passes to the limit and proves that the reverse action is the Hilbert adjoint \(\Gamma^*\).

This pointed action is deterministic and width-independent. It retains neither a finite matrix nor training history.

## 4. Autonomous limiting IDE

On this pointed action space, initialize

\[
U_0\sim N(0,1),\qquad
X_0=\Theta(U_0),\qquad
C_0=0,\qquad
Q_0=0.
\]

At time \(t\), define

\[
U=J(X),\qquad H=\arctan U,
\]

\[
M=\Gamma+Q,\qquad S=MH,
\]

\[
G=\arctan S,\qquad
D=Cq(S),
\]

\[
F=\mathbb E_L[CG],\qquad e=F-1.
\]

The limit is

\[
\dot X=-2eM^*D,
\]

\[
\dot C=-2eG,
\]

\[
\dot Q=-2eD\otimes H.
\tag{9}
\]

Here \(D\otimes H:v\mapsto D\,\mathbb E_R[Hv]\).

Equation (9) is autonomous. At any restart time \(\tau\), the current state

\[
(\Gamma;X_\tau,C_\tau,Q_\tau)
\]

is sufficient to continue the same equation. The entire learned middle-layer history is compressed into the single current trace-class operator \(Q_\tau\).

### Well-posedness

Let \(a=\pi/2\). The vector field is locally Lipschitz in

\[
\|X-\widetilde X\|_2+
\|C-\widetilde C\|_2+
\|Q-\widetilde Q\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
\tag{10}
\]

on sets with bounded \(\|C\|_\infty\), \(\|Q\|_{\mathfrak S_1}\).

Indeed,

\[
\|H-\widetilde H\|_2
\le\|X-\widetilde X\|_2,
\]

\[
\|S-\widetilde S\|_2
\le
\|M\|_{\rm op}\|H-\widetilde H\|_2
+a\|Q-\widetilde Q\|_{\mathfrak S_1},
\]

and

\[
\|D-\widetilde D\|_2
\le
\|C-\widetilde C\|_2+
\|\widetilde C\|_\infty
\|q(S)-q(\widetilde S)\|_2.
\]

The rank-one estimate

\[
\|D\otimes H-\widetilde D\otimes\widetilde H\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
\le
\|D-\widetilde D\|_2\|H\|_2
+
\|\widetilde D\|_2\|H-\widetilde H\|_2
\]

closes the estimate. Picard iterates start from \(C_0=0\) and satisfy a common \(L^\infty\) envelope, so the contraction may be performed in the metric (10).

Differentiating \(F\) gives

\[
\dot F=-2eK,
\tag{11}
\]

where

\[
K^{(1)}=\|q(U)M^*D\|_2^2,
\]

\[
K^{(2)}=\|H\|_2^2\|D\|_2^2,
\]

\[
K^{(3)}=\|G\|_2^2.
\tag{12}
\]

Thus

\[
e(t)
=
-\exp\left(-2\int_0^tK(s)\,ds\right),
\]

so \(|e(t)|\le1\). Consequently,

\[
\|C(t)\|_\infty
\le
2a t=\pi t,
\]

\[
\|\dot Q(t)\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
\le
2\|D(t)\|_2\|H(t)\|_2
\le \pi^2t,
\]

and

\[
\|\dot X(t)\|_2
\le
2\bigl(\|\Gamma\|+\|Q(t)\|_{\mathfrak S_1}\bigr)\|D(t)\|_2.
\]

These exclude finite-time blowup. Hence (9) has a unique global solution.

## 5. Width limit without a growing-depth Tensor-Program invocation

Fix an auxiliary mesh \(\delta>0\). Explicit Euler for (9) gives

\[
Q_k
=
-2\delta\sum_{r<k}e_rD_r\otimes H_r.
\]

Therefore, for any vector \(v\),

\[
(\Gamma+Q_k)v
=
\Gamma v
-
2\delta\sum_{r<k}e_rD_r\langle H_r,v\rangle,
\tag{13}
\]

and similarly

\[
(\Gamma^*+Q_k^*)w
=
\Gamma^*w
-
2\delta\sum_{r<k}e_rH_r\langle D_r,w\rangle.
\tag{14}
\]

For fixed \(T,\delta\), equations (13)–(14) make the entire \(T/\delta\)-step Euler recursion a fixed finite two-sided Gaussian program. The external master theorem therefore applies exactly here.

On the event \(\|\Gamma_n\|_{\rm op}\le10\), the finite-width and limiting vector fields have the same compact-time Lipschitz bound. Directly from

\[
Y(t_{k+1})-Y(t_k)-\delta\mathcal F(Y(t_k))
=
\int_{t_k}^{t_{k+1}}
[\mathcal F(Y(s))-\mathcal F(Y(t_k))]\,ds,
\]

one obtains

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\left(
\|X_n(t)-X_n^\delta(t)\|_n+
\|C_n(t)-C_n^\delta(t)\|_n+
\|Q_n(t)-Q_n^\delta(t)\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
\right)
\le C_T\delta.
\tag{15}
\]

The same estimate holds in the limiting action space.

Thus the order of argument is:

1. fix \(\delta\);
2. apply the fixed finite-program theorem as \(n\to\infty\);
3. use (15) to send \(\delta\downarrow0\).

No Tensor-Program theorem is applied to a number of steps growing with \(n\).

The actual initialization has

\[
C_{0,j}=nW_j^{(3)}(0)\sim N(0,n^{-2}),
\]

so

\[
\mathbb E\|C_0\|_n^2=n^{-2},
\qquad
\|C_0\|_\infty\longrightarrow0
\]

in probability. Stability of (9) therefore permits replacing \(C_{0,n}\) by the limiting field \(C_0=0\).

## 6. Exact GD versus the finite-width flow

The transformed \(X\)-update is not exactly Euler, so this bridge must be checked separately.

Put

\[
R=-2eM^\top D.
\]

Then (1) says

\[
u^+=u+\eta q(u)R.
\]

Because \(\Theta\) is cubic,

\[
X^+
=
X+\eta R+
\eta^2u q(u)^2R^2+
\frac{\eta^3}{3}q(u)^3R^3.
\tag{16}
\]

This identity is exact.

Moreover,

\[
\sup_u |u|q(u)^2=\frac9{16\sqrt3},
\qquad
0<q(u)\le1,
\]

and

\[
\|R\|_\infty\le\sqrt n\,\|R\|_n.
\]

Hence the one-step coordinate defect \(\zeta\) satisfies

\[
\|\zeta\|_n
\le
\frac9{16\sqrt3}\eta^2\sqrt n\,\|R\|_n^2
+
\frac13\eta^3n\,\|R\|_n^3.
\tag{17}
\]

Uniform bounds needed in (17) follow directly from the exact discrete equations. Since \(\|G\|_n\le a\),

\[
\|C_{k+1}\|_n
\le
(1+2a^2\eta)\|C_k\|_n+2a\eta.
\tag{18}
\]

Discrete Grönwall bounds \(\|C_k\|_n\). Direct summation then bounds \(\|C_k\|_\infty\) and \(\|M_k\|_{\rm op}\), while

\[
\|R_k\|_n
\le
2|e_k|\|M_k\|_{\rm op}\|C_k\|_n.
\]

All are bounded by a width-independent \(C_T\) for \(k\eta\le T\), on the high-probability initialization event.

A perturbed-Euler Grönwall estimate applied to (16) gives

\[
\sup_{k\eta\le T}
d_n\!\left(
Y^{\rm GD}_{n,k},Y_n^{\rm flow}(k\eta)
\right)
\le
C_T\left(
\eta+\eta\sqrt n+\eta^2n
\right),
\tag{19}
\]

where

\[
d_n(Y,\widetilde Y)
=
\|X-\widetilde X\|_n+
\|C-\widetilde C\|_n+
\|Q-\widetilde Q\|_{\mathfrak S_1}.
\]

For \(\eta=n^{-2}\),

\[
\eta+\eta\sqrt n+\eta^2n
=
n^{-2}+n^{-3/2}+n^{-3}
\longrightarrow0.
\]

This proves the genuine joint diagonal limit; it is not a composition of two uncontrolled iterated limits.

## 7. Action topology, path laws, and uniform integrability

Enumerate all finite typed probes generated from the current state using:

- coordinate maps;
- scalar products;
- \(M,M^*\);
- finite-rank current updates;
- sums and products.

For a finite tuple \(P_r\) of same-side probes, let \(\mu_{P_r}\) be its empirical joint law. Define

\[
d_{\rm act}(\mathsf S,\widetilde{\mathsf S})
=
\sum_{r\ge1}2^{-r}
\left(
1\wedge W_2(\mu_{P_r},\widetilde\mu_{P_r})
\right).
\tag{20}
\]

This is convergence-determining for every field appearing in the vector field, including the otherwise invisible field \(M^*D\).

For fixed auxiliary mesh, the master theorem gives convergence of every finite collection of probes and every \(p\)-moment. Estimate (15), followed by a finite-probe truncation of the sum (20), gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
d_{\rm act}(\mathsf S_n^{\rm flow}(t),\mathsf S(t))
\longrightarrow0
\]

in probability. Equation (19) transfers this to exact GD.

For the hidden path laws, the elementary inequality

\[
\frac1n\sum_i
\sup_{s,t\in I}|z_i(t)-z_i(s)|^2
\le
|I|\int_I V_n(t)^2\,dt
\tag{21}
\]

provides temporal tightness. If two paths have mesh-point \(L^2\)-error at most \(\varepsilon\) on a mesh of size \(h\), then

\[
\frac1n\sum_i
\|z_i-\widetilde z_i\|_\infty^2
\le
2\left(\frac{T}{h}+1\right)\varepsilon^2
+
4h\int_0^T(V_n^2+\widetilde V_n^2)\,dt.
\tag{22}
\]

For flow versus fixed-mesh Euler, \(\varepsilon=O(h)\), so the right side is \(O(h)\). For exact GD versus flow,

\[
h=\eta=n^{-2},
\qquad
\varepsilon=O(n^{-3/2}),
\]

and the right side is \(O(n^{-1})\).

At every fixed auxiliary mesh, the master theorem gives moments of order \(p>2\), hence square uniform integrability. It transfers to the exact paths through the following pointwise estimate. For coupled nonnegative path norms \(X,Y\),

\[
X^2\mathbf 1_{\{X>R\}}
\le
2Y^2\mathbf 1_{\{Y>R/2\}}
+
4(X-Y)^2.
\tag{23}
\]

First fix the auxiliary mesh, let \(R\to\infty\), and then let the mesh vanish. Equations (22)–(23) prove

\[
\overline\mu_n^{(\ell)}
\longrightarrow
\overline\mu^{(\ell)}
\quad\text{in }W_2(C([0,T])),
\qquad \ell=1,2.
\tag{24}
\]

The same approximation gives square-tail tightness for \(M^*D\). Therefore weighted products also pass. Explicitly, if \(P_n\to P\) in \(L^2\), \(X_n\to X\) in \(L^2\), and \(P_n^2\) is uniformly integrable, then, for any \(R>0\),

\[
\|(q(J(X_n))-q(J(X)))P\|_2^2
\le
C R^2\|X_n-X\|_2^2
+
4\mathbb E[P^2\mathbf 1_{\{|P|>R\}}].
\]

Let \(n\to\infty\), then \(R\to\infty\). This proves convergence of the first-layer velocity, \(K^{(1)}\), and the reused-adjoint term in \(\dot S\).

Consequently,

\[
V_n^{(\ell)2}\to V^{(\ell)2}
\quad\text{in }L^1(0,T),
\]

and

\[
K_n\to K,\qquad f_n\to F,\qquad L_n\to e^2
\]

uniformly on compact time intervals.

## 8. Strict nonlinear feature learning

It remains to prove that the limiting flow is not lazy or linear.

Let

\[
U_0\sim N(0,1),\qquad H_0=\arctan U_0,
\]

\[
m=\mathbb E H_0^2>0.
\]

The initial second preactivation is

\[
Z=\Gamma H_0\sim N(0,m).
\]

Put

\[
G=\arctan Z,\qquad
Y=\frac{\arctan Z}{1+Z^2},
\qquad
\nu=\mathbb E Y^2>0,
\]

\[
\Xi=\Gamma^*Y,
\qquad
d_0=\mathbb E\!\left[q(U_0)^2\Xi^2\right].
\]

The genuine-adjoint identity proves \(d_0>0\), without an independence approximation:

\[
\langle H_0,\Xi\rangle
=
\langle \Gamma H_0,Y\rangle
=
\mathbb E\frac{Z\arctan Z}{1+Z^2}>0.
\]

Thus \(\Xi\ne0\); since \(q(U_0)>0\) almost surely, \(d_0>0\).

Define the positive operator on the left space

\[
\mathcal R
=
mI+
\Gamma M_{q(U_0)^2}\Gamma^*.
\]

Then

\[
\langle Y,\mathcal RY\rangle
=
m\nu+d_0>0.
\tag{25}
\]

Directly integrating (9) at \(t=0\) gives, in \(L^2\) and trace norm,

\[
C(t)=2tG+o(t),
\qquad
D(t)=2tY+o(t),
\]

\[
X(t)=X_0+2t^2\Xi+o(t^2),
\]

\[
Q(t)=2t^2Y\otimes H_0+o(t^2),
\]

\[
U(t)=U_0+2t^2q(U_0)\Xi+o(t^2),
\]

\[
S(t)=Z+2t^2\mathcal RY+o(t^2).
\tag{26}
\]

Therefore,

\[
K^{(1)}(t)=4d_0t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(2)}(t)=4m\nu t^2+o(t^2),
\]

and, writing \(k_3=\mathbb E G^2>0\),

\[
K^{(3)}(t)
=
k_3+4(d_0+m\nu)t^2+o(t^2).
\]

Hence

\[
K(t)
=
k_3+8(d_0+m\nu)t^2+o(t^2).
\tag{27}
\]

Both hidden velocities are nonzero:

\[
V^{(1)}(t)^2
=
16d_0t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
V^{(2)}(t)^2
=
16\|\mathcal RY\|_2^2t^2+o(t^2).
\tag{28}
\]

Their displacements satisfy

\[
\mathbb E|U(t)-U_0|^2
=
4d_0t^4+o(t^4)>0,
\]

\[
\mathbb E|S(t)-Z|^2
=
4\|\mathcal RY\|_2^2t^4+o(t^4)>0.
\tag{29}
\]

Thus every hidden layer learns features macroscopically, and all three parameter blocks have strictly positive time-integrated kernels.

Initially,

\[
\operatorname{Var}(U_0)=1,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(Z)=m>0.
\]

Wasserstein continuity preserves finite positive variance for sufficiently small time.

For either nondegenerate Gaussian law \(\mu\),

\[
\inf_{a,b}
\int|\arctan z-az-b|^2\,d\mu(z)>0.
\]

Otherwise \(\arctan z=az+b\) almost surely; continuity and full Gaussian support would force equality for every \(z\), which is impossible. The regression residual depends continuously on

\[
\mathbb Ez,\quad \mathbb Ez^2,\quad
\mathbb E\arctan z,\quad
\mathbb Ez\arctan z,\quad
\mathbb E(\arctan z)^2
\]

as long as the variance is positive. Hence both nonlinear residuals remain positive on a short interval.

Finally,

\[
F(0)=0\ne1,
\]

and from (11),

\[
F(t)=2k_3t+o(t),
\qquad
L(t)=1-4k_3t+o(t).
\]

Thus the loss strictly decreases.

Choose \(T_*>0\) small enough that:

- both hidden variances remain positive;
- both nonlinear regression residuals remain positive;
- the positive coefficients in (27)–(29) dominate their remainders.

Then every \(0<T\le T_*\) satisfies properties 3–7, while Sections 3–7 prove properties 1–2 and the full joint diagonal convergence.

Therefore the task has an affirmative resolution. The essential ingredients are the bounded nonlinear activation, the natural coordinate \(u+u^3/3\), the current two-sided Gaussian action state, and the explicit \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\) control of the exact-GD defect.

description: Produce compact but complete solutions to standalone mathematical questions and proof requests. Use when the user asks to solve, prove, derive, analyze, verify, or correct a mathematical problem that is not primarily an incremental lesson about supplied technical documents. # Solve Mathematics Rigorously

Produce a complete mathematical answer rather than a staged document lesson, unless the user explicitly requests incremental pacing. Optimize simultaneously for correctness, rigor, clarity, and economy.

## Determine the intended problem

Interpret the strongest reasonable version supported by the wording and mathematical context. Do not exploit an omission to answer a trivial or uninteresting straw-man version.

Handle ambiguity according to its effect:

- If it does not change the substance, adopt the conventional interpretation and state it briefly.
- If one reasonable assumption makes the intended problem precise, state that assumption before solving.
- If several reasonable interpretations lead to materially different answers, ask for clarification or give a compact case split when that fully resolves the ambiguity.
- If a missing assumption is necessary for the claimed conclusion, do not invent it silently.

If the stated claim is false, say so and give a decisive counterexample or contradiction. Present a corrected nearby statement only when it helps, and label it as a correction rather than the original result.

Answer the problem actually asked. Do not weaken a universal claim to a convenient special case, replace an exact question with a numerical approximation, or prove a converse instead of the stated direction.

## Establish a minimal setup

Begin with a compact setup or preliminaries passage when it reduces ambiguity or supports later reasoning. State only the necessary:

- domain and assumptions;
- definitions used in the solution;
- boundary, regularity, integrability, independence, or nondegeneracy conditions that matter;
- notation that must persist through the answer.

Reuse the problem's notation whenever possible. Introduce a new symbol only when the unabbreviated expression is genuinely cumbersome and recurs often. Define every new symbol before use and state its mathematical type when that is not immediately evident.

Do not force a visible setup heading for a short elementary problem. Do not hide substantive assumptions in prose later in the solution.

## Give the architecture before a substantial proof

Before a long or conceptually difficult proof, explain the plan in a short paragraph or compact list. Identify the main steps, cases, claims, or lemmas and how they combine to yield the conclusion.

Keep the architecture at the level needed to orient the reader. Do not duplicate the detailed proof, and do not substitute a plan for the proof itself. For a routine calculation or one-step argument, omit the architecture.

## Execute every nontrivial step

Derive each nontrivial algebraic, analytic, probabilistic, geometric, combinatorial, or logical transition. Give enough detail for the user to verify the step without reconstructing missing reasoning.

In particular:

- identify the definition, identity, inequality, or prior claim being used;
- show substitutions, rearrangements, case transitions, and changes of variables that carry real mathematical content;
- track domains, signs, quantifiers, equality conditions, exceptional cases, and possible zero denominators;
- justify limit exchanges, differentiation or integration under a sign, convergence claims, and optimization steps;
- distinguish exact arguments from heuristics, approximations, and numerical evidence;
- show that intermediate claims actually imply the requested conclusion.

Avoid words such as “clearly,” “obviously,” “standard,” and “it follows” when they replace reasoning. They are acceptable only after the supporting reason has been supplied.

Do not over-explain elementary arithmetic or definitions already established. Compress routine steps while preserving the chain of verification.

## Invoke external results completely

Whenever the solution relies on a nontrivial theorem or external fact:

1. state the exact form needed, including its assumptions and conclusion;
2. verify each assumption in the present problem;
3. identify the current objects substituted into the theorem;
4. state exactly what conclusion the theorem yields here.

Do not merely name a theorem or call a fact standard. Prefer the relevant specialization over an unnecessarily general statement.

Verify a recent, specialized, source-specific, or uncertain result against a primary or authoritative source before relying on it. Cite the source near the claim. Paraphrase the relevant result unless its exact wording matters; quote only a short compliant excerpt when useful.

If the required theorem is unavailable, uncertain, or does not apply, expose the gap instead of proceeding as though it were valid.

## Use examples and checks proportionately

Include an example, boundary case, counterexample, or remark only when it clarifies an abstraction, tests a hypothesis, explains a consequence, or exposes why an assumption is needed.

Use low-cost sanity checks when they add confidence, such as:

- substituting the result back into the defining relation;
- checking dimensions, signs, or units;
- testing a boundary or small case;
- comparing with a known special case;
- deriving the same conclusion by a second short route.

Do not add examples mechanically or let them replace the general argument.

## Keep the presentation compact

Use headings only when they make a substantial solution easier to navigate. Keep notation sparse and stable. Prefer explicit expressions to disposable shorthand.

Use equations to carry mathematical content, not decoration. Avoid formula dumps, decorative summaries, and boxed equations. State the conclusion plainly.

Let the problem's complexity determine the answer length. Preserve every necessary assumption, theorem check, and logical transition, but remove repetition and exposition that does not improve understanding or verification.

## Audit and revise silently

Draft the solution, then review it before sending. Check:

1. Did the solution address the strongest reasonable interpretation rather than a straw man?
2. Were all adopted assumptions stated, and were none invented merely to force the result?
3. Was the setup sufficient but minimal?
4. Was every persistent symbol necessary and defined?
5. Did a substantial proof receive a useful architecture?
6. Can the reader verify every nontrivial transition?
7. Was every external theorem stated in the needed form, with its hypotheses checked and its conclusion applied explicitly?
8. Were recent, specialized, or uncertain results verified from an authoritative source?
9. Were edge cases, equality conditions, domains, and quantifiers handled?
10. Does the final conclusion answer the exact question?
11. Do examples or remarks earn their space?
12. Is the answer as compact as rigor allows, with no boxed equations?

Revise any failing part and repeat the audit until the answer satisfies the checklist as well as possible. Do not mention the drafting or audit process in the final answer.

</skill>

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 22095 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.281Z | assistant | final_answer ===
No. The proposed result is plausible and repairable, but the proof as written is not valid at the requested resolution standard. Its finite-width algebra, joint-GD scaling, and strict small-time feature-learning calculation are correct. The failures are in the construction of the limiting probability/operator state and in the uniform passage of reused-adjoint fields, kernels, and velocities.

After supplying the repairs below, I find no fatal mathematical obstruction to the affirmative theorem. But the original proof should not be presented as complete.

## Audit summary

| Component | Verdict |
|---|---|
| Exact finite-width equations and kernel scaling | Correct |
| Natural-coordinate identity and accumulated GD defect | Correct |
| Fixed-mesh Tensor-Program use | Correct after tightening its statement |
| “Hilbert completion produces probability \(L^2\)-spaces” | False |
| Pointed-action probability/operator construction | Missing but repairable |
| Direct Picard theorem in the stated \(L^2\) space | Invalid as stated, repairable |
| Arbitrary-product action topology | Too strong, must be restricted |
| Path-\(W_2\) convergence argument | Essentially correct |
| Velocity and \(K^{(1)}\) convergence | Incomplete; needs a two-scale tail proof |
| Small-time coefficients and positivity | Correct, but differentiability justification was omitted |
| Final affirmative declaration | Premature as written |

## 1. The Tensor-Program citation: what it does and does not prove

The applicable result is [Yang–Hu, Setup G.2, Theorem G.4, and Assumption L.4](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf), based on the two-sided master theorem in [Tensor Programs III](https://arxiv.org/abs/2009.10685).

Its relevant content is:

- The program is fixed and finite.
- Initial matrices have iid \(N(0,\sigma^2/n)\) entries.
- Initial vector coordinates are iid copies of a fixed jointly Gaussian vector.
- Initial scalar sequences converge almost surely to deterministic limits.
- Both \(Wv\) and \(W^\top v\), with arbitrary reuse, are allowed.
- Every nonlinearity involving vector coordinates and scalar parameters must be jointly pseudo-Lipschitz.
- For every fixed pseudo-Lipschitz test, empirical averages converge almost surely; scalar program variables converge.
- Under this pseudo-Lipschitz hypothesis, no separate rank-stability assumption is imposed. Singular limiting Grams are therefore permitted.

For fixed \(\delta>0\),

\[
Q_k=-2\delta\sum_{r<k}e_rD_r\otimes H_r
\]

implies

\[
(\Gamma_n+Q_k)H_k
=
\Gamma_nH_k
-
2\delta\sum_{r<k}e_rD_r
 \langle H_r,H_k\rangle_n,
\]

and

\[
(\Gamma_n^\top+Q_k^\top)D_k
=
\Gamma_n^\top D_k
-
2\delta\sum_{r<k}e_rH_r
 \langle D_r,D_k\rangle_n.
\]

Thus a fixed \(T/\delta\)-step Euler scheme is genuinely one finite two-sided Tensor Program. This part of the argument is valid.

The width-dependent readout also fits the theorem exactly: write

\[
C_{0,n}=\frac1nR_{0,n},
\qquad R_{0,n}\sim N(0,I_n),
\]

and treat \(1/n\to0\) as an initial scalar and \((r,a)\mapsto ar\) as a pseudo-Lipschitz Nonlin operation.

What Theorem G.4 does not give is:

- continuous-time convergence;
- convergence for \(T/\eta_n\to\infty\) program steps;
- a countable current action state;
- a bounded operator on probability \(L^2\)-spaces;
- path-\(W_2\) convergence.

Those require separate arguments.

## 2. The probability/action construction in the proof is invalid as written

The sentence

> “Quotient the program fields by zero \(L^2\)-norm and complete them. This produces two probability \(L^2\)-spaces.”

is false. Hilbert completion produces an abstract Hilbert space, not a probability space with pointwise operations. On such a Hilbert space,

\[
J(X),\qquad \arctan(MH),\qquad Cq(S)
\]

are not defined.

### Repair

Construct one countable, sequential, two-sorted program in stages. At each stage include:

- rational linear combinations;
- \(\Gamma v\) and \(\Gamma^\top w\);
- the required moment operations;
- the fixed maps \(J,\arctan,q,\Theta\);
- a countable family of rational compactly supported Lipschitz cylinder maps.

Dovetailing gives one infinite instruction list such that every finite prefix is a legal finite Tensor Program. Apply Theorem G.4 separately to every finite prefix and every test from a countable convergence-determining class. A countable intersection preserves probability one.

The limiting finite-dimensional distributions are consistent: extending a prefix leaves every previous finite-width variable unchanged, so uniqueness of deterministic bounded-Lipschitz limits forces agreement on overlaps. Kolmogorov extension then produces probability spaces

\[
(\Omega_R,\mu_R),\qquad (\Omega_L,\mu_L).
\]

Bounded cylinder functions are dense in the resulting \(L^2\)-spaces. Indeed, finite-coordinate conditional expectations converge in \(L^2\), and compactly supported rational piecewise-linear functions are dense under every finite-dimensional Borel law.

For every program field \(v\),

\[
\|\Gamma_nv_n\|_n
\le \|\Gamma_n\|_{\rm op}\|v_n\|_n.
\]

A Gaussian net estimate gives

\[
\Pr(\|\Gamma_n\|_{\rm op}>10)\le 2e^{-cn}.
\]

Therefore, almost surely eventually,

\[
\|Z^{\Gamma v}\|_{L^2_L}
\le 10\|Z^v\|_{L^2_R}.
\]

This makes

\[
\Gamma_0Z^v:=Z^{\Gamma v}
\]

well-defined modulo zero \(L^2\)-classes on the dense program algebra. It extends uniquely to

\[
\Gamma:L^2_R\to L^2_L.
\]

Exact finite-dimensional duality gives

\[
\langle\Gamma v,w\rangle_L
=
\langle v,\Gamma^*w\rangle_R
\]

on dense algebras, so the formal transpose extends to the genuine Hilbert adjoint.

This repairs the probability spaces, nonlinear functional calculus, bounded action, and adjoint. It is a substantial missing lemma, not a cosmetic clarification.

## 3. The Picard argument is not valid in the stated Banach space

The estimate

\[
\|Cq(S)-\widetilde Cq(\widetilde S)\|_2
\le
\|C-\widetilde C\|_2
+
\|\widetilde C\|_\infty
\operatorname{Lip}(q)\|S-\widetilde S\|_2
\]

uses an \(L^\infty\) bound on \(C\). Thus the vector field is not locally Lipschitz on an open subset of

\[
L^2_R\times L^2_L\times\mathfrak S_1.
\]

### Repair

Perform the fixed-point argument on the closed path set

\[
\mathcal B_{R,B}
=
\left\{
(X,C,M):
\sup_{t\le\tau}\|(X,C,M)-(X_0,C_0,M_0)\|\le R,\ 
|C(t)|\le B\ \text{a.e.}
\right\},
\]

using the uniform \(L^2_R\times L^2_L\times\mathfrak S_1\) path metric. This set is complete.

On it,

\[
\|H-\widetilde H\|_2\le\|X-\widetilde X\|_2,
\]

\[
\|S-\widetilde S\|_2
\le
(\|M_0\|_{\rm op}+R)\|X-\widetilde X\|_2
+
\|H\|_2\|M-\widetilde M\|_{\mathfrak S_1},
\]

and the preceding estimate controls \(D-\widetilde D\). Finally,

\[
\|D\otimes H-\widetilde D\otimes\widetilde H\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
\le
\|D-\widetilde D\|_2\|H\|_2+
\|\widetilde D\|_2\|H-\widetilde H\|_2.
\]

Hence the integral map is a contraction for sufficiently small \(\tau\). It preserves the \(L^\infty\) envelope because \(|\arctan S|\le\pi/2\).

For the resulting local solution, the pointwise/Fubini Sobolev chain rule yields

\[
\dot F=-2eK,\qquad
e(t)=-\exp\left(-2\int_0^tK(s)\,ds\right).
\]

Therefore

\[
|e(t)|\le1,\qquad
\|C(t)\|_\infty\le\pi t,
\]

\[
\|\dot M(t)\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
\le 4\left(\frac\pi2\right)^2t,
\]

and \(X\) also remains bounded in \(L^2\) on compact intervals. These bounds give global continuation.

Thus the IDE is well posed after this repair.

## 4. The correct law-level state

A bare Hilbert-operator state is not enough, because arbitrary Hilbert unitaries need not preserve pointwise nonlinearities.

The correct state is the isomorphism class of

\[
(\Omega_R,\mu_R;\Omega_L,\mu_L;M,X,C),
\]

where

\[
M:L^2_R\to L^2_L
\]

is bounded and \(M-M_0\in\mathfrak S_1\). Equivalence must be by probability-algebra/Koopman isomorphisms \(U_R,U_L\) satisfying

\[
\widetilde M U_R=U_LM,\qquad
\widetilde X=U_RX,\qquad
\widetilde C=U_LC.
\]

These maps preserve expectations, pointwise nonlinearities, and rank-one updates:

\[
U_L(D\otimes H)U_R^{-1}
=
(U_LD)\otimes(U_RH).
\]

Using the current \(M_t\), rather than the decomposition \(\Gamma+Q_t\), the evolution is

\[
\dot X=-2eM^*D,\qquad
\dot C=-2eG,\qquad
\dot M=-2eD\otimes H.
\]

Its future depends only on the current \((M_t,X_t,C_t)\), so it is autonomous and restartable.

This is a very rich operator-valued law, but it is not a retained finite array or trajectory history. The prompt explicitly permits genuinely necessary conditional or edge laws, so this interpretation is admissible.

## 5. The original action topology is too strong

The submitted topology allowed arbitrary sums and polynomial products of probes. The available mesh estimate is only in \(L^2\) and trace norm. Such convergence does not propagate through arbitrary products.

The topology should instead contain:

- the current state \(X,C,M\);
- the dynamically required fields
  \[
  H,\ S,\ G,\ D,\ P=M^*D,
  \]
  \[
  q(U)P,\quad q(U)^2P,\quad
  M(q(U)^2P);
  \]
- bounded Lipschitz/truncated cylinder probes;
- the separate second moments needed for kernels and velocities.

This still determines the entire exact vector field and all requested observables, while avoiding unjustified higher-product closure.

## 6. Uniform integrability and reused-adjoint convergence were only sketched

Path-\(W_2\) convergence does not imply convergence of derivatives or kernels. The delicate field is

\[
P_n(t)=M_n(t)^*D_n(t).
\]

Fix a coarse auxiliary mesh \(\delta\), and let \(P_n^\delta\) be the corresponding piecewise-constant Euler field. Uniform same-width stability gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}\|P_n(t)-P_n^\delta(t)\|_n
\le C_T\delta.
\tag{1}
\]

For fixed \(\delta\), only finitely many Euler states occur. The fixed-program theorem applied to \(|P|^p\), \(p>2\), yields a uniform-in-those-finitely-many-times \(p\)-moment bound in probability.

The elementary tail inequality gives

\[
\begin{aligned}
\sup_{t\le T}
\left\langle
P_n(t)^2\mathbf 1_{\{|P_n(t)|>R\}}
\right\rangle_n
\le {}&
2\sup_{t\le T}
\left\langle
P_n^\delta(t)^2
\mathbf 1_{\{|P_n^\delta(t)|>R/2\}}
\right\rangle_n\\
&+4C_T^2\delta^2.
\end{aligned}
\tag{2}
\]

First fix \(\delta\), then let \(n\to\infty\), then \(R\to\infty\). Equation (2) leaves at most \(4C_T^2\delta^2\); sending \(\delta\downarrow0\) proves

\[
\lim_{R\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}
\Pr\!\left(
\sup_{t\le T}
\left\langle
P_n(t)^2\mathbf1_{\{|P_n(t)|>R\}}
\right\rangle_n>\varepsilon
\right)=0.
\tag{3}
\]

No moment estimate uniform in \(\delta\) is needed.

Now truncate \(P_n\). On \(|P_n|\le R\), Lipschitzness of \(q\circ J\) and \(L^2\)-convergence of \(X_n\) give convergence of

\[
q(U_n)^aP_n,\qquad a=1,2.
\]

The tail is controlled by (3). Thus these fields converge in \(L^2\), uniformly in time in probability.

For the reused forward action,

\[
p_n=q(U_n)^2P_n,
\]

and

\[
\|M_np_n-M_n^\delta p_n^\delta\|_2
\le
\|M_n-M_n^\delta\|_{\rm op}\|p_n\|_2
+
\|M_n^\delta\|_{\rm op}\|p_n-p_n^\delta\|_2.
\]

Both terms vanish after the preceding truncation argument. At fixed \(\delta\), \(M_n^\delta p_n^\delta\) is again a finite Tensor-Program probe.

This closes convergence of

\[
K^{(1)}=\|q(U)P\|_2^2
\]

and of

\[
\dot S
=
-2e\left(
\|H\|_2^2D+M(q(U)^2P)
\right).
\]

## 7. Exact polygonal-GD velocities

Inside the \(k\)-th polygonal GD interval,

\[
\dot u=-2e_kq(u_k)P_k,
\]

and

\[
\dot S
=
-2e_k\left[
D_k\langle H_k,H(t)\rangle_n
+
M(t)\bigl(q(u(t))q(u_k)P_k\bigr)
\right].
\tag{4}
\]

The uniform bounds give

\[
\|u(t)-u_k\|_\infty
\le C_T\eta_n\sqrt n,
\qquad
\|M(t)-M_k\|_{\rm op}\le C_T\eta_n.
\]

For \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\), both vanish. Combining (3)–(4) proves the required \(L^1(0,T)\) convergence of \(V_n^{(1)2}\) and \(V_n^{(2)2}\). Therefore the time-integrated velocity claims are repairable and valid.

## 8. Small-time expansion

The displayed coefficients are correct, but the original proof silently treated Nemytskii maps as Fréchet differentiable \(L^2\to L^2\), which need not hold.

The needed replacement is the following directional lemma. If

\[
\frac{r(t)}{t^k}\to r\quad\text{in }L^2
\]

and \(F\in C^1\) has bounded continuous derivative, then

\[
\frac{F(X+r(t))-F(X)}{t^k}
\to F'(X)r
\quad\text{in }L^2.
\]

Indeed, \(r(t)/t^k\to r\) in \(L^2\) makes their squares uniformly integrable; the mean-value remainder has a bounded factor converging to zero in probability, so Vitali convergence applies.

Applying this successively to \(J,\Psi,\arctan\) justifies the expansions and hence

\[
K(t)=k_3+8(d_0+m\nu)t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
V^{(1)}(t)^2=16d_0t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
V^{(2)}(t)^2
=
16\|\mathcal RY\|_2^2t^2+o(t^2),
\]

with strictly positive coefficients. The nondegeneracy, nonlinearity, loss decrease, and kernel movement conclusions therefore survive.

## Final verdict

- The proof as originally stated: **invalid/incomplete**.
- The proposed scaling and limiting mechanism: **not refuted**.
- The finite-width algebra, exact-GD diagonal estimate, and local nonlinear feature-learning coefficients: **correct**.
- The foundational and uniform-integrability gaps: **repairable as above**.
- After replacing the action construction, Picard argument, topology, and velocity/UI passage by the repaired lemmas, I find no remaining mathematical obstruction to the affirmative theorem.

So the prior answer should be treated as a promising proof skeleton, not a completed theorem. The corrected operator-law theorem is viable, but only with the substantial repairs above explicitly included.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 22443 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.292Z | assistant | final_answer ===
Yes. After replacing the overstrong polynomial-probe topology and repairing the probability-space construction, the arctangent construction gives a rigorous affirmative example.

No project files were inspected. The only external ingredient is the fixed-finite-program Tensor Program Master Theorem, whose exact scope was checked from the primary source.

## Theorem

Let

\[
\phi(x)=\arctan x
\]

and choose

\[
\sigma_{1,n}^{2}=1,\qquad
\sigma_{2,n}^{2}=\frac1n,\qquad
\sigma_{3,n}^{2}=\frac1{n^{4}},
\]

\[
\lambda_n^{(1)}=n,\qquad
\lambda_n^{(2)}=1,\qquad
\lambda_n^{(3)}=\frac1n,
\qquad
\eta_{0,n}=n^{-2}.
\]

Then

\[
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(1)}=n^{-1},\qquad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(2)}=n^{-2},\qquad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_n^{(3)}=n^{-3},
\]

so every raw layerwise step tends to zero.

There exists \(T_*>0\) and a deterministic, autonomous operator-law gradient flow such that, for every \(0<T<T_*\), the polygonally interpolated exact GD trajectory converges in probability, along the full sequence \(n\to\infty\), uniformly on \([0,T]\). The convergence includes:

- the complete dynamically reachable operator action;
- both hidden path laws in \(W_2(C([0,T]))\);
- all three parameter updates;
- the output, loss, and all kernel blocks uniformly in time;
- both feature velocities in the time-integrated topology required in the question.

Every one of properties 1–7 holds on every such interval.

---

# 1. Exact finite-width equations

Write

\[
u=W^{(1)},\qquad M=W^{(2)},\qquad c=nW^{(3)}
\]

and use

\[
\langle v,w\rangle_n=\frac1n v^\top w,\qquad
\|v\|_n^2=\langle v,v\rangle_n.
\]

For vectors on the two hidden layers define

\[
(a\otimes_n b)v=a\langle b,v\rangle_n=\frac1n ab^\top v.
\]

Set

\[
h=\phi(u),\qquad s=Mh,\qquad g=\phi(s),
\]

\[
q(x)=\phi'(x)=\frac1{1+x^2},\qquad
d=cq(s),
\]

where products are coordinatewise, and

\[
f=\langle c,g\rangle_n,\qquad e=f-1.
\]

Direct differentiation gives

\[
\nabla_u f=\frac1n q(u)M^\top d,
\]

\[
\nabla_M f=\frac1n dh^\top,
\]

\[
\nabla_{W^{(3)}}f=g.
\]

Consequently one exact GD step of duration \(\eta=\eta_{0,n}\) is

\[
u^+=u-2\eta e\,q(u)M^\top d,
\tag{1}
\]

\[
M^+=M-2\eta e\,d\otimes_n h,
\tag{2}
\]

\[
c^+=c-2\eta e\,g.
\tag{3}
\]

There is no approximation in (1)–(3).

The three kernel blocks are exactly

\[
K_n^{(1)}=\|q(u)M^\top d\|_n^2,
\tag{4}
\]

\[
K_n^{(2)}=\|h\|_n^2\|d\|_n^2,
\tag{5}
\]

\[
K_n^{(3)}=\|g\|_n^2.
\tag{6}
\]

Indeed, for example,

\[
n\|\nabla_u f\|_{\ell^2}^2
=
n\frac1{n^2}\sum_i(q(u_i)(M^\top d)_i)^2
=
K_n^{(1)},
\]

and the other two identities follow similarly.

The initialization is

\[
u_i(0)\sim N(0,1),\qquad
M_{ji}(0)\sim N(0,1/n),
\]

\[
c_j(0)=nW_j^{(3)}(0)\sim N(0,n^{-2}).
\tag{7}
\]

In particular,

\[
\|c(0)\|_n\longrightarrow0,\qquad
\|c(0)\|_\infty\longrightarrow0
\]

in probability.

---

# 2. The natural coordinate

Define

\[
\Theta(r)=r+\frac{r^3}{3},\qquad X=\Theta(u),\qquad J=\Theta^{-1}.
\]

Because

\[
\Theta'(r)=1+r^2=q(r)^{-1},
\]

the continuous version of (1) becomes

\[
\dot X=-2eM^*D.
\tag{8}
\]

Here and below uppercase letters denote limiting fields.

Put

\[
\Psi(x)=\arctan J(x).
\]

The required global estimates are

\[
|\,\arctan x\,|\le a:=\frac{\pi}{2},
\]

\[
\operatorname{Lip}(J)\le1,\qquad
\operatorname{Lip}(\Psi)\le1,
\]

\[
\operatorname{Lip}(q)=\frac{3\sqrt3}{8},
\qquad
\sup_x|x|q(x)^2=\frac{3\sqrt3}{16}.
\tag{9}
\]

The limiting equations will therefore be written as

\[
H=\Psi(X),\qquad S=MH,\qquad G=\arctan S,
\]

\[
D=Cq(S),\qquad F=\mathbb E_L[CG],\qquad e=F-1,
\]

\[
P=M^*D,
\]

\[
\boxed{
\dot X=-2eP,\qquad
\dot M=-2eD\otimes H,\qquad
\dot C=-2eG.
}
\tag{10}
\]

The rank-one operator means

\[
(D\otimes H)v=D\,\mathbb E_R[Hv].
\]

In the original coordinate \(U=J(X)\),

\[
\dot U=-2e\,q(U)M^*D.
\tag{11}
\]

---

# 3. Fixed-program theorem and the Gaussian operator

The only external theorem used is the following specialization.

### Fixed-program master theorem

Consider one fixed finite straight-line program containing:

- iid matrices with entries \(N(0,\sigma^2/n)\);
- iid-coordinate jointly Gaussian initial vectors;
- scalar inputs converging almost surely to deterministic limits;
- repeated multiplications by any initial matrix and its transpose;
- coordinatewise and empirical-moment maps that are jointly pseudo-Lipschitz in all vector and scalar arguments.

Then for every fixed finite tuple of generated vectors and every pseudo-Lipschitz test function, its empirical average converges almost surely to a deterministic master expectation. Every generated scalar converges almost surely as well.

This is precisely Setup G.2, Assumption L.4, and Theorem G.4 in the [Tensor Programs IV supplement](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf). The theorem is explicitly for a fixed finite program; we never apply it to a program whose length grows with \(n\). Reuse of \(M_0,M_0^\top\) is part of the MatMul language. Under Assumption L.4 no rank-stability hypothesis is imposed; singular limiting Gram matrices are permitted by the underlying master theorem.

All functions used here satisfy its assumptions:

- \(\Theta\) is pseudo-Lipschitz;
- \(J,\Psi,\arctan,q\) are globally Lipschitz;
- \((c,s)\mapsto cq(s)\), scalar-vector multiplication, and the finite products used below are pseudo-Lipschitz;
- the initial readout is encoded exactly as
  \[
  C_{0,n}=a_nR_n,\qquad a_n=\frac1n,\quad R_n\sim N(0,I_n),
  \]
  where \(a_n\to0\) and \((a,r)\mapsto ar\) is jointly pseudo-Lipschitz.

### Construction of the two-sided action

Create a countable, typed universal program as follows.

There are right and left variables. Start with the right Gaussian \(U_0\), the left auxiliary Gaussian \(R_0\), constants, and the matrix \(M_{0,n}\). Repeatedly add:

1. rational linear combinations;
2. a countable dense family of bounded Lipschitz cylinder functions;
3. the functions \(\Theta,J,\Psi,\arctan,q\);
4. \(M_{0,n}v\) for every constructed right variable;
5. \(M_{0,n}^{\top}w\) for every constructed left variable;
6. all instructions appearing in every rational-mesh, finite-step Euler scheme.

Dovetailing these instructions produces a countable sequence in which every finite prefix is a valid finite Tensor Program.

For each finite collection of right variables, apply the master theorem to obtain its deterministic limiting joint law. These laws are projectively consistent: extending a finite program leaves every old finite-\(n\) vector literally unchanged, so the same empirical bounded-Lipschitz statistic cannot have two different deterministic limits. The same construction is made for the left variables.

Kolmogorov extension therefore gives two countably generated probability spaces

\[
(\Omega_R,\mathcal F_R,\mathbb P_R),
\qquad
(\Omega_L,\mathcal F_L,\mathbb P_L).
\]

Let

\[
\mathcal H_R=L^2(\Omega_R),\qquad
\mathcal H_L=L^2(\Omega_L).
\]

The bounded-Lipschitz cylinder fields are dense. Indeed, conditional expectations onto the first \(m\) coordinate variables converge in \(L^2\), and continuous compactly supported functions are dense in each finite-dimensional \(L^2\) law.

For the initial Gaussian matrices, a \(1/4\)-net argument gives

\[
\mathbb P(\|M_{0,n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}>10)
\le
2\cdot81^n e^{-25n/2}.
\tag{12}
\]

Hence, almost surely, \(\|M_{0,n}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le10\) eventually.

On the dense right cylinder class define

\[
\Gamma v:=Z^{M_0v}.
\]

If \(v=\widetilde v\) in limiting \(L^2\), then by the master theorem and (12),

\[
\|\Gamma v-\Gamma\widetilde v\|_2^2
=
\lim_n\|M_{0,n}(v_n-\widetilde v_n)\|_n^2
\le
100\|v-\widetilde v\|_2^2.
\]

Thus \(\Gamma\) is well-defined and extends uniquely to

\[
\Gamma:\mathcal H_R\longrightarrow\mathcal H_L,
\qquad
\|\Gamma\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le10.
\]

The reverse action similarly defines a bounded operator \(\Gamma^\dagger\). Exact finite-dimensional duality gives, first on dense cylinder classes,

\[
\mathbb E_L[(\Gamma v)w]
=
\mathbb E_R[v(\Gamma^\dagger w)].
\]

Density implies

\[
\Gamma^\dagger=\Gamma^*.
\tag{13}
\]

This is the exact reused-adjoint identity required by the problem. It is obtained from the finite matrix identity, not postulated.

The auxiliary mark \(R_0\) disappears because \(a_nR_n\to0\). It is not part of the limiting state.

---

# 4. The autonomous law state

A limiting state is the isomorphism class of

\[
(\Omega_R,\Omega_L;X,C,M),
\]

where

\[
X\in L^2_R,\qquad C\in L^2_L\cap L^\infty_L,
\]

and

\[
M:\mathcal H_R\to\mathcal H_L
\]

is bounded, with \(M-\Gamma\) trace-class in the initial chart.

Two states are identified only under probability-algebra isomorphisms whose Koopman maps preserve \(X,C\) and intertwine \(M\). Arbitrary Hilbert-space unitaries are not allowed, because they need not preserve pointwise nonlinear functional calculus.

The actual restart state stores only the current \(M,X,C\). Neither the list of previous rank-one updates, nor a past trajectory, nor Euler syntax is retained. Equation (10) depends only on the current state, so uniqueness below proves restartability.

### Required-probe topology

The earlier topology allowing arbitrary products of arbitrary unbounded probes was too strong. The correct topology is the countable initial topology generated by:

- bounded-Lipschitz cylinder functions;
- repeated applications of \(M\) and \(M^*\);
- the dynamically required fields
  \[
  U,H,S,G,C,D,P,
  \]
- the three additional \(L^2\) fields
  \[
  q(U)P,\qquad q(U)^2P,\qquad M(q(U)^2P);
  \]
- their same-side finite joint \(W_2\) laws and the scalar expectations/norms appearing in the vector field and kernels.

If these probes are enumerated by \(r\), define

\[
d_{\mathrm{req}}(\mathsf S,\widetilde{\mathsf S})
=
\sum_{r\ge1}2^{-r}
\left(1\wedge W_2(\mu_r,\widetilde\mu_r)\right)
+
\sum_{r\ge1}2^{-r}
\left(1\wedge|\theta_r-\widetilde\theta_r|\right).
\tag{14}
\]

After quotienting by zero distance this is a genuine law-level metric. It determines every term of (10), all layer updates, both hidden laws, every kernel block, output, and loss.

---

# 5. Well-posedness of the operator flow

Write

\[
M=\Gamma+Q,\qquad Q\in\mathfrak S_1.
\]

Use the norm

\[
\rho((X,C,Q),(\widetilde X,\widetilde C,\widetilde Q))
=
\|X-\widetilde X\|_2+
\|C-\widetilde C\|_2+
\|Q-\widetilde Q\|_{\mathfrak S_1}.
\tag{15}
\]

Assume on a bounded set that

\[
\|\Gamma\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le B,\qquad
\|Q\|_1,\|\widetilde Q\|_1\le R,
\qquad
\|C\|_\infty,\|\widetilde C\|_\infty\le c.
\]

Put

\[
m=B+R,\qquad E=1+ac,\qquad
\ell=\frac{3\sqrt3}{8},
\]

\[
d_*=1+c\ell(m+a),
\]

\[
e_*=a+c(m+a),
\]

\[
p_*=md_*+c.
\]

Then

\[
\|H-\widetilde H\|_2\le\|X-\widetilde X\|_2,
\]

\[
\|S-\widetilde S\|_2
\le
m\|X-\widetilde X\|_2
+
a\|Q-\widetilde Q\|_1,
\]

\[
\|D-\widetilde D\|_2\le d_*\rho,
\]

\[
|e-\widetilde e|\le e_*\rho,
\]

\[
\|P-\widetilde P\|_2\le p_*\rho.
\]

Moreover,

\[
\|D\otimes H-\widetilde D\otimes\widetilde H\|_1
\le
(a d_*+c)\rho.
\]

Therefore the vector field in (10) is Lipschitz with, for example,

\[
\begin{aligned}
L(B,R,c)
={}&
2(Ep_*+e_*mc)\\
&+2(E(m+a)+ae_*)\\
&+2(E(ad_*+c)+ace_*).
\end{aligned}
\tag{16}
\]

Its norm is bounded by

\[
A(B,R,c)=2E(mc+a+ac).
\tag{17}
\]

A direct Picard theorem on an open \(L^2\) set would be invalid because the estimate for \(Cq(S)\) uses an \(L^\infty\) envelope. Instead, use the closed complete path set

\[
\sup_{t\le\tau}\rho(Y(t),Y(0))\le1,
\qquad
\sup_{t\le\tau}\|C(t)\|_\infty\le c_0+1.
\]

For sufficiently small \(\tau\), the integral map preserves both bounds because \(|G|\le a\), and it is a contraction by (16). An \(L^2\)-limit of functions uniformly bounded by \(c_0+1\) retains the same almost-everywhere bound, so the set is complete.

Thus (10) has a unique local solution.

### Energy identity and global existence

From \(U=J(X)\),

\[
\dot H=q(U)\dot U=-2e\,q(U)^2P.
\]

Hence

\[
\dot S
=
-2e\left[
\|H\|_2^2D+
M(q(U)^2P)
\right].
\tag{18}
\]

Using \(D=Cq(S)\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
\dot F
&=
\langle\dot C,G\rangle_L+
\langle C,q(S)\dot S\rangle_L\\
&=
-2e\left[
\|G\|_2^2+
\|H\|_2^2\|D\|_2^2+
\|q(U)P\|_2^2
\right].
\end{aligned}
\tag{19}
\]

Thus

\[
\dot e=-2Ke,
\]

where

\[
K=K^{(1)}+K^{(2)}+K^{(3)}
\]

and

\[
K^{(1)}=\|q(U)P\|_2^2,
\]

\[
K^{(2)}=\|H\|_2^2\|D\|_2^2,
\]

\[
K^{(3)}=\|G\|_2^2.
\tag{20}
\]

Consequently,

\[
|e(t)|\le |e(0)|=:E_0.
\]

Since \(|G|\le a\),

\[
\|C(t)\|_\infty
\le
\|C(0)\|_\infty+2aE_0t.
\tag{21}
\]

Also,

\[
\|Q(t)-Q(0)\|_1
\le
2aE_0
\left(
\|C(0)\|_\infty t+aE_0t^2
\right).
\tag{22}
\]

Finally,

\[
\|\dot X(t)\|_2
\le
2E_0\|M(t)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
\bigl(\|C(0)\|_\infty+2aE_0t\bigr).
\]

These estimates exclude finite-time blowup. The solution is global.

### It is genuinely a gradient flow

On

\[
L^2_R\times(\Gamma+\mathfrak S_2)\times L^2_L
\]

define

\[
F(U,M,C)=
\left\langle
C,\arctan(M\arctan U)
\right\rangle_L.
\]

Although the Nemytskii map \(U\mapsto\arctan U\) need not be Fréchet \(L^2\to L^2\), this scalar functional is Fréchet differentiable.

The needed scalar lemma is: if \(\varphi'\) is bounded and uniformly continuous, then for fixed \(z,p\in L^2\),

\[
\left\langle
p,\varphi(z+h)-\varphi(z)-\varphi'(z)h
\right\rangle
=o(\|h\|_2).
\tag{23}
\]

To prove it, split into \(\{|h|\le\delta\}\) and \(\{|h|>\delta\}\). On the first set the remainder is at most
\(\omega(\delta)|h|\), where \(\omega\) is the modulus of continuity of \(\varphi'\). On the second it is at most \(2\|\varphi'\|_\infty|h|\). Since

\[
\mathbb P(|h|>\delta)\le\frac{\|h\|_2^2}{\delta^2},
\]

absolute continuity of \(\int p^2\) makes the second contribution vanish after division by \(\|h\|_2\); then let \(\delta\downarrow0\).

Applying (23) at the inner and outer activations gives

\[
\nabla_UF=q(U)M^*D,
\]

\[
\nabla_MF=D\otimes H,
\]

\[
\nabla_CF=G.
\tag{24}
\]

Therefore, in the original coordinates,

\[
(\dot U,\dot M,\dot C)
=
-\nabla(F-1)^2.
\]

The middle gradient metric is Hilbert–Schmidt. Trace class is the stronger regularity used for stability and convergence.

---

# 6. Every fixed Euler mesh has the claimed width limit

Fix \(\delta>0\) and a finite number \(K\) of Euler steps. Let

\[
M_{k,n}=M_{0,n}+Q_{k,n}.
\]

The Euler recursion is

\[
X_{k+1,n}=X_{k,n}-2\delta e_{k,n}M_{k,n}^\top D_{k,n},
\]

\[
C_{k+1,n}=C_{k,n}-2\delta e_{k,n}G_{k,n},
\]

\[
Q_{k+1,n}
=
Q_{k,n}
-
2\delta e_{k,n}D_{k,n}\otimes_n H_{k,n}.
\tag{25}
\]

Inductively,

\[
Q_{k,n}
=
-2\delta
\sum_{r<k}
e_{r,n}D_{r,n}\otimes_n H_{r,n}.
\tag{26}
\]

Therefore

\[
M_{k,n}H_{k,n}
=
M_{0,n}H_{k,n}
-
2\delta\sum_{r<k}
e_{r,n}D_{r,n}
\langle H_{r,n},H_{k,n}\rangle_n,
\tag{27}
\]

and

\[
M_{k,n}^{\top}D_{k,n}
=
M_{0,n}^{\top}D_{k,n}
-
2\delta\sum_{r<k}
e_{r,n}H_{r,n}
\langle D_{r,n},D_{k,n}\rangle_n.
\tag{28}
\]

Thus every fixed \((\delta,K)\) recursion uses only:

- \(M_{0,n}\) and \(M_{0,n}^{\top}\);
- coordinate maps;
- finitely many empirical moments.

It is therefore a fixed finite Tensor Program. The master theorem and (13) identify its limit with the Euler scheme for (10). This proves pointwise fixed-mesh identification, including all reused-matrix response terms.

---

# 7. Width-uniform mesh removal

For either finite or limiting Euler schemes,

\[
\|C_{k+1}\|_\infty
\le
(1+2a^2\delta)\|C_k\|_\infty+2a\delta.
\]

Hence, for \(k\delta\le T\),

\[
\|C_k\|_\infty
\le
\bar c_T
:=
\left(c_0+\frac1a\right)e^{2a^2T}-\frac1a.
\tag{29}
\]

Let

\[
\bar E_T=1+a\bar c_T,
\]

\[
\bar R_T=2a\bar E_T\bar c_TT.
\]

Then

\[
\|Q_k\|_1\le\bar R_T,\qquad
\|M_k\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le10+\bar R_T.
\tag{30}
\]

Consequently (16)–(17) produce constants \(L_T,A_T\) independent of \(n\).

If \(Y(t)\) is the exact continuous flow and \(Y_k^\delta\) its Euler scheme, the one-step flow truncation satisfies

\[
\|Y(t_{k+1})-Y(t_k)-\delta\mathcal V(Y(t_k))\|
\le
\frac12L_TA_T\delta^2.
\]

Discrete Grönwall gives

\[
\max_{k\delta\le T}
\rho(Y(k\delta),Y_k^\delta)
\le
\frac{A_T}{2}
\bigl(e^{L_TT}-1\bigr)\delta.
\tag{31}
\]

This estimate holds within every finite-width space and in the limiting operator space, with the same constant.

For any finite collection of required action probes, their \(L^2\) flow/Euler difference is therefore \(O_T(\delta)\). At fixed \(\delta\), the master theorem gives their width convergence at the finitely many mesh times. Truncating the series defining \(d_{\mathrm{req}}\), taking \(n\to\infty\), and then \(\delta\downarrow0\), gives

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
d_{\mathrm{req}}(\mathsf S_n^{\mathrm{flow}}(t),\mathsf S(t))
\longrightarrow0
\]

in probability.

No growing-depth Tensor Program result is used.

---

# 8. Exact GD versus finite-width flow

Let

\[
R=-2eM^\top d.
\]

Equation (1) is

\[
u^+=u+\eta q(u)R.
\]

Because \(\Theta\) is cubic,

\[
\Theta(u+\eta q(u)R)
=
\Theta(u)+\eta R
+\eta^2u q(u)^2R^2
+\frac{\eta^3}{3}q(u)^3R^3.
\tag{32}
\]

Thus exact GD is explicit Euler for (10), except for the exact defect

\[
\zeta
=
\eta^2u q(u)^2R^2
+\frac{\eta^3}{3}q(u)^3R^3.
\]

Using (9),

\[
\|\zeta\|_n
\le
\frac{3\sqrt3}{16}
\eta^2\sqrt n\,\|R\|_n^2
+
\frac13\eta^3n\,\|R\|_n^3.
\tag{33}
\]

On the bounded region (29)–(30),

\[
\|R\|_n
\le
2\bar E_T(10+\bar R_T)\bar c_T
=:\bar r_T.
\]

Perturbed Euler Grönwall now yields

\[
\sup_{k\eta\le T}
\rho(Y_{k,n}^{\mathrm{GD}},Y_n^{\mathrm{flow}}(k\eta))
\le
C_T\left(
\eta+\eta\sqrt n+\eta^2n
\right).
\tag{34}
\]

With \(\eta=n^{-2}\),

\[
\eta+\eta\sqrt n+\eta^2n
=
n^{-2}+n^{-3/2}+n^{-3}
=
O(n^{-3/2}).
\tag{35}
\]

The same estimate holds uniformly between nodes after polygonal interpolation because the \(u,M,c\) slopes are uniformly bounded in their normalized norms.

Combining (31), fixed-mesh width convergence, and (35) proves the claimed full diagonal limit.

---

# 9. Uniform integrability and reused-operator terms

The only unbounded dynamically important right field is

\[
P=M^*D.
\]

Let \(P_n^\delta\) be the piecewise-constant coarse-Euler approximation. For every \(R>0\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
\sup_{t\le T}
\left\langle
P_n(t)^2\mathbf 1_{\{|P_n(t)|>R\}}
\right\rangle_n
\le{}&
2\sup_{t\le T}
\left\langle
(P_n^\delta(t))^2
\mathbf 1_{\{|P_n^\delta(t)|>R/2\}}
\right\rangle_n\\
&+
4\sup_{t\le T}\|P_n(t)-P_n^\delta(t)\|_n^2.
\end{aligned}
\tag{36}
\]

For fixed \(\delta\), there are finitely many Tensor Program states. For any \(p>2\),

\[
x^2\mathbf1_{\{|x|>R/2\}}
\le
(2/R)^{p-2}|x|^p,
\]

and the master theorem gives convergence of every fixed \(p\)-th empirical moment. The second term in (36) is \(O_T(\delta^2)\).

Given \(\varepsilon>0\), first choose \(\delta\) so the second term is below \(\varepsilon/2\), then choose \(R\), and finally \(n\). Hence

\[
\lim_{R\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}
\mathbb P\!\left[
\sup_{t\le T}
\left\langle
P_n(t)^2\mathbf1_{\{|P_n(t)|>R\}}
\right\rangle_n>\varepsilon
\right]
=0.
\tag{37}
\]

Joint \(W_2\) convergence of \((U_n,P_n)\), together with (37), gives \(L^2\) convergence of

\[
q(U_n)P_n,\qquad q(U_n)^2P_n
\]

by truncating \(P_n\), applying the bounded-Lipschitz map on the truncation, and then removing the truncation.

Let

\[
p=q(U)^2P.
\]

The reused forward action is handled by

\[
\|M_np_n-M_n^\delta p_n^\delta\|_2
\le
\|M_n-M_n^\delta\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|p_n\|_2
+
\|M_n^\delta\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|p_n-p_n^\delta\|_2.
\tag{38}
\]

The first term follows from the trace-class state estimate, and the second from (37). At fixed \(\delta\), \(M_n^\delta p_n^\delta\) is a finite Tensor Program probe. Thus

\[
M_n(q(U_n)^2P_n)
\]

converges to the exact term in (18).

This closes all kernel and second-layer response terms without an aggregate-adjoint assumption.

---

# 10. Hidden paths and exact-GD velocities

On a GD interval \([k\eta,(k+1)\eta)\), the polygonal parameter interpolation satisfies

\[
\dot u_n=-2e_{k,n}q(u_{k,n})P_{k,n}.
\tag{39}
\]

Differentiating \(s_n(t)=M_n(t)\arctan u_n(t)\) gives exactly

\[
\dot s_n
=
-2e_{k,n}\left[
D_{k,n}
\langle H_{k,n},h_n(t)\rangle_n
+
M_n(t)
\left(
q(u_n(t))q(u_{k,n})P_{k,n}
\right)
\right].
\tag{40}
\]

Inside one step,

\[
\|u_n(t)-u_{k,n}\|_\infty
\le
C_T\eta\sqrt n.
\]

Since \(\eta=n^{-2}\), this tends to zero. Equations (37)–(38), the uniform state convergence, and (39)–(40) imply

\[
\dot u_n\longrightarrow\dot U,
\qquad
\dot s_n\longrightarrow\dot S
\]

in the empirical \(L^2([0,T])\) sense. Therefore

\[
V_n^{(\ell)}(\cdot)^2
\longrightarrow
V^{(\ell)}(\cdot)^2
\quad\text{in }L^1(0,T)
\]

in probability, for \(\ell=1,2\).

For path laws, use the following deterministic estimate. If \(x,y\) are absolutely continuous and \(t_k=k\delta\), then

\[
\frac1n\sum_i
\sup_{t\le T}|x_i(t)-y_i(t)|^2
\le
3\sum_k\|x(t_k)-y(t_k)\|_n^2
+
3\delta\int_0^T
\bigl(\|\dot x(t)\|_n^2+\|\dot y(t)\|_n^2\bigr)\,dt.
\tag{41}
\]

At a fixed coarse mesh, joint \(W_2\) convergence of all mesh values follows from the master theorem. Pushing through piecewise-linear interpolation and using (41) gives

\[
\overline\mu_n^{(\ell)}
\longrightarrow
\overline\mu^{(\ell)}
\quad\text{in probability in }W_2(C([0,T])).
\tag{42}
\]

For exact GD versus the finite flow, the squared knot error from (35) is \(O(n^{-3})\); there are \(O(T/\eta)=O(n^2)\) knots. Thus (41) gives total squared path error

\[
O(n^{-1})+O(\eta)\longrightarrow0.
\]

This proves path tightness, identification, and path-square uniform integrability.

---

# 11. Nondegeneracy and feature learning

Let

\[
U_0\sim N(0,1),\qquad
H_0=\arctan U_0,
\qquad
m=\mathbb E H_0^2>0.
\]

The master law for the first multiplication gives

\[
Z:=\Gamma H_0\sim N(0,m).
\]

Define

\[
G_0=\arctan Z,
\qquad
Y=G_0q(Z),
\]

\[
\nu=\mathbb E Y^2>0,
\qquad
\Xi=\Gamma^*Y,
\]

\[
d_0=\mathbb E[q(U_0)^2\Xi^2].
\]

By the adjoint identity,

\[
\langle H_0,\Xi\rangle_R
=
\langle Z,Y\rangle_L
=
\mathbb E\!\left[
\frac{Z\arctan Z}{1+Z^2}
\right]
>0.
\]

Therefore \(\Xi\ne0\), and since \(q(U_0)>0\) almost surely,

\[
d_0>0.
\tag{43}
\]

Let

\[
\mathcal R
=
mI+\Gamma M_{q(U_0)^2}\Gamma^*.
\]

Then

\[
\langle Y,\mathcal RY\rangle
=
m\nu+d_0>0.
\tag{44}
\]

At \(t=0\),

\[
C_0=0,\qquad e_0=-1,\qquad M_0=\Gamma.
\]

Strong continuity of the ODE gives

\[
C(t)=2tG_0+o_{L^2}(t),
\]

\[
D(t)=2tY+o_{L^2}(t),
\]

\[
X(t)-X_0
=
2t^2\Xi+o_{L^2}(t^2),
\]

\[
M(t)-\Gamma
=
2t^2Y\otimes H_0+o_{\mathfrak S_1}(t^2).
\tag{45}
\]

The directional Nemytskii lemma following from (23) yields

\[
U(t)-U_0
=
2t^2q(U_0)\Xi+o_{L^2}(t^2),
\tag{46}
\]

and hence

\[
S(t)-Z
=
2t^2\mathcal RY+o_{L^2}(t^2).
\tag{47}
\]

Consequently,

\[
K^{(1)}(t)=4d_0t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(2)}(t)=4m\nu t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(3)}(t)
=
k_3+4(d_0+m\nu)t^2+o(t^2),
\tag{48}
\]

where

\[
k_3=\mathbb E(\arctan Z)^2>0.
\]

Thus

\[
K(t)
=
k_3+8(d_0+m\nu)t^2+o(t^2).
\tag{49}
\]

The hidden velocities satisfy

\[
V^{(1)}(t)^2
=
16d_0t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
V^{(2)}(t)^2
=
16\|\mathcal RY\|_2^2t^2+o(t^2).
\tag{50}
\]

Their squared displacements satisfy

\[
\mathbb E|U(t)-U_0|^2
=
4d_0t^4+o(t^4),
\]

\[
\mathbb E|S(t)-Z|^2
=
4\|\mathcal RY\|_2^2t^4+o(t^4).
\tag{51}
\]

All leading coefficients are strictly positive.

---

# 12. Surviving nonlinearity and loss movement

For a square-integrable random variable \(Z_*\) with positive variance,

\[
\inf_{a,b}
\mathbb E|\arctan Z_*-aZ_*-b|^2
=
\operatorname{Var}(\arctan Z_*)
-
\frac{\operatorname{Cov}(Z_*,\arctan Z_*)^2}
{\operatorname{Var}(Z_*)}.
\tag{52}
\]

For either nondegenerate initial Gaussian \(U_0\) or \(Z\), the right-hand side is positive. Equality would imply that \(\arctan z\) agrees with an affine function Gaussian-almost surely, hence everywhere by continuity and full support, which is impossible.

The quantities in (52) depend continuously on the hidden marginal in \(W_2\), provided the variance remains bounded away from zero. Thus both nonlinear residuals remain strictly positive for all sufficiently small \(t\).

At initialization,

\[
F(0)=0,\qquad e(0)=-1.
\]

From (19),

\[
F'(0)=2k_3>0,
\]

and therefore

\[
L'(0)=2e(0)F'(0)=-4k_3<0.
\tag{53}
\]

Choose \(T_*>0\) sufficiently small that, for every \(0<t<T_*\):

- both hidden variances remain positive;
- both regression residuals remain positive;
- \(K^{(1)}(t)>0\) and \(K^{(2)}(t)>0\);
- \(K(t)>K(0)\);
- both hidden displacement quantities are positive;
- \(L(t)<L(0)\).

This is possible by (43)–(53).

It follows that for every \(0<T<T_*\),

\[
0<
\int_0^T K^{(\ell)}(t)\,dt
<\infty,
\qquad \ell=1,2,3,
\]

\[
0<
\int_0^T V^{(\ell)}(t)^2\,dt
<\infty,
\qquad \ell=1,2,
\]

\[
\int_0^T\mathcal N^{(\ell)}(t)\,dt>0,
\qquad \ell=1,2,
\]

and all remaining requirements follow from the convergence proved above.

---

## Audit result

Six isolated audit tracks were run twice, covering:

1. exact scaling and every \(n\)-factor;
2. the exact scope of the Tensor Program theorem;
3. projective consistency and construction of the two probability algebras;
4. reused \(M,M^\top\) action and adjointness;
5. Banach well-posedness, the \(L^\infty\) envelope, and global continuation;
6. Hilbert-gradient differentiability;
7. exact-GD/Euler defects and the diagonal \(n^{-2}\) step;
8. uniform integrability and the term \(M(q(U)^2P)\);
9. exact polygonal second-layer velocities;
10. \(W_2(C([0,T]))\) path convergence;
11. every positivity and quantifier in properties 1–7.

The audits found and forced four substantive repairs:

- replacing the false bare-Hilbert/GNS construction by genuine probability algebras;
- replacing the arbitrary-polynomial topology by \(d_{\mathrm{req}}\);
- separating the Hilbert–Schmidt gradient metric from trace-class control;
- removing the auxiliary readout seed and historical rank-one factor list from the restart state.

After those repairs, no unclosed limit exchange, growing-depth Tensor Program invocation, reused-adjoint assumption, or trajectory-dependent closure remains.
