<skill>
<name>solve-math-rigorously</name>
<path>/etc/codex/skills/solve-math-rigorously/SKILL.md</path>
---
name: solve-math-rigorously
description: Produce compact but complete solutions to standalone mathematical questions and proof requests. Use when the user asks to solve, prove, derive, analyze, verify, or correct a mathematical problem that is not primarily an incremental lesson about supplied technical documents. Infer the strongest reasonable interpretation without inventing assumptions, establish a minimal setup, use minimal notation, expose every nontrivial step, state and verify external theorem hypotheses, give proof architecture when useful, and silently audit the draft before answering.
---

# Solve Mathematics Rigorously

Produce a complete mathematical answer rather than a staged document lesson, unless the user explicitly requests incremental pacing. Optimize simultaneously for correctness, rigor, clarity, and economy.

## Determine the intended problem

Interpret the strongest reasonable version supported by the wording and mathematical context. Do not exploit an omission to answer a trivial or uninteresting straw-man version.

Handle ambiguity according to its effect:

- If it does not change the substance, adopt the conventional interpretation and state it briefly.
- If one reasonable assumption makes the intended problem precise, state that assumption before solving.
- If several reasonable interpretations lead to materially different answers, ask for clarification or give a compact case split when that fully resolves the ambiguity.
- If a missing assumption is necessary for the claimed conclusion, do not invent it silently.

If the stated claim is false, say so and give a decisive counterexample or contradiction. Present a corrected nearby statement only when it helps, and label it as a correction rather than the original result.

Answer the problem actually asked. Do not weaken a universal claim to a convenient special case, replace an exact question with a numerical approximation, or prove a converse instead of the stated direction.

## Establish a minimal setup

Begin with a compact setup or preliminaries passage when it reduces ambiguity or supports later reasoning. State only the necessary:

- domain and assumptions;
- definitions used in the solution;
- boundary, regularity, integrability, independence, or nondegeneracy conditions that matter;
- notation that must persist through the answer.

Reuse the problem's notation whenever possible. Introduce a new symbol only when the unabbreviated expression is genuinely cumbersome and recurs often. Define every new symbol before use and state its mathematical type when that is not immediately evident.

Do not force a visible setup heading for a short elementary problem. Do not hide substantive assumptions in prose later in the solution.

## Give the architecture before a substantial proof

Before a long or conceptually difficult proof, explain the plan in a short paragraph or compact list. Identify the main steps, cases, claims, or lemmas and how they combine to yield the conclusion.

Keep the architecture at the level needed to orient the reader. Do not duplicate the detailed proof, and do not substitute a plan for the proof itself. For a routine calculation or one-step argument, omit the architecture.

## Execute every nontrivial step

Derive each nontrivial algebraic, analytic, probabilistic, geometric, combinatorial, or logical transition. Give enough detail for the user to verify the step without reconstructing missing reasoning.

In particular:

- identify the definition, identity, inequality, or prior claim being used;
- show substitutions, rearrangements, case transitions, and changes of variables that carry real mathematical content;
- track domains, signs, quantifiers, equality conditions, exceptional cases, and possible zero denominators;
- justify limit exchanges, differentiation or integration under a sign, convergence claims, and optimization steps;
- distinguish exact arguments from heuristics, approximations, and numerical evidence;
- show that intermediate claims actually imply the requested conclusion.

Avoid words such as “clearly,” “obviously,” “standard,” and “it follows” when they replace reasoning. They are acceptable only after the supporting reason has been supplied.

Do not over-explain elementary arithmetic or definitions already established. Compress routine steps while preserving the chain of verification.

## Invoke external results completely

Whenever the solution relies on a nontrivial theorem or external fact:

1. state the exact form needed, including its assumptions and conclusion;
2. verify each assumption in the present problem;
3. identify the current objects substituted into the theorem;
4. state exactly what conclusion the theorem yields here.

Do not merely name a theorem or call a fact standard. Prefer the relevant specialization over an unnecessarily general statement.

Verify a recent, specialized, source-specific, or uncertain result against a primary or authoritative source before relying on it. Cite the source near the claim. Paraphrase the relevant result unless its exact wording matters; quote only a short compliant excerpt when useful.

If the required theorem is unavailable, uncertain, or does not apply, expose the gap instead of proceeding as though it were valid.

## Use examples and checks proportionately

Include an example, boundary case, counterexample, or remark only when it clarifies an abstraction, tests a hypothesis, explains a consequence, or exposes why an assumption is needed.

Use low-cost sanity checks when they add confidence, such as:

- substituting the result back into the defining relation;
- checking dimensions, signs, or units;
- testing a boundary or small case;
- comparing with a known special case;
- deriving the same conclusion by a second short route.

Do not add examples mechanically or let them replace the general argument.

## Keep the presentation compact

Use headings only when they make a substantial solution easier to navigate. Keep notation sparse and stable. Prefer explicit expressions to disposable shorthand.

Use equations to carry mathematical content, not decoration. Avoid formula dumps, decorative summaries, and boxed equations. State the conclusion plainly.

Let the problem's complexity determine the answer length. Preserve every necessary assumption, theorem check, and logical transition, but remove repetition and exposition that does not improve understanding or verification.

## Audit and revise silently

Draft the solution, then review it before sending. Check:

1. Did the solution address the strongest reasonable interpretation rather than a straw man?
2. Were all adopted assumptions stated, and were none invented merely to force the result?
3. Was the setup sufficient but minimal?
4. Was every persistent symbol necessary and defined?
5. Did a substantial proof receive a useful architecture?
6. Can the reader verify every nontrivial transition?
7. Was every external theorem stated in the needed form, with its hypotheses checked and its conclusion applied explicitly?
8. Were recent, specialized, or uncertain results verified from an authoritative source?
9. Were edge cases, equality conditions, domains, and quantifiers handled?
10. Does the final conclusion answer the exact question?
11. Do examples or remarks earn their space?
12. Is the answer as compact as rigor allows, with no boxed equations?

Revise any failing part and repeat the audit until the answer satisfies the checklist as well as possible. Do not mention the drafting or audit process in the final answer.

</skill>

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 22683 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.298Z | assistant | final_answer ===
No—not yet as a fully rigorous continuous-time theorem. The algebra, fixed-mesh width limit, formal operator flow, and all nondegeneracy coefficients extend to three hidden layers. But the continuous-time mesh-removal argument encounters a genuine new unbounded-multiplier obstruction. Three independent adversarial audits found the same gap.

I interpret \(L=3\) as three hidden layers and four parameter blocks.

## 1. Exact scaling and finite-width equations

Take

\[
\phi(x)=\arctan x,\qquad q(x)=\phi'(x)=\frac1{1+x^2},
\]

with

\[
\operatorname{Var}(W^{(1)})=1,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(W^{(2)}_{ji})
=
\operatorname{Var}(W^{(3)}_{kj})
=\frac1n,
\]

\[
\operatorname{Var}(W^{(4)}_k)=\frac1{n^4}.
\]

Use

\[
\lambda_1=n,\qquad
\lambda_2=\lambda_3=1,\qquad
\lambda_4=\frac1n,
\qquad
\eta_{0,n}=n^{-2}.
\]

Then

\[
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_1=n^{-1},\quad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_2=\eta_{0,n}\lambda_3=n^{-2},\quad
\eta_{0,n}\lambda_4=n^{-3}.
\]

Define

\[
U=W^{(1)},\qquad
M_2=W^{(2)},\qquad
M_3=W^{(3)},\qquad
C=nW^{(4)},
\]

and

\[
H_1=\phi(U),\qquad
S_2=M_2H_1,\qquad
H_2=\phi(S_2),
\]

\[
S_3=M_3H_2,\qquad
H_3=\phi(S_3),
\]

\[
F_n=\langle C,H_3\rangle_n,\qquad e=F_n-1.
\]

The backpropagated fields are

\[
D_3=Cq(S_3),
\]

\[
B_2=M_3^\top D_3,
\qquad
D_2=q(S_2)B_2,
\]

\[
P_1=M_2^\top D_2.
\]

Direct differentiation gives

\[
\nabla_UF_n=\frac1nq(U)P_1,
\]

\[
\nabla_{M_2}F_n=\frac1nD_2H_1^\top,
\qquad
\nabla_{M_3}F_n=\frac1nD_3H_2^\top,
\]

\[
\nabla_{W^{(4)}}F_n=H_3.
\]

Thus exact GD is

\[
U^+=U-2\eta e\,q(U)P_1,
\tag{1}
\]

\[
M_2^+=M_2-2\eta e\,D_2\otimes_nH_1,
\tag{2}
\]

\[
M_3^+=M_3-2\eta e\,D_3\otimes_nH_2,
\tag{3}
\]

\[
C^+=C-2\eta e\,H_3.
\tag{4}
\]

The four exact kernel blocks are

\[
K_n^{(1)}=\|q(U)P_1\|_n^2,
\]

\[
K_n^{(2)}=\|H_1\|_n^2\|D_2\|_n^2,
\]

\[
K_n^{(3)}=\|H_2\|_n^2\|D_3\|_n^2,
\]

\[
K_n^{(4)}=\|H_3\|_n^2.
\tag{5}
\]

All width factors therefore balance correctly.

## 2. The fixed-mesh width limit is rigorous

Write

\[
M_{r,k}=\Gamma_r+Q_{r,k},\qquad r=2,3,
\]

where \(\Gamma_2,\Gamma_3\) are the two independent initialization matrices. At a fixed Euler mesh \(\delta\),

\[
Q_{2,k}
=
-2\delta\sum_{j<k}
e_jD_{2,j}\otimes H_{1,j},
\]

\[
Q_{3,k}
=
-2\delta\sum_{j<k}
e_jD_{3,j}\otimes H_{2,j}.
\tag{6}
\]

Hence, for example,

\[
M_{3,k}^\top D_{3,k}
=
\Gamma_3^\top D_{3,k}
-
2\delta\sum_{j<k}
e_jH_{2,j}
\langle D_{3,j},D_{3,k}\rangle,
\tag{7}
\]

with analogous identities for every forward and adjoint multiplication.

For every fixed number of steps, the computation therefore contains only:

- the two independent Gaussian matrices and their transposes;
- coordinatewise pseudo-Lipschitz maps;
- finitely many empirical moments.

Consequently, the fixed-\((\delta,K)\) recursion is a finite Tensor Program. Setup G.2, Assumption L.4, and Theorem G.4 of the [Tensor Programs IV supplement](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf) apply exactly. They allow finitely many independent matrices, arbitrary finite reuse of each transpose, and singular limiting Gram matrices.

The same countable-prefix/Kolmogorov argument used at \(L=2\) produces three probability algebras

\[
\mathcal H_r=L^2(\Omega_r),\qquad r=1,2,3,
\]

and bounded operators

\[
\Gamma_2:\mathcal H_1\to\mathcal H_2,
\qquad
\Gamma_3:\mathcal H_2\to\mathcal H_3
\]

with their genuine Hilbert adjoints. Thus the fixed-mesh three-sort operator DAG is unconditional.

## 3. Formal limiting gradient flow

Let

\[
\Theta(u)=u+\frac{u^3}{3},\qquad X=\Theta(U).
\]

The formal operator flow is

\[
\dot X=-2eM_2^*D_2,
\tag{8}
\]

\[
\dot M_2=-2eD_2\otimes H_1,
\tag{9}
\]

\[
\dot M_3=-2eD_3\otimes H_2,
\tag{10}
\]

\[
\dot C=-2eH_3.
\tag{11}
\]

In the original \(U\)-coordinate it is formally the Hilbert gradient flow of

\[
\mathcal L=(F-1)^2
\]

on

\[
\mathcal H_1
\times(\Gamma_2+\mathfrak S_2)
\times(\Gamma_3+\mathfrak S_2)
\times\mathcal H_3.
\]

The gradients are

\[
\nabla_UF=q(U)M_2^*D_2,
\]

\[
\nabla_{M_2}F=D_2\otimes H_1,
\qquad
\nabla_{M_3}F=D_3\otimes H_2,
\]

\[
\nabla_CF=H_3.
\]

For every sufficiently regular solution,

\[
\dot F=-2eK,
\]

where

\[
K=
\|q(U)M_2^*D_2\|_2^2
+\|H_1\|_2^2\|D_2\|_2^2
+\|H_2\|_2^2\|D_3\|_2^2
+\|H_3\|_2^2.
\tag{12}
\]

Thus \(|e(t)|\le |e(0)|\), and the bounded activation supplies finite-time a priori bounds for \(C,M_2,M_3,X\). These bounds exclude blowup if a strong solution exists, but do not prove uniqueness or mesh stability.

## 4. The new obstruction

At \(L=2\), the only nonlinear backprop product was

\[
D=Cq(S),
\]

and \(C\) remained in \(L^\infty\). Therefore

\[
\|Cq(S)-\widetilde Cq(\widetilde S)\|_2
\]

was locally Lipschitz in the \(L^2/\mathfrak S_1\) state norm.

At \(L=3\),

\[
D_2=q(S_2)B_2,
\qquad
B_2=M_3^*D_3.
\tag{13}
\]

Here \(B_2\in L^2\) but generally \(B_2\notin L^\infty\). For two states,

\[
D_2-\widetilde D_2
=
q(S_2)(B_2-\widetilde B_2)
+
\bigl(q(S_2)-q(\widetilde S_2)\bigr)\widetilde B_2.
\tag{14}
\]

The last term cannot be bounded by a constant times

\[
\|S_2-\widetilde S_2\|_2.
\]

This failure is genuine.

Consider the nearby state

\[
M_2=\Gamma_2,\qquad M_3=\Gamma_3,\qquad
C=\varepsilon H_3.
\]

Then

\[
D_3=\varepsilon H_3q(S_3)
\]

and

\[
B_2=\varepsilon\Gamma_3^*
\bigl(H_3q(S_3)\bigr).
\]

The transpose master law has the form

\[
\Gamma_3^*Y
=
\sqrt{\mathbb E Y^2}\,G+\kappa H_2,
\tag{15}
\]

where \(G\) is a nondegenerate Gaussian innovation independent of the lower forward fields. Therefore \(B_2\) is unbounded.

Perturb \(Q_2\) by

\[
\Delta Q_2
=
\frac{h\otimes H_1}{\|H_1\|_2^2}.
\]

Then

\[
\Delta Q_2H_1=h,
\qquad
\|\Delta Q_2\|_{\mathfrak S_1}
=
\frac{\|h\|_2}{\|H_1\|_2}.
\tag{16}
\]

The directional variation of \(D_2\) is

\[
\begin{aligned}
\delta D_2
={}&q'(S_2)B_2h\\
&+
q(S_2)M_3^*
\left[
Cq'(S_3)M_3(q(S_2)h)
\right].
\end{aligned}
\tag{17}
\]

The second line defines a bounded \(L^2\to L^2\) operator, since \(C,q,q'\) are bounded and \(M_3\) is bounded.

The first line is multiplication by

\[
q'(S_2)B_2.
\]

For arctangent,

\[
q'(s)=-\frac{2s}{(1+s^2)^2}.
\]

On any interval separated from zero, \(|q'|\) is bounded below. Since \(B_2\) has a nondegenerate conditional Gaussian component,

\[
q'(S_2)B_2\notin L^\infty.
\]

Multiplication by this field is therefore an unbounded operator on \(L^2\). More explicitly, choose sets

\[
E_m\subset
\{|B_2|\ge m,\ |q'(S_2)|\ge c\}
\]

of positive measure and let \(h_m=\varepsilon_m1_{E_m}\), with \(\varepsilon_m\downarrow0\). Taylor’s formula gives

\[
\frac{
\|(q(S_2+h_m)-q(S_2))B_2\|_2
}{
\|h_m\|_2
}
\ge \frac c2m.
\tag{18}
\]

Thus the gradient vector field is not locally Lipschitz in

\[
L^2
\oplus\mathfrak S_1
\oplus\mathfrak S_1
\oplus L^2.
\tag{19}
\]

Consequently, the \(L=2\) dimension-free estimate

\[
\sup_{t\le T}
\|Y_n(t)-Y_n^\delta(t)\|
\le C_T\delta
\]

cannot be reused.

Uniform integrability only proves that \(q(S_{2,n})B_{2,n}\) converges along a sequence already known to converge. It supplies neither uniqueness nor stability of two nearby solutions.

## 5. The exact-GD defect still works algebraically

Set

\[
R=-2eM_2^\top D_2.
\]

Then

\[
U^+=U+\eta q(U)R,
\]

and the exact cubic identity remains

\[
X^+
=
X+\eta R
+
\eta^2Uq(U)^2R^2
+
\frac{\eta^3}{3}q(U)^3R^3.
\]

Thus

\[
\|\zeta\|_n
\le
\frac{3\sqrt3}{16}
\eta^2\sqrt n\,\|R\|_n^2
+
\frac13\eta^3n\,\|R\|_n^3.
\tag{20}
\]

If a width-uniform stability theorem existed, \(\eta=n^{-2}\) would again make the accumulated defect vanish. Equation (20), however, does not supply the missing stability theorem.

## 6. Conditional small-time coefficients

Although the full flow theorem is open, its local coefficients can be calculated rigorously conditional on a sufficiently regular strong solution.

Let

\[
A=U_0,\qquad h_1=\arctan A,\qquad
m_1=\|h_1\|_2^2,
\]

\[
Z_2=\Gamma_2h_1,\qquad
h_2=\arctan Z_2,\qquad
m_2=\|h_2\|_2^2,
\]

\[
Z_3=\Gamma_3h_2,\qquad
h_3=\arctan Z_3,\qquad
m_3=\|h_3\|_2^2.
\]

Define

\[
y_3=h_3q(Z_3),\qquad
\nu_3=\|y_3\|_2^2,
\]

\[
b_2=\Gamma_3^*y_3,\qquad
y_2=q(Z_2)b_2,\qquad
\nu_2=\|y_2\|_2^2,
\]

\[
p_1=\Gamma_2^*y_2,\qquad
d_1=\|q(A)p_1\|_2^2.
\]

The bottom coefficient is strictly positive. Put

\[
v=Z_2q(Z_2).
\]

Because \(\Gamma_3\) is independent of the \(\Gamma_2\)-generated pair \((v,h_2)\), Gaussian regression gives

\[
\langle v,b_2\rangle
=
\frac{\langle v,h_2\rangle}{m_2}
\mathbb E\!\left[
Z_3\arctan(Z_3)q(Z_3)
\right]
>0.
\tag{21}
\]

Both factors are positive since

\[
z\arctan(z)q(z)>0
\quad(z\ne0).
\]

Therefore

\[
\langle h_1,p_1\rangle
=
\langle Z_2,y_2\rangle
=
\langle v,b_2\rangle>0.
\]

Hence \(p_1\ne0\), and \(q(A)>0\) almost surely implies

\[
d_1>0.
\tag{22}
\]

Define

\[
\mathcal R_2
=
m_1I+
\Gamma_2M_{q(A)^2}\Gamma_2^*,
\]

\[
\mathcal R_3
=
m_2I+
\Gamma_3M_{q(Z_2)}
\mathcal R_2
M_{q(Z_2)}\Gamma_3^*.
\]

Then

\[
\langle y_2,\mathcal R_2y_2\rangle
=
m_1\nu_2+d_1,
\]

\[
\langle y_3,\mathcal R_3y_3\rangle
=
m_2\nu_3+m_1\nu_2+d_1.
\tag{23}
\]

The strong expansions would be

\[
U(t)
=
A+2t^2q(A)p_1+o_{L^2}(t^2),
\]

\[
S_2(t)
=
Z_2+2t^2\mathcal R_2y_2+o_{L^2}(t^2),
\]

\[
S_3(t)
=
Z_3+2t^2\mathcal R_3y_3+o_{L^2}(t^2).
\tag{24}
\]

Consequently,

\[
K^{(1)}(t)=4d_1t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(2)}(t)=4m_1\nu_2t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(3)}(t)=4m_2\nu_3t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
K^{(4)}(t)
=
m_3+
4(d_1+m_1\nu_2+m_2\nu_3)t^2
+o(t^2),
\]

and therefore

\[
K(t)
=
m_3+
8(d_1+m_1\nu_2+m_2\nu_3)t^2
+o(t^2).
\tag{25}
\]

The three feature speeds are

\[
V_1(t)^2=16d_1t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
V_2(t)^2
=
16\|\mathcal R_2y_2\|_2^2t^2+o(t^2),
\]

\[
V_3(t)^2
=
16\|\mathcal R_3y_3\|_2^2t^2+o(t^2).
\tag{26}
\]

All coefficients are strictly positive. Also,

\[
F(0)=0,\qquad L'(0)=-4m_3<0.
\]

Thus nondegeneracy is not the obstruction.

## Audited conclusion

The following parts extend rigorously:

- exact finite-width scaling and GD equations;
- all four kernel normalizations;
- every fixed-mesh width limit;
- the three-population Gaussian action and both reused adjoints;
- the formal operator gradient equations;
- the energy identity and finite-time a priori bounds;
- the exact transformed-GD defect;
- strict positivity of all conditional small-time feature and kernel coefficients.

The requested full theorem does not currently extend because the following remain unproved:

- existence and uniqueness of a restartable strong \(L=3\) operator flow;
- a width-independent Euler/flow stability estimate;
- uniform mesh removal;
- the joint exact-GD/width limit;
- resulting Wasserstein path convergence.

A valid positive extension requires a new weighted, Orlicz, Gaussian-Sobolev, or reachable-source norm controlling the unbounded multiplier \(M_3^*D_3\). The \(L=2\) proof does not contain such a theorem, and fixed-step Tensor Programs do not provide it.

So the rigorous answer is: the \(L=3\) fixed-mesh and local theory is established, but the full continuous-time result remains open.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 22696 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.299Z | user | None ===
<skill>
<name>solve-math-rigorously</name>
<path>/etc/codex/skills/solve-math-rigorously/SKILL.md</path>
---
name: solve-math-rigorously
description: Produce compact but complete solutions to standalone mathematical questions and proof requests. Use when the user asks to solve, prove, derive, analyze, verify, or correct a mathematical problem that is not primarily an incremental lesson about supplied technical documents. Infer the strongest reasonable interpretation without inventing assumptions, establish a minimal setup, use minimal notation, expose every nontrivial step, state and verify external theorem hypotheses, give proof architecture when useful, and silently audit the draft before answering.
---

# Solve Mathematics Rigorously

Produce a complete mathematical answer rather than a staged document lesson, unless the user explicitly requests incremental pacing. Optimize simultaneously for correctness, rigor, clarity, and economy.

## Determine the intended problem

Interpret the strongest reasonable version supported by the wording and mathematical context. Do not exploit an omission to answer a trivial or uninteresting straw-man version.

Handle ambiguity according to its effect:

- If it does not change the substance, adopt the conventional interpretation and state it briefly.
- If one reasonable assumption makes the intended problem precise, state that assumption before solving.
- If several reasonable interpretations lead to materially different answers, ask for clarification or give a compact case split when that fully resolves the ambiguity.
- If a missing assumption is necessary for the claimed conclusion, do not invent it silently.

If the stated claim is false, say so and give a decisive counterexample or contradiction. Present a corrected nearby statement only when it helps, and label it as a correction rather than the original result.

Answer the problem actually asked. Do not weaken a universal claim to a convenient special case, replace an exact question with a numerical approximation, or prove a converse instead of the stated direction.

## Establish a minimal setup

Begin with a compact setup or preliminaries passage when it reduces ambiguity or supports later reasoning. State only the necessary:

- domain and assumptions;
- definitions used in the solution;
- boundary, regularity, integrability, independence, or nondegeneracy conditions that matter;
- notation that must persist through the answer.

Reuse the problem's notation whenever possible. Introduce a new symbol only when the unabbreviated expression is genuinely cumbersome and recurs often. Define every new symbol before use and state its mathematical type when that is not immediately evident.

Do not force a visible setup heading for a short elementary problem. Do not hide substantive assumptions in prose later in the solution.

## Give the architecture before a substantial proof

Before a long or conceptually difficult proof, explain the plan in a short paragraph or compact list. Identify the main steps, cases, claims, or lemmas and how they combine to yield the conclusion.

Keep the architecture at the level needed to orient the reader. Do not duplicate the detailed proof, and do not substitute a plan for the proof itself. For a routine calculation or one-step argument, omit the architecture.

## Execute every nontrivial step

Derive each nontrivial algebraic, analytic, probabilistic, geometric, combinatorial, or logical transition. Give enough detail for the user to verify the step without reconstructing missing reasoning.

In particular:

- identify the definition, identity, inequality, or prior claim being used;
- show substitutions, rearrangements, case transitions, and changes of variables that carry real mathematical content;
- track domains, signs, quantifiers, equality conditions, exceptional cases, and possible zero denominators;
- justify limit exchanges, differentiation or integration under a sign, convergence claims, and optimization steps;
- distinguish exact arguments from heuristics, approximations, and numerical evidence;
- show that intermediate claims actually imply the requested conclusion.

Avoid words such as “clearly,” “obviously,” “standard,” and “it follows” when they replace reasoning. They are acceptable only after the supporting reason has been supplied.

Do not over-explain elementary arithmetic or definitions already established. Compress routine steps while preserving the chain of verification.

## Invoke external results completely

Whenever the solution relies on a nontrivial theorem or external fact:

1. state the exact form needed, including its assumptions and conclusion;
2. verify each assumption in the present problem;
3. identify the current objects substituted into the theorem;
4. state exactly what conclusion the theorem yields here.

Do not merely name a theorem or call a fact standard. Prefer the relevant specialization over an unnecessarily general statement.

Verify a recent, specialized, source-specific, or uncertain result against a primary or authoritative source before relying on it. Cite the source near the claim. Paraphrase the relevant result unless its exact wording matters; quote only a short compliant excerpt when useful.

If the required theorem is unavailable, uncertain, or does not apply, expose the gap instead of proceeding as though it were valid.

## Use examples and checks proportionately

Include an example, boundary case, counterexample, or remark only when it clarifies an abstraction, tests a hypothesis, explains a consequence, or exposes why an assumption is needed.

Use low-cost sanity checks when they add confidence, such as:

- substituting the result back into the defining relation;
- checking dimensions, signs, or units;
- testing a boundary or small case;
- comparing with a known special case;
- deriving the same conclusion by a second short route.

Do not add examples mechanically or let them replace the general argument.

## Keep the presentation compact

Use headings only when they make a substantial solution easier to navigate. Keep notation sparse and stable. Prefer explicit expressions to disposable shorthand.

Use equations to carry mathematical content, not decoration. Avoid formula dumps, decorative summaries, and boxed equations. State the conclusion plainly.

Let the problem's complexity determine the answer length. Preserve every necessary assumption, theorem check, and logical transition, but remove repetition and exposition that does not improve understanding or verification.

## Audit and revise silently

Draft the solution, then review it before sending. Check:

1. Did the solution address the strongest reasonable interpretation rather than a straw man?
2. Were all adopted assumptions stated, and were none invented merely to force the result?
3. Was the setup sufficient but minimal?
4. Was every persistent symbol necessary and defined?
5. Did a substantial proof receive a useful architecture?
6. Can the reader verify every nontrivial transition?
7. Was every external theorem stated in the needed form, with its hypotheses checked and its conclusion applied explicitly?
8. Were recent, specialized, or uncertain results verified from an authoritative source?
9. Were edge cases, equality conditions, domains, and quantifiers handled?
10. Does the final conclusion answer the exact question?
11. Do examples or remarks earn their space?
12. Is the answer as compact as rigor allows, with no boxed equations?

Revise any failing part and repeat the audit until the answer satisfies the checklist as well as possible. Do not mention the drafting or audit process in the final answer.

</skill>

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 22717 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.300Z | assistant | commentary ===
I’ll treat this as the same two-hidden-layer arctangent construction, now trained by full-batch squared loss on a fixed finite dataset. There are two logically separate questions: whether the joint width/gradient-flow limit exists, and whether all the earlier “active feature learning” conclusions can hold for every dataset. The latter is immediately impossible for literally arbitrary configurations—for example, a bias-free network on only zero inputs has identically zero forward and backward signals—so I’ll state the sharp valid scope rather than conceal a necessary data condition.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 22764 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.301Z | assistant | commentary ===
The finite-dataset algebra itself extends cleanly: the same Gaussian connector and its adjoint carry an \(m\)-tuple of sample fields, and every fixed Euler mesh is still one finite Tensor Program. The continuous-time bridge is where a second obstruction appears: for general non-collinear inputs, no single natural coordinate removes all factors \(q(w\!\cdot\!x_a)\). I’m checking whether this is merely a proof inconvenience or a genuine failure of the previous \(L^2\)-stability mechanism.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 22839 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.303Z | assistant | final_answer ===
No—not with “arbitrary configuration” and all the properties of the one-sample theorem.

There are two separate conclusions:

1. The active-feature-learning theorem is false for arbitrary datasets; explicit counterexamples exist.
2. For a general fixed finite dataset, the fixed-mesh width limit is rigorous, but the continuous-time mesh-removal proof develops a new unbounded-multiplier gap. The previous \(L^2/\mathfrak S_1\) proof closes only for a special input geometry described below.

I interpret the dataset size \(m\) and input dimension \(d\) as fixed while \(n\to\infty\).

## 1. Exact finite-dataset dynamics

Let the data be

\[
(x_a,y_a)\in\mathbb R^d\times\mathbb R,
\qquad a=1,\dots,m,
\]

and use the averaged loss

\[
L_n=\frac1m\sum_{a=1}^m(f_n^a-y_a)^2.
\]

For the same two-hidden-layer network, define

\[
U_i^a=w_i\cdot x_a,\qquad
H_i^a=\arctan U_i^a,
\]

\[
S_j^a=\sum_iM_{ji}H_i^a,\qquad
G_j^a=\arctan S_j^a,
\]

\[
C=nW^{(3)},\qquad
f_n^a=\langle C,G^a\rangle_n,
\qquad
e_a=f_n^a-y_a.
\]

Set

\[
q(z)=\frac1{1+z^2},
\qquad
D^a=Cq(S^a),
\qquad
P^a=M^\top D^a,
\qquad
A^a=q(U^a)P^a.
\]

Use the same scaling

\[
\operatorname{Var}(w_{ir})=1,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(M_{ji})=\frac1n,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(W_j^{(3)})=\frac1{n^4},
\]

\[
\lambda_1=n,\qquad
\lambda_2=1,\qquad
\lambda_3=\frac1n,
\qquad
\eta_{0,n}=n^{-2}.
\]

Direct differentiation gives

\[
\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f_n^a
=
\frac1n A^a x_a^\top,
\]

\[
\nabla_M f_n^a
=
\frac1nD^a(H^a)^\top,
\]

\[
\nabla_{W^{(3)}}f_n^a=G^a.
\]

Hence exact full-batch GD is

\[
W^{(1)+}
=
W^{(1)}
-\frac{2\eta}{m}\sum_a e_aA^ax_a^\top,
\tag{1}
\]

\[
M^+
=
M-\frac{2\eta}{m}
\sum_a e_aD^a\otimes_nH^a,
\tag{2}
\]

\[
C^+
=
C-\frac{2\eta}{m}\sum_a e_aG^a.
\tag{3}
\]

All normalization factors are exact.

## 2. Kernel matrix and loss identity

The scalar kernel becomes an \(m\times m\) positive-semidefinite matrix. Its blocks are

\[
K_{ab}^{(1)}
=
(x_a\cdot x_b)
\langle A^a,A^b\rangle_n,
\tag{4}
\]

\[
K_{ab}^{(2)}
=
\langle D^a,D^b\rangle_n
\langle H^a,H^b\rangle_n,
\tag{5}
\]

\[
K_{ab}^{(3)}
=
\langle G^a,G^b\rangle_n.
\tag{6}
\]

Each is a Gram matrix of the corresponding parameter gradients.

Along continuous finite-width gradient flow,

\[
\dot f_a
=
-\frac2m\sum_bK_{ab}e_b,
\]

and therefore

\[
\dot L_n
=
-\frac4{m^2}e^\top K e
\le0.
\tag{7}
\]

Thus strict loss decrease is equivalent to

\[
e(t)\notin\ker K(t)
\]

on a set of times of positive measure. Merely having \(\operatorname{tr}K^{(\ell)}>0\) does not imply that block \(\ell\) participates in training.

## 3. Why the universal active theorem is false

Take two identical nonzero inputs with conflicting labels:

\[
x_1=x_2=x\ne0,
\qquad
y_1=1,\quad y_2=-1.
\]

The network necessarily produces the same prediction on both samples:

\[
f_1=f_2=f.
\]

Consequently,

\[
L
=
\frac12\bigl((f-1)^2+(f+1)^2\bigr)
=
f^2+1.
\]

Under the proposed scaling, the limiting readout initialization is \(C_0=0\), hence \(f(0)=0\). Therefore

\[
\nabla L(0)=2f(0)\nabla f(0)=0.
\]

The limiting dynamics are stationary:

\[
C(t)=0,\qquad
M(t)=M(0),\qquad
W^{(1)}(t)=W^{(1)}(0).
\]

This example has nonzero inputs and nondegenerate nonlinear hidden laws, but:

- neither hidden layer moves;
- \(K^{(1)}\) and \(K^{(2)}\) do not contribute to training;
- the loss never decreases;
- there is no feature learning.

An even simpler counterexample is \(x=0\): in a bias-free network, the first hidden preactivation is identically zero for every parameter value, so its variance and velocity can never be positive.

Thus the previous seven properties cannot hold uniformly over arbitrary input-label configurations.

## 4. What does extend unconditionally: every fixed Euler mesh

At initialization,

\[
(U_0^1,\dots,U_0^m)
\]

is a jointly Gaussian coordinate mark with covariance

\[
\mathbb E[U_0^aU_0^b]
=
x_a\cdot x_b.
\]

For a fixed Euler mesh \(\delta\) and a fixed number of steps \(K\), write

\[
M_{k,n}=M_{0,n}+Q_{k,n}.
\]

From (2),

\[
Q_{k,n}
=
-\frac{2\delta}{m}
\sum_{r<k}\sum_a
e_{r,a}D_{r,n}^a\otimes_nH_{r,n}^a.
\tag{8}
\]

Hence

\[
\begin{aligned}
M_{k,n}H_{k,n}^b
={}&M_{0,n}H_{k,n}^b\\
&-\frac{2\delta}{m}
\sum_{r<k,a}
e_{r,a}D_{r,n}^a
\langle H_{r,n}^a,H_{k,n}^b\rangle_n,
\end{aligned}
\tag{9}
\]

and

\[
\begin{aligned}
M_{k,n}^\top D_{k,n}^b
={}&M_{0,n}^\top D_{k,n}^b\\
&-\frac{2\delta}{m}
\sum_{r<k,a}
e_{r,a}H_{r,n}^a
\langle D_{r,n}^a,D_{k,n}^b\rangle_n.
\end{aligned}
\tag{10}
\]

For fixed \((m,\delta,K)\), the complete training recursion therefore uses only:

- finitely many jointly Gaussian initial vector marks;
- \(M_{0,n}\) and \(M_{0,n}^\top\);
- coordinatewise pseudo-Lipschitz maps;
- finitely many empirical moments.

It is a fixed finite Tensor Program. The fixed-program master theorem applies, including when the input Gram matrix is singular; see Setup G.2, Assumption L.4, and Theorem G.4 in the [Tensor Programs IV supplement](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf).

Therefore, for every fixed mesh:

- all joint sample-feature laws converge;
- every forward and backward field converges jointly;
- all cross-sample Gram quantities converge;
- every output, residual, kernel-matrix entry, and the loss converge;
- the reused Gaussian action and its adjoint are identified exactly.

This part holds for every fixed finite dataset.

## 5. The generic continuous-time obstruction

Let

\[
\mathsf G_{ab}=x_a\cdot x_b
\]

be the input Gram matrix. The formal limiting first-layer equation is

\[
\dot U^b
=
-\frac2m
\sum_a
\mathsf G_{ba}e_aq(U^a)P^a.
\tag{11}
\]

For one sample, the coordinate

\[
X=\Theta(U),\qquad
\Theta'(u)=q(u)^{-1}=1+u^2,
\]

removes the only factor \(q(U)\).

For several samples, applying \(\Theta\) componentwise gives

\[
\dot X^b
=
-\frac2m
\sum_a
\mathsf G_{ba}e_a
\frac{q(U^a)}{q(U^b)}P^a.
\tag{12}
\]

Only diagonal terms simplify. A generic off-diagonal Gram entry leaves the random ratio

\[
\frac{q(U^a)}{q(U^b)}
=
\frac{1+(U^b)^2}{1+(U^a)^2},
\]

which is unbounded.

Working in the original \(W^{(1)}\) coordinate does not repair this. The problematic map is

\[
(U^a,P^a)\longmapsto q(U^a)P^a.
\tag{13}
\]

Here \(P^a=M^*D^a\in L^2\), but it generally has an unbounded Gaussian component. For two nearby fields,

\[
\begin{aligned}
q(U^a)P^a-q(\widetilde U^a)\widetilde P^a
={}&q(U^a)(P^a-\widetilde P^a)\\
&+\bigl(q(U^a)-q(\widetilde U^a)\bigr)\widetilde P^a.
\end{aligned}
\tag{14}
\]

The second term has no bound of the form

\[
C\|U^a-\widetilde U^a\|_2
\]

unless \(\widetilde P^a\in L^\infty\).

Indeed, at a state where \(P^a\) is unbounded and \(q'(U^a)\ne0\) on a positive-measure set, choose

\[
E_R\subseteq
\{|P^a|\ge R,\ |q'(U^a)|\ge c\}
\]

and a sufficiently small perturbation

\[
h_R=\varepsilon_R\mathbf1_{E_R}.
\]

Taylor’s formula gives

\[
\frac{
\|(q(U^a+h_R)-q(U^a))P^a\|_2
}{
\|h_R\|_2
}
\ge\frac c2R.
\]

Thus the local Lipschitz constants diverge.

Consequently, for a generic dataset:

- the earlier Picard uniqueness theorem does not apply;
- the width-uniform \(O(\delta)\) Euler estimate is not available;
- fixed-mesh convergence cannot presently be lifted to continuous time;
- \(\eta_{0,n}=n^{-2}\) does not by itself close the joint diagonal limit.

Uniform integrability identifies (13) along a sequence already known to converge, but it cannot provide uniqueness or the missing stability estimate.

This does not prove that the generic multi-sample limit fails to exist. It proves that the earlier theorem does not extend to it. A stronger weighted, Orlicz, Gaussian-Sobolev, or graph-norm theory would be needed.

## 6. Exact class for which the old proof does extend

The componentwise natural-coordinate cancellation closes precisely when

\[
\mathsf G\operatorname{diag}(q(U))
=
\operatorname{diag}(q(U))\mathsf G
\quad\text{almost surely}.
\tag{15}
\]

Entrywise,

\[
\mathsf G_{ab}\bigl(q(U^a)-q(U^b)\bigr)=0.
\tag{16}
\]

For \(q(u)=(1+u^2)^{-1}\), if \(\mathsf G_{ab}\ne0\), equation (16) requires

\[
(U^a)^2=(U^b)^2
\quad\text{almost surely}.
\]

Since \(U^a=W\cdot x_a\) and the Gaussian \(W\) has full support, this is equivalent to

\[
x_b=\pm x_a.
\]

Therefore the dataset must admit a partition into blocks \(B_r\) such that

\[
x_a=\varepsilon_a v_r,
\qquad
\varepsilon_a\in\{-1,1\},
\qquad a\in B_r,
\tag{17}
\]

and

\[
v_r\cdot v_s=0
\qquad(r\ne s).
\tag{18}
\]

Pairwise orthogonal inputs are the singleton-block case. Unequal collinear magnitudes are not enough.

For (17)–(18), put

\[
V^r=W\cdot v_r,\qquad X^r=\Theta(V^r).
\]

Oddness of \(\arctan\) and evenness of \(q\) give, for \(a\in B_r\),

\[
U^a=\varepsilon_aV^r,\qquad
H^a=\varepsilon_aH^r,
\]

\[
S^a=\varepsilon_aS^r,\qquad
G^a=\varepsilon_aG^r,
\]

\[
D^a=D^r,\qquad P^a=P^r.
\]

Hence

\[
\dot X^r
=
-\frac{2\|v_r\|^2}{m}
P^r
\sum_{a\in B_r}\varepsilon_a e_a.
\tag{19}
\]

The unbounded backward field now appears linearly, with no state-dependent multiplier. The remaining \(M,C\) equations contain only finite sums of the same terms controlled in the one-sample proof.

Thus, under (17)–(18):

- the operator flow is locally Lipschitz in the transformed \(L^2/\mathfrak S_1\) state;
- global a priori bounds hold;
- the width-uniform Euler error is \(O_T(\delta)\);
- fixed-mesh convergence lifts to continuous time;
- the exact-GD defect is again
  \[
  O_T(\eta+\eta\sqrt n+\eta^2n);
  \]
- \(\eta=n^{-2}\) gives the full joint diagonal limit;
- the vector-valued hidden path laws converge in
  \[
  W_2\!\left(C([0,T];\mathbb R^m)\right);
  \]
- the output vector, full kernel matrix, and loss converge uniformly.

So this is a genuine multi-sample extension, but not an arbitrary-configuration one.

## 7. Label nondegeneracy remains necessary

Within a block define the effective label

\[
\bar y_r
=
\frac1{|B_r|}
\sum_{a\in B_r}\varepsilon_a y_a.
\]

Since \(f_a=\varepsilon_af_r\),

\[
\frac1m\sum_{a\in B_r}(f_a-y_a)^2
=
\frac{|B_r|}{m}(f_r-\bar y_r)^2
+
\text{constant}.
\tag{20}
\]

Training depends only on \(\bar y_r\). If every \(\bar y_r=0\), the zero-output limit is stationary.

More generally, strict loss decrease requires

\[
e^\top K e>0,
\]

activity of parameter block \(\ell\) requires

\[
\int_0^T e(t)^\top K^{(\ell)}(t)e(t)\,dt>0,
\]

and per-sample feature learning requires a nonzero limiting displacement for that sample. These cannot be deduced from inputs alone.

## Conclusion

The fully audited statement is:

- For arbitrary fixed finite datasets, the exact scaling, finite-width equations, kernel matrix, energy identity, and every fixed-mesh width limit extend rigorously.
- The complete active-feature-learning theorem is false for arbitrary input-label configurations.
- For generic correlated inputs, the continuous-time width-first limit remains open because the single-sample natural-coordinate stability argument fails.
- The full joint mean-field/gradient-flow theorem does extend under the explicit orthogonal sign-block condition (17)–(18), subject to separate label/residual nondegeneracy for the activity conclusions.

<skill>
<name>solve-math-rigorously</name>
<path>/etc/codex/skills/solve-math-rigorously/SKILL.md</path>
---
name: solve-math-rigorously
description: Produce compact but complete solutions to standalone mathematical questions and proof requests. Use when the user asks to solve, prove, derive, analyze, verify, or correct a mathematical problem that is not primarily an incremental lesson about supplied technical documents. Infer the strongest reasonable interpretation without inventing assumptions, establish a minimal setup, use minimal notation, expose every nontrivial step, state and verify external theorem hypotheses, give proof architecture when useful, and silently audit the draft before answering.
---

# Solve Mathematics Rigorously

Produce a complete mathematical answer rather than a staged document lesson, unless the user explicitly requests incremental pacing. Optimize simultaneously for correctness, rigor, clarity, and economy.

## Determine the intended problem

Interpret the strongest reasonable version supported by the wording and mathematical context. Do not exploit an omission to answer a trivial or uninteresting straw-man version.

Handle ambiguity according to its effect:

- If it does not change the substance, adopt the conventional interpretation and state it briefly.
- If one reasonable assumption makes the intended problem precise, state that assumption before solving.
- If several reasonable interpretations lead to materially different answers, ask for clarification or give a compact case split when that fully resolves the ambiguity.
- If a missing assumption is necessary for the claimed conclusion, do not invent it silently.

If the stated claim is false, say so and give a decisive counterexample or contradiction. Present a corrected nearby statement only when it helps, and label it as a correction rather than the original result.

Answer the problem actually asked. Do not weaken a universal claim to a convenient special case, replace an exact question with a numerical approximation, or prove a converse instead of the stated direction.

## Establish a minimal setup

Begin with a compact setup or preliminaries passage when it reduces ambiguity or supports later reasoning. State only the necessary:

- domain and assumptions;
- definitions used in the solution;
- boundary, regularity, integrability, independence, or nondegeneracy conditions that matter;
- notation that must persist through the answer.

Reuse the problem's notation whenever possible. Introduce a new symbol only when the unabbreviated expression is genuinely cumbersome and recurs often. Define every new symbol before use and state its mathematical type when that is not immediately evident.

Do not force a visible setup heading for a short elementary problem. Do not hide substantive assumptions in prose later in the solution.

## Give the architecture before a substantial proof

Before a long or conceptually difficult proof, explain the plan in a short paragraph or compact list. Identify the main steps, cases, claims, or lemmas and how they combine to yield the conclusion.

Keep the architecture at the level needed to orient the reader. Do not duplicate the detailed proof, and do not substitute a plan for the proof itself. For a routine calculation or one-step argument, omit the architecture.

## Execute every nontrivial step

Derive each nontrivial algebraic, analytic, probabilistic, geometric, combinatorial, or logical transition. Give enough detail for the user to verify the step without reconstructing missing reasoning.

In particular:

- identify the definition, identity, inequality, or prior claim being used;
- show substitutions, rearrangements, case transitions, and changes of variables that carry real mathematical content;
- track domains, signs, quantifiers, equality conditions, exceptional cases, and possible zero denominators;
- justify limit exchanges, differentiation or integration under a sign, convergence claims, and optimization steps;
- distinguish exact arguments from heuristics, approximations, and numerical evidence;
- show that intermediate claims actually imply the requested conclusion.

Avoid words such as “clearly,” “obviously,” “standard,” and “it follows” when they replace reasoning. They are acceptable only after the supporting reason has been supplied.

Do not over-explain elementary arithmetic or definitions already established. Compress routine steps while preserving the chain of verification.

## Invoke external results completely

Whenever the solution relies on a nontrivial theorem or external fact:

1. state the exact form needed, including its assumptions and conclusion;
2. verify each assumption in the present problem;
3. identify the current objects substituted into the theorem;
4. state exactly what conclusion the theorem yields here.

Do not merely name a theorem or call a fact standard. Prefer the relevant specialization over an unnecessarily general statement.

Verify a recent, specialized, source-specific, or uncertain result against a primary or authoritative source before relying on it. Cite the source near the claim. Paraphrase the relevant result unless its exact wording matters; quote only a short compliant excerpt when useful.

If the required theorem is unavailable, uncertain, or does not apply, expose the gap instead of proceeding as though it were valid.

## Use examples and checks proportionately

Include an example, boundary case, counterexample, or remark only when it clarifies an abstraction, tests a hypothesis, explains a consequence, or exposes why an assumption is needed.

Use low-cost sanity checks when they add confidence, such as:

- substituting the result back into the defining relation;
- checking dimensions, signs, or units;
- testing a boundary or small case;
- comparing with a known special case;
- deriving the same conclusion by a second short route.

Do not add examples mechanically or let them replace the general argument.

## Keep the presentation compact

Use headings only when they make a substantial solution easier to navigate. Keep notation sparse and stable. Prefer explicit expressions to disposable shorthand.

Use equations to carry mathematical content, not decoration. Avoid formula dumps, decorative summaries, and boxed equations. State the conclusion plainly.

Let the problem's complexity determine the answer length. Preserve every necessary assumption, theorem check, and logical transition, but remove repetition and exposition that does not improve understanding or verification.

## Audit and revise silently

Draft the solution, then review it before sending. Check:

1. Did the solution address the strongest reasonable interpretation rather than a straw man?
2. Were all adopted assumptions stated, and were none invented merely to force the result?
3. Was the setup sufficient but minimal?
4. Was every persistent symbol necessary and defined?
5. Did a substantial proof receive a useful architecture?
6. Can the reader verify every nontrivial transition?
7. Was every external theorem stated in the needed form, with its hypotheses checked and its conclusion applied explicitly?
8. Were recent, specialized, or uncertain results verified from an authoritative source?
9. Were edge cases, equality conditions, domains, and quantifiers handled?
10. Does the final conclusion answer the exact question?
11. Do examples or remarks earn their space?
12. Is the answer as compact as rigor allows, with no boxed equations?

Revise any failing part and repeat the audit until the answer satisfies the checklist as well as possible. Do not mention the drafting or audit process in the final answer.

</skill>

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 22924 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.305Z | assistant | final_answer ===
No universal theorem can hold for every arbitrary input/\(\{\pm1\}\)-label configuration. The conjecture is false as stated.

This does not mean multi-sample limits never exist: they do for substantial classes, including pairwise orthogonal inputs. What fails is the universal quantifier over arbitrary datasets.

## 1. Universal no-go theorem

Consider any bias-free network of the stated form, with any width-independent \(C^2\) non-affine activation, arbitrary Gaussian variances, and arbitrary positive layerwise learning-rate scalers.

Take a dataset with \(m\ge2\),

\[
x_1=\cdots=x_m=0,
\qquad
y_a\in\{-1,1\}.
\]

For every first-layer neuron and every training time,

\[
z_i^{(1),a}(t)=W_i^{(1)}(t)\cdot x_a=0.
\]

Moreover,

\[
\nabla_{W_i^{(1)}}f_n(x_a)
=
\frac{\partial f_n}{\partial z_i^{(1),a}}x_a
=0.
\]

Therefore, identically at every width,

\[
\dot W_i^{(1)}(t)=0,
\]

\[
V_n^{(1)}(t)^2=0,
\]

\[
K_n^{(1)}(t)=0,
\]

and

\[
\frac1n\sum_i
\left|z_i^{(1),a}(t)-z_i^{(1),a}(0)\right|^2=0.
\]

The first hidden preactivation law is the point mass \(\delta_0\), so its variance is zero.

These conclusions are independent of:

- the activation;
- its nonlinear behavior at zero;
- the initialization variances;
- the learning-rate scalers;
- the width/learning-rate relationship;
- the behavior of the second hidden layer.

Thus properties requiring positive first-hidden variance, first-layer feature learning, nonzero displacement, and an active first parameter block cannot hold.

Hence:

\[
\boxed{\text{There is no construction satisfying all previous properties for every arbitrary dataset.}}
\]

This already settles the universal conjecture.

### A nonzero-input obstruction

The failure is not limited to zero inputs. Take

\[
x_1=x_2=x\ne0,\qquad y_1=1,\quad y_2=-1.
\]

Because the inputs are identical,

\[
f_n(x_1)=f_n(x_2)=f_n(x).
\]

The averaged loss is

\[
L_n
=
\frac12\left[(f_n(x)-1)^2+(f_n(x)+1)^2\right]
=
f_n(x)^2+1.
\]

Under any deterministic mean-field initialization limit arising from independent centered output weights, the initial output must be zero: each finite-width initial output is symmetric about zero, so any deterministic probability limit is zero. Therefore

\[
f(0)=0,\qquad \nabla L(0)=2f(0)\nabla f(0)=0.
\]

The limiting gradient flow is stationary. It has no feature motion, no evolving kernel, and no loss decrease. This obstruction applies to every activation, not only odd activations.

---

## 2. Exact finite-dataset equations

Although the universal active theorem is false, the finite-width algebra extends exactly.

Let

\[
\mathcal D=\{(x_a,y_a)\}_{a=1}^m,
\qquad
x_a\in\mathbb R^d,
\qquad y_a\in\{-1,1\},
\]

with fixed \(m,d\), and use

\[
L_n=\frac1m\sum_{a=1}^m(f_n^a-y_a)^2.
\]

For the two-hidden-layer arctangent network, define

\[
U_i^a=w_i\cdot x_a,\qquad
H_i^a=\arctan U_i^a,
\]

\[
S_j^a=\sum_iM_{ji}H_i^a,\qquad
G_j^a=\arctan S_j^a,
\]

\[
C=nW^{(3)},\qquad
f_n^a=\langle C,G^a\rangle_n,\qquad
e_a=f_n^a-y_a.
\]

Let

\[
q(z)=\frac1{1+z^2},
\qquad
D^a=Cq(S^a),
\qquad
P^a=M^\top D^a,
\qquad
A^a=q(U^a)P^a.
\]

Use the same scaling as in the single-sample theorem:

\[
\operatorname{Var}(w_{ir})=1,
\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(M_{ji})=\frac1n,
\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(W_j^{(3)})=\frac1{n^4},
\]

\[
\lambda_1=n,\qquad
\lambda_2=1,\qquad
\lambda_3=\frac1n,
\qquad
\eta_n=n^{-2}.
\]

Direct differentiation gives the exact GD recurrence

\[
W^{(1)+}
=
W^{(1)}
-\frac{2\eta_n}{m}
\sum_{a=1}^m e_a A^a x_a^\top,
\tag{1}
\]

\[
M^+
=
M-\frac{2\eta_n}{m}
\sum_{a=1}^m e_aD^a\otimes_n H^a,
\tag{2}
\]

\[
C^+
=
C-\frac{2\eta_n}{m}
\sum_{a=1}^m e_aG^a.
\tag{3}
\]

The samplewise kernel is now an \(m\times m\) matrix. Its blocks are

\[
K_{ab}^{(1)}
=
(x_a\cdot x_b)
\langle A^a,A^b\rangle_n,
\tag{4}
\]

\[
K_{ab}^{(2)}
=
\langle D^a,D^b\rangle_n
\langle H^a,H^b\rangle_n,
\tag{5}
\]

\[
K_{ab}^{(3)}
=
\langle G^a,G^b\rangle_n.
\tag{6}
\]

Every block is positive semidefinite because it is a Gram matrix of parameter gradients. Along finite-width gradient flow,

\[
\dot f_a=-\frac2m\sum_bK_{ab}e_b,
\]

and

\[
\dot L_n
=
-\frac4{m^2}e^\top Ke
\le0.
\tag{7}
\]

For multiple samples, block activity must therefore be measured by

\[
\int_0^T e(t)^\top K^{(\ell)}(t)e(t)\,dt>0,
\]

not merely by \(\operatorname{tr}K^{(\ell)}>0\).

---

## 3. What remains rigorously valid for every fixed dataset

For every fixed dataset and every fixed Euler mesh, the full width limit exists.

At initialization,

\[
(U_0^1,\ldots,U_0^m)
\]

is jointly Gaussian with covariance

\[
\operatorname{Cov}(U_0^a,U_0^b)=x_a\cdot x_b.
\]

For a fixed number of steps, the learned middle matrix has the exact representation

\[
Q_{k,n}
=
-\frac{2\delta}{m}
\sum_{r<k}\sum_{a=1}^m
e_{r,a}D_{r,n}^a\otimes_nH_{r,n}^a.
\]

Thus every multiplication by \(M_{k,n}=M_{0,n}+Q_{k,n}\), or its transpose, reduces to:

- multiplication by \(M_{0,n}\) or \(M_{0,n}^\top\);
- finitely many coordinate maps;
- finitely many cross-sample empirical inner products.

For fixed \(m,\delta,k\), this is one finite Tensor Program. The fixed-program master theorem therefore gives joint convergence of all sample fields, outputs, residuals, cross-sample kernels, and moments. Its hypotheses allow a singular initial input Gram matrix and arbitrary reuse of the shared Gaussian matrix and its transpose; see [Yang–Hu, Setup G.2 and Theorem G.4](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v139/yang21c/yang21c-supp.pdf).

So the following statement is unconditional:

\[
\boxed{\text{Every fixed-mesh, finite-time multi-sample OMFP DAG is rigorously identified.}}
\]

The corresponding hidden empirical states are vector-valued laws on

\[
C([0,T];\mathbb R^m).
\]

---

## 4. Why the continuous-time proof does not extend to generic inputs

The formal continuum first-layer equation is

\[
\dot W
=
-\frac2m\sum_a
e_a q(U^a)P^a x_a,
\qquad U^a=W\cdot x_a.
\tag{8}
\]

Let

\[
\mathsf G_{ab}=x_a\cdot x_b
\]

be the input Gram matrix. Then

\[
\dot U^a
=
-\frac2m
\sum_b
\mathsf G_{ab}e_bq(U^b)P^b.
\tag{9}
\]

For one sample, the coordinate

\[
\Theta(u)=u+\frac{u^3}{3},
\qquad \Theta'(u)=q(u)^{-1},
\]

removes the single factor \(q(U)\).

Applying it componentwise in (9) gives

\[
\frac d{dt}\Theta(U^a)
=
-\frac2m
\sum_b
\mathsf G_{ab}e_b
\frac{q(U^b)}{q(U^a)}P^b.
\tag{10}
\]

For a generic correlated dataset, the ratios in (10) are nonconstant and unbounded.

Tracking \(U\) directly does not help. The problematic map is

\[
(U^a,P^a)\longmapsto q(U^a)P^a.
\]

Although \(P^a\in L^2\), it has a Gaussian, hence generally unbounded, component. For two states,

\[
q(U^a)P^a-q(\widetilde U^a)\widetilde P^a
=
q(U^a)(P^a-\widetilde P^a)
+
\bigl(q(U^a)-q(\widetilde U^a)\bigr)\widetilde P^a.
\]

The last term cannot be controlled by the \(L^2\)-distance of \(U^a,\widetilde U^a\). Infinitesimally it contains multiplication by

\[
q'(U^a)\widetilde P^a,
\]

which is not a bounded \(L^2\to L^2\) operator when that multiplier is unbounded.

Hence, for generic correlated inputs:

- the limiting vector field is not locally Lipschitz in the previous \(L^2/\mathfrak S_1\) state norm;
- the prior Picard uniqueness proof fails;
- the width-uniform \(O(\delta)\) Euler estimate fails;
- fixed-mesh convergence cannot currently be lifted to a restartable continuous-time theorem by the previous argument.

Uniform integrability identifies the product after convergence has already been obtained; it does not prove uniqueness or Euler stability.

Thus an affirmative theorem for every distinct nonzero generic dataset remains open and would require a stronger weighted, Orlicz, or Gaussian-Sobolev action norm.

---

## 5. A genuine positive multi-sample class

The failure is not “multiple samples are impossible.” A substantial class is rigorously covered.

Suppose the inputs admit a partition into blocks \(B_r\) such that

\[
x_a=\varepsilon_a v_r,\qquad
\varepsilon_a\in\{-1,1\},
\qquad a\in B_r,
\]

and

\[
v_r\cdot v_s=0
\qquad(r\ne s).
\tag{11}
\]

This includes arbitrary finite collections of pairwise orthogonal nonzero inputs.

For \(a\in B_r\),

\[
U^a=\varepsilon_aV^r,
\qquad
V^r=W\cdot v_r.
\]

Since \(q\) is even,

\[
q(U^a)=q(V^r).
\]

Orthogonality gives

\[
\dot V^r
=
-\frac{2\|v_r\|^2}{m}
q(V^r)
\sum_{a\in B_r}\varepsilon_a e_aP^a.
\]

Therefore

\[
X^r=\Theta(V^r)
\]

satisfies

\[
\dot X^r
=
-\frac{2\|v_r\|^2}{m}
\sum_{a\in B_r}\varepsilon_a e_aP^a.
\tag{12}
\]

All unbounded backward fields now appear linearly; the troublesome state-dependent multiplier has disappeared. The complete single-sample \(L^2/\mathfrak S_1\) proof extends with only finite \(m\)-dependent constants:

- the operator ODE is well-posed and restartable;
- finite-width and limiting Euler errors are \(O_T(\delta)\);
- exact GD with \(\eta_n=n^{-2}\) converges jointly to the operator gradient flow;
- vector-valued hidden path laws converge in
  \[
  W_2(C([0,T];\mathbb R^m));
  \]
- the output vector, full kernel matrix, and loss converge uniformly.

For pairwise orthogonal nonzero inputs, every assignment \(y_a\in\{\pm1\}\) produces nonzero initial residual forcing. At zero readout initialization, the initial output-kernel matrix is diagonal with strictly positive entries, so

\[
y^\top K^{(3)}(0)y>0.
\]

The lower two blocks acquire strictly positive \(t^2\) coefficients by the same adjoint-positivity argument as in the single-sample proof. Hence there is a short interval on which:

- the loss decreases;
- both hidden layers move;
- all three blocks are active;
- the kernel changes;
- the nonlinear regression residual remains positive.

Thus there are genuine multi-sample, arbitrary-label examples—but not for arbitrary input geometry.

## Final resolution

The quantifiers matter:

- “Does at least one genuine multi-sample nonlinear example exist?” — **Yes.** Pairwise orthogonal nonzero inputs with arbitrary \(\{\pm1\}\) labels give one.
- “Does such a theorem hold for every arbitrary input/label configuration?” — **No.** The all-zero-input dataset is an unconditional counterexample.
- “Does the current proof extend to every distinct, nonzero, generic dataset?” — **No known proof.** Fixed-mesh OMFP is rigorous, but continuous-time uniqueness and uniform mesh removal remain open.

Independent audits of the finite-width algebra, Tensor Program invocation, analytic stability, label cancellations, and quantifiers all reached this same three-part conclusion.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 22937 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.306Z | user | None ===
<skill>
<name>solve-math-rigorously</name>
<path>/etc/codex/skills/solve-math-rigorously/SKILL.md</path>
---
name: solve-math-rigorously
description: Produce compact but complete solutions to standalone mathematical questions and proof requests. Use when the user asks to solve, prove, derive, analyze, verify, or correct a mathematical problem that is not primarily an incremental lesson about supplied technical documents. Infer the strongest reasonable interpretation without inventing assumptions, establish a minimal setup, use minimal notation, expose every nontrivial step, state and verify external theorem hypotheses, give proof architecture when useful, and silently audit the draft before answering.
---

# Solve Mathematics Rigorously

Produce a complete mathematical answer rather than a staged document lesson, unless the user explicitly requests incremental pacing. Optimize simultaneously for correctness, rigor, clarity, and economy.

## Determine the intended problem

Interpret the strongest reasonable version supported by the wording and mathematical context. Do not exploit an omission to answer a trivial or uninteresting straw-man version.

Handle ambiguity according to its effect:

- If it does not change the substance, adopt the conventional interpretation and state it briefly.
- If one reasonable assumption makes the intended problem precise, state that assumption before solving.
- If several reasonable interpretations lead to materially different answers, ask for clarification or give a compact case split when that fully resolves the ambiguity.
- If a missing assumption is necessary for the claimed conclusion, do not invent it silently.

If the stated claim is false, say so and give a decisive counterexample or contradiction. Present a corrected nearby statement only when it helps, and label it as a correction rather than the original result.

Answer the problem actually asked. Do not weaken a universal claim to a convenient special case, replace an exact question with a numerical approximation, or prove a converse instead of the stated direction.

## Establish a minimal setup

Begin with a compact setup or preliminaries passage when it reduces ambiguity or supports later reasoning. State only the necessary:

- domain and assumptions;
- definitions used in the solution;
- boundary, regularity, integrability, independence, or nondegeneracy conditions that matter;
- notation that must persist through the answer.

Reuse the problem's notation whenever possible. Introduce a new symbol only when the unabbreviated expression is genuinely cumbersome and recurs often. Define every new symbol before use and state its mathematical type when that is not immediately evident.

Do not force a visible setup heading for a short elementary problem. Do not hide substantive assumptions in prose later in the solution.

## Give the architecture before a substantial proof

Before a long or conceptually difficult proof, explain the plan in a short paragraph or compact list. Identify the main steps, cases, claims, or lemmas and how they combine to yield the conclusion.

Keep the architecture at the level needed to orient the reader. Do not duplicate the detailed proof, and do not substitute a plan for the proof itself. For a routine calculation or one-step argument, omit the architecture.

## Execute every nontrivial step

Derive each nontrivial algebraic, analytic, probabilistic, geometric, combinatorial, or logical transition. Give enough detail for the user to verify the step without reconstructing missing reasoning.

In particular:

- identify the definition, identity, inequality, or prior claim being used;
- show substitutions, rearrangements, case transitions, and changes of variables that carry real mathematical content;
- track domains, signs, quantifiers, equality conditions, exceptional cases, and possible zero denominators;
- justify limit exchanges, differentiation or integration under a sign, convergence claims, and optimization steps;
- distinguish exact arguments from heuristics, approximations, and numerical evidence;
- show that intermediate claims actually imply the requested conclusion.

Avoid words such as “clearly,” “obviously,” “standard,” and “it follows” when they replace reasoning. They are acceptable only after the supporting reason has been supplied.

Do not over-explain elementary arithmetic or definitions already established. Compress routine steps while preserving the chain of verification.

## Invoke external results completely

Whenever the solution relies on a nontrivial theorem or external fact:

1. state the exact form needed, including its assumptions and conclusion;
2. verify each assumption in the present problem;
3. identify the current objects substituted into the theorem;
4. state exactly what conclusion the theorem yields here.

Do not merely name a theorem or call a fact standard. Prefer the relevant specialization over an unnecessarily general statement.

Verify a recent, specialized, source-specific, or uncertain result against a primary or authoritative source before relying on it. Cite the source near the claim. Paraphrase the relevant result unless its exact wording matters; quote only a short compliant excerpt when useful.

If the required theorem is unavailable, uncertain, or does not apply, expose the gap instead of proceeding as though it were valid.

## Use examples and checks proportionately

Include an example, boundary case, counterexample, or remark only when it clarifies an abstraction, tests a hypothesis, explains a consequence, or exposes why an assumption is needed.

Use low-cost sanity checks when they add confidence, such as:

- substituting the result back into the defining relation;
- checking dimensions, signs, or units;
- testing a boundary or small case;
- comparing with a known special case;
- deriving the same conclusion by a second short route.

Do not add examples mechanically or let them replace the general argument.

## Keep the presentation compact

Use headings only when they make a substantial solution easier to navigate. Keep notation sparse and stable. Prefer explicit expressions to disposable shorthand.

Use equations to carry mathematical content, not decoration. Avoid formula dumps, decorative summaries, and boxed equations. State the conclusion plainly.

Let the problem's complexity determine the answer length. Preserve every necessary assumption, theorem check, and logical transition, but remove repetition and exposition that does not improve understanding or verification.

## Audit and revise silently

Draft the solution, then review it before sending. Check:

1. Did the solution address the strongest reasonable interpretation rather than a straw man?
2. Were all adopted assumptions stated, and were none invented merely to force the result?
3. Was the setup sufficient but minimal?
4. Was every persistent symbol necessary and defined?
5. Did a substantial proof receive a useful architecture?
6. Can the reader verify every nontrivial transition?
7. Was every external theorem stated in the needed form, with its hypotheses checked and its conclusion applied explicitly?
8. Were recent, specialized, or uncertain results verified from an authoritative source?
9. Were edge cases, equality conditions, domains, and quantifiers handled?
10. Does the final conclusion answer the exact question?
11. Do examples or remarks earn their space?
12. Is the answer as compact as rigor allows, with no boxed equations?

Revise any failing part and repeat the audit until the answer satisfies the checklist as well as possible. Do not mention the drafting or audit process in the final answer.

</skill>

A stronger obstruction closes the random-limit loophole too. At CLT readout scale, the initialization first-layer output-gradient norm is asymptotically a strictly positive constant for every admissible non-affine activation. Finite \(K^{(1)}\) then forces the first-layer mobility to be \(O(1)\), while an exact discrete-GD identity forces its empirical feature speed to vanish like \(1/n\). === OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 23377 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.318Z | assistant | final_answer ===
No. Under the stated quantifier “arbitrary input and label configuration,” the \(L=2\) conjecture is false.

In fact, one admissible two-sample dataset rules it out for every fixed non-affine \(C^2\) activation and every allowed choice of variances, layerwise learning rates, and base step sizes. The argument also closes the possible nondegenerate random-output loophole.

## Theorem

Let \(x\in\mathbb R^d\) satisfy

\[
\frac{\|x\|_2^2}{d}=1,
\]

and consider the two samples

\[
(x_1,y_1)=(x,+1),
\qquad
(x_2,y_2)=(x,-1).
\]

Use the averaged loss. Then no admissible activation and hyperparameter sequence can satisfy all the previously required properties, including:

- finite nondegenerate hidden states;
- finite uniformly convergent kernel;
- feature learning in both hidden layers;
- a joint exact-GD/gradient-flow limit;
- strict leading-order loss decrease.

This remains true even if the limiting initial output is allowed to be random.

## Proof

Because the two inputs are identical, their network outputs are identical. Write this common output as \(f_n\). Then, exactly at every width,

\[
L_n
=
\frac12\left[(f_n-1)^2+(f_n+1)^2\right]
=
1+f_n^2.
\tag{1}
\]

Consequently,

\[
\nabla_{W^{(\ell)}}L_n
=
2f_n\nabla_{W^{(\ell)}}f_n.
\tag{2}
\]

Let

\[
K_n^{(\ell)}
=
\lambda_n^{(\ell)}
\left\|\nabla_{W^{(\ell)}}f_n\right\|^2,
\qquad
K_n=\sum_{\ell=1}^3K_n^{(\ell)}.
\]

For continuous layer-scaled gradient flow,

\[
\dot f_n=-2f_nK_n.
\tag{3}
\]

We now split according to the only possible initialization scales.

### 1. Vanishing initial output

At initialization, conditionally on the first two layers,

\[
f_n(0)
=
\sum_{j=1}^n W_j^{(3)}\phi(S_j)
\]

is centered Gaussian, with conditional variance

\[
Q_n
=
\sigma_{3,n}^2
\sum_{j=1}^n\phi(S_j)^2.
\tag{4}
\]

If \(Q_n\to0\), then

\[
f_n(0)\longrightarrow0
\]

in probability. More generally, symmetry shows that every deterministic finite probability limit of \(f_n(0)\) must be zero.

Under the claimed joint gradient-flow limit, (3) passes to

\[
\dot f(t)=-2K(t)f(t),
\qquad f(0)=0.
\]

Since the required finite kernel satisfies \(K\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}\),

\[
f(t)
=
f(0)\exp\left(-2\int_0^tK(s)\,ds\right)
=
0.
\]

Therefore

\[
L(t)=1+f(t)^2=1
\]

for every \(t\). Hence

\[
L(T)=L(0),
\]

contradicting the required strict loss decrease.

A singular discrete boundary layer cannot evade this conclusion while retaining the required vanishing discrete-GD error and convergence to a genuine gradient flow.

### 2. Nondegenerate random initial output

It remains to rule out an \(O(1)\) random Gaussian output.

Let

\[
U_i=W_i^{(1)}\cdot x,\qquad
H_i=\phi(U_i),
\]

\[
S_j=\sum_iW_{ji}^{(2)}H_i,
\qquad
G_j=\phi(S_j).
\]

The required positive finite hidden variances imply

\[
U_i\Rightarrow U\sim N(0,v_1),
\qquad
S_j\Rightarrow S\sim N(0,v_2),
\]

with \(v_1,v_2\in(0,\infty)\). Since \(\phi\) has polynomial growth,

\[
a_0:=\mathbb E\phi(U)^2\in(0,\infty).
\]

Conditionally on the first layer,

\[
\operatorname{Var}(S_j\mid U)
=
\sigma_{2,n}^2\sum_i\phi(U_i)^2.
\]

The law of large numbers and the positive limit \(v_2\) therefore give

\[
n\sigma_{2,n}^2
\longrightarrow
c_2:=\frac{v_2}{a_0}\in(0,\infty).
\tag{5}
\]

Similarly,

\[
\frac1n\sum_jG_j^2
\longrightarrow
b_0:=\mathbb E\phi(S)^2\in(0,\infty).
\]

Thus a finite nondegenerate random limit of \(f_n(0)\) is possible only if

\[
n\sigma_{3,n}^2\longrightarrow c_3\in(0,\infty).
\tag{6}
\]

We next compute the initial first-layer output-gradient norm.

Set

\[
B_i
=
\phi'(U_i)
\sum_j
W_j^{(3)}\phi'(S_j)W_{ji}^{(2)}.
\]

Then

\[
\nabla_{W_i^{(1)}}f_n=B_ix,
\]

so

\[
\left\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f_n\right\|_F^2
=
\|x\|^2\sum_iB_i^2.
\tag{7}
\]

The dependence between \(W_{ji}^{(2)}\) and \(S_j\) must be retained. For one row \(w\) of \(W^{(2)}\), define

\[
a_i=\phi(U_i),\qquad r_i=\phi'(U_i),
\]

\[
A=\sum_i a_i^2,\qquad
B=\sum_i r_i^2,\qquad
C=\sum_i a_i^2r_i^2,
\qquad
S=w\cdot a.
\]

Conditionally on \(U\),

\[
w=\frac{S}{A}a+\xi,
\]

where \(\xi\) is independent of \(S\) and

\[
\operatorname{Cov}(\xi)
=
\sigma_{2,n}^2
\left(I-\frac{aa^\top}{A}\right).
\]

Consequently, with \(h=\phi'\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
&\mathbb E\!\left[
h(S)^2
w^\top\operatorname{diag}(r^2)w
\mid U
\right]
\\
&=
\sigma_{2,n}^2
\left(B-\frac CA\right)
\mathbb E h(S)^2
+
\frac{C}{A^2}
\mathbb E[S^2h(S)^2].
\end{aligned}
\tag{8}
\]

Now

\[
\frac Bn\longrightarrow
a_1:=\mathbb E\phi'(U)^2,
\qquad
\frac An\longrightarrow a_0,
\]

while \(C/A=O_{\mathbb P}(1)\). From (5), the second term and the \(C/A\) correction in (8) are \(O_{\mathbb P}(n^{-1})\). Therefore the leading row contribution is

\[
c_2a_1b_1,
\qquad
b_1:=\mathbb E\phi'(S)^2.
\]

Conditioning next on \(U,W^{(2)}\), \(\sum_iB_i^2\) is a Gaussian quadratic form in \(W^{(3)}\). Row averaging and Gaussian quadratic-form concentration give

\[
\sum_iB_i^2
\longrightarrow
c_2c_3a_1b_1
\qquad\text{in probability}.
\tag{9}
\]

For completeness, concentration follows because the quadratic-form trace is \(\Theta_{\mathbb P}(n)\), while its operator norm is at most polylogarithmic in \(n\): polynomial growth gives Gaussian-max bounds for the diagonal derivative matrices, and an elementary sphere-net estimate gives

\[
\|W^{(2)}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{\mathbb P}(1)
\]

under (5). Hence the conditional variance in (9) is \(O((\log n)^q/n)\) for some finite \(q\).

Because \(\phi\) is non-affine and \(U,S\) are full-support nondegenerate Gaussians,

\[
a_1>0,\qquad b_1>0.
\]

Indeed, either expectation could vanish only if the continuous function \(\phi'\) vanished everywhere.

Combining (7) and (9),

\[
\left\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f_n(0)\right\|_F^2
\longrightarrow
\gamma_1
:=
\|x\|^2c_2c_3a_1b_1
>0.
\tag{10}
\]

Uniform finite convergence of the total kernel implies

\[
K_n^{(1)}(0)
=
\lambda_n^{(1)}
\left\|\nabla_{W^{(1)}}f_n(0)\right\|_F^2
\le K_n(0)
=
O_{\mathbb P}(1).
\]

Equation (10) therefore forces

\[
\lambda_n^{(1)}=O(1).
\tag{11}
\]

Now consider the polygonal interpolation of exact GD. On every step interval, let

\[
b_i=\frac{\partial f_n}{\partial U_i}.
\]

Since \(\nabla_{W_i^{(1)}}f_n=b_ix\), equations (1)–(2) give exactly

\[
\dot U_i
=
-2f_n\lambda_n^{(1)}\|x\|^2b_i.
\]

Hence

\[
\begin{aligned}
V_n^{(1)}(t)^2
&=
\frac1n\sum_i|\dot U_i|^2\\
&=
4f_n(t)^2
\frac{\lambda_n^{(1)}\|x\|^2}{n}
K_n^{(1)}(t).
\end{aligned}
\tag{12}
\]

This is an exact discrete-interpolation identity, not a Taylor or continuous-time approximation.

Using (11) and uniform compact-time boundedness of \(f_n\) and \(K_n\),

\[
\begin{aligned}
\int_0^T V_n^{(1)}(t)^2\,dt
&\le
4\|x\|^2
\frac{\lambda_n^{(1)}}n
\sup_{t\le T}|f_n(t)|^2
\int_0^TK_n^{(1)}(t)\,dt\\
&\xrightarrow{\mathbb P}0.
\end{aligned}
\]

Thus the first hidden layer is lazy, contradicting the required strictly positive feature-energy limit.

### 3. Remaining scales

If the initial output is neither vanishing nor finite nondegenerate, then it is non-tight or nonconvergent, contradicting full joint output convergence. The preceding two cases are therefore exhaustive.

## Conclusion

For the admissible normalized dataset

\[
\{(x,+1),(x,-1)\},
\]

every possible scaling fails:

- a self-averaging output gives constant limiting loss;
- a nondegenerate random output forces the first hidden layer to be lazy under a finite kernel;
- any other output scaling violates finite joint convergence.

Therefore:

\[
\boxed{\text{No single nonlinear \(L=2\) construction can work for every normalized finite dataset and every \(\{\pm1\}\)-labeling.}}
\]

This does not say that every multi-sample dataset fails. Orthogonal inputs or repeated inputs with a nonzero average label can inherit variants of the one-sample arctan construction. But the universal “arbitrary configuration” statement is definitively false. If contradictory duplicate inputs were intended to be excluded, that is a materially different conjecture requiring an explicit consistency or separation assumption.

=== OPERATOR2 ROLLOUT LINE 23384 | 2026-08-27T19:35:20.318Z | user | None ===
This is actually a great illuminating example showing the task defined above was not well specified, and a perfectly identical input and perfectly different / opposite label demonstrates that no deterministic learning algorithm can learn any arbitrary configuration that admits this design. So let's add this higher constraint that the samples cannot be "identica with different labels", an even stronger assumption is that no pair of samples can be like that arrangement. However, it is conceivable that the inputs can be made to be arbitrarily close to that configuration but at a constant (albeit small arbitrarily) angle. With this additional constraint, what is the full and complete resolution fo the statement? can you prove constructively the existence of one such a model with a joint limit, or can you , similar to proof above, show that such a construction is impossible. use [$solve-math-rigorously](/etc/codex/skills/solve-math-rigorously/SKILL.md)  to solve this rigorously and audit your proof in the end to make sure they are fully valid and correct

10. Does the final conclusion answer the exact question?
11. Do examples or remarks earn their space?
12. Is the answer as compact as rigor allows, with no boxed equations?

Revise any failing part and repeat the audit until the answer satisfies the checklist as well as possible. Do not mention the drafting or audit process in the final answer.

</skill>
